
<title>Up from Slavery: An Autobiography</title>

<author>Booker T. Washington</author>

<line>This volume is dedicated to my Wife</line>
<line>MRS. MARGARET JAMES WASHINGTON</line>
<line>And to my Brother</line>
<line>MR. JOHN H. WASHINGTON</line>
<line>Whose patience, fidelity, and hard work have gone far</line>
<line>to make the work at Tuskegee successful.</line>

<line>UP FROM SLAVERY:</line>




<preface>Preface</preface>


<line>This volume is the outgrowth of a series of articles, dealing with</line>
<line>incidents in my life, which were published consecutively in the</line>
<line>Outlook. While they were appearing in that magazine I was constantly</line>
<line>surprised at the number of requests which came to me from all parts of</line>
<line>the country, asking that the articles be permanently preserved in book</line>
<line>form. I am most grateful to the Outlook for permission to gratify these</line>
<line>requests.</line>

<line>I have tried to tell a simple, straightforward story, with no attempt</line>
<line>at embellishment. My regret is that what I have attempted to do has</line>
<line>been done so imperfectly. The greater part of my time and strength is</line>
<line>required for the executive work connected with the Tuskegee Normal and</line>
<line>Industrial Institute, and in securing the money necessary for the</line>
<line>support of the institution. Much of what I have said has been written</line>
<line>on board trains, or at hotels or railroad stations while I have been</line>
<line>waiting for trains, or during the moments that I could spare from my</line>
<line>work while at Tuskegee. Without the painstaking and generous assistance</line>
<line>of Mr. Max Bennett Thrasher I could not have succeeded in any</line>
<line>satisfactory degree.</line>




<introduction>Introduction</introduction>


<line>The details of Mr. Washington’s early life, as frankly set down in “Up</line>
<line>from Slavery,” do not give quite a whole view of his education. He had</line>
<line>the training that a coloured youth receives at Hampton, which, indeed,</line>
<line>the autobiography does explain. But the reader does not get his</line>
<line>intellectual pedigree, for Mr. Washington himself, perhaps, does not as</line>
<line>clearly understand it as another man might. The truth is he had a</line>
<line>training during the most impressionable period of his life that was</line>
<line>very extraordinary, such a training as few men of his generation have</line>
<line>had. To see its full meaning one must start in the Hawaiian Islands</line>
<line>half a century or more ago.* There Samuel Armstrong, a youth of</line>
<line>missionary parents, earned enough money to pay his expenses at an</line>
<line>American college. Equipped with this small sum and the earnestness that</line>
<line>the undertaking implied, he came to Williams College when Dr. Mark</line>
<line>Hopkins was president. Williams College had many good things for youth</line>
<line>in that day, as it has in this, but the greatest was the strong</line>
<line>personality of its famous president. Every student does not profit by a</line>
<line>great teacher; but perhaps no young man ever came under the influence</line>
<line>of Dr. Hopkins, whose whole nature was so ripe for profit by such an</line>
<line>experience as young Armstrong. He lived in the family of President</line>
<line>Hopkins, and thus had a training that was wholly out of the common; and</line>
<line>this training had much to do with the development of his own strong</line>
<line>character, whose originality and force we are only beginning to</line>
<line>appreciate.</line>

<line>* For this interesting view of Mr. Washington’s education, I am</line>
<line>indebted to Robert C. Ogden, Esq., Chairman of the Board of Trustees</line>
<line>of Hampton Institute and the intimate friend of General Armstrong</line>
<line>during the whole period of his educational work.</line>

<line>In turn, Samuel Armstrong, the founder of Hampton Institute, took up</line>
<line>his work as a trainer of youth. He had very raw material, and doubtless</line>
<line>most of his pupils failed to get the greatest lessons from him; but, as</line>
<line>he had been a peculiarly receptive pupil of Dr. Hopkins, so Booker</line>
<line>Washington became a peculiarly receptive pupil of his. To the formation</line>
<line>of Mr. Washington’s character, then, went the missionary zeal of New</line>
<line>England, influenced by one of the strongest personalities in modern</line>
<line>education, and the wide-reaching moral earnestness of General Armstrong</line>
<line>himself. These influences are easily recognizable in Mr. Washington</line>
<line>to-day by men who knew Dr. Hopkins and General Armstrong.</line>

<line>I got the cue to Mr. Washington’s character from a very simple incident</line>
<line>many years ago. I had never seen him, and I knew little about him,</line>
<line>except that he was the head of a school at Tuskegee, Alabama. I had</line>
<line>occasion to write to him, and I addressed him as “The Rev. Booker T.</line>
<line>Washington.” In his reply there was no mention of my addressing him as</line>
<line>a clergyman. But when I had occasion to write to him again, and</line>
<line>persisted in making him a preacher, his second letter brought a</line>
<line>postscript: “I have no claim to ‘Rev.’” I knew most of the coloured men</line>
<line>who at that time had become prominent as leaders of their race, but I</line>
<line>had not then known one who was neither a politician nor a preacher; and</line>
<line>I had not heard of the head of an important coloured school who was not</line>
<line>a preacher. “A new kind of man in the coloured world,” I said to</line>
<line>myself—“a new kind of man surely if he looks upon his task as an</line>
<line>economic one instead of a theological one.” I wrote him an apology for</line>
<line>mistaking him for a preacher.</line>

<line>The first time that I went to Tuskegee I was asked to make an address</line>
<line>to the school on Sunday evening. I sat upon the platform of the large</line>
<line>chapel and looked forth on a thousand coloured faces, and the choir of</line>
<line>a hundred or more behind me sang a familiar religious melody, and the</line>
<line>whole company joined in the chorus with unction. I was the only white</line>
<line>man under the roof, and the scene and the songs made an impression on</line>
<line>me that I shall never forget. Mr. Washington arose and asked them to</line>
<line>sing one after another of the old melodies that I had heard all my</line>
<line>life; but I had never before heard them sung by a thousand voices nor</line>
<line>by the voices of educated Negroes. I had associated them with the Negro</line>
<line>of the past, not with the Negro who was struggling upward. They brought</line>
<line>to my mind the plantation, the cabin, the slave, not the freedman in</line>
<line>quest of education. But on the plantation and in the cabin they had</line>
<line>never been sung as these thousand students sang them. I saw again all</line>
<line>the old plantations that I had ever seen; the whole history of the</line>
<line>Negro ran through my mind; and the inexpressible pathos of his life</line>
<line>found expression in these songs as I had never before felt it.</line>

<line>And the future? These were the ambitious youths of the race, at work</line>
<line>with an earnestness that put to shame the conventional student life of</line>
<line>most educational institutions. Another song rolled up along the</line>
<line>rafters. And as soon as silence came, I found myself in front of this</line>
<line>extraordinary mass of faces, thinking not of them, but of that long and</line>
<line>unhappy chapter in our country’s history which followed the one great</line>
<line>structural mistake of the Fathers of the Republic; thinking of the one</line>
<line>continuous great problem that generations of statesmen had wrangled</line>
<line>over, and a million men fought about, and that had so dwarfed the mass</line>
<line>of English men in the Southern States as to hold them back a hundred</line>
<line>years behind their fellows in every other part of the world—in England,</line>
<line>in Australia, and in the Northern and Western States; I was thinking of</line>
<line>this dark shadow that had oppressed every large-minded statesman from</line>
<line>Jefferson to Lincoln. These thousand young men and women about me were</line>
<line>victims of it. I, too, was an innocent victim of it. The whole Republic</line>
<line>was a victim of that fundamental error of importing Africa into</line>
<line>America. I held firmly to the first article of my faith that the</line>
<line>Republic must stand fast by the principle of a fair ballot; but I</line>
<line>recalled the wretched mess that Reconstruction had made of it; I</line>
<line>recalled the low level of public life in all the “black” States. Every</line>
<line>effort of philanthropy seemed to have miscarried, every effort at</line>
<line>correcting abuses seemed of doubtful value, and the race friction</line>
<line>seemed to become severer. Here was the century-old problem in all its</line>
<line>pathos seated singing before me. Who were the more to be pitied—these</line>
<line>innocent victims of an ancient wrong, or I and men like me, who had</line>
<line>inherited the problem? I had long ago thrown aside illusions and</line>
<line>theories, and was willing to meet the facts face to face, and to do</line>
<line>whatever in God’s name a man might do towards saving the next</line>
<line>generation from such a burden. But I felt the weight of twenty</line>
<line>well-nigh hopeless years of thought and reading and observation; for</line>
<line>the old difficulties remained and new ones had sprung up. Then I saw</line>
<line>clearly that the way out of a century of blunders had been made by this</line>
<line>man who stood beside me and was introducing me to this audience. Before</line>
<line>me was the material he had used. All about me was the indisputable</line>
<line>evidence that he had found the natural line of development. He had</line>
<line>shown the way. Time and patience and encouragement and work would do</line>
<line>the rest.</line>

<line>It was then more clearly than ever before that I understood the</line>
<line>patriotic significance of Mr. Washington’s work. It is this conception</line>
<line>of it and of him that I have ever since carried with me. It is on this</line>
<line>that his claim to our gratitude rests.</line>

<line>To teach the Negro to read, whether English, or Greek, or Hebrew,</line>
<line>butters no parsnips. To make the Negro work, that is what his master</line>
<line>did in one way and hunger has done in another; yet both these left</line>
<line>Southern life where they found it. But to teach the Negro to do skilful</line>
<line>work, as men of all the races that have risen have worked,—responsible</line>
<line>work, which IS education and character; and most of all when Negroes so</line>
<line>teach Negroes to do this that they will teach others with a missionary</line>
<line>zeal that puts all ordinary philanthropic efforts to shame,—this is to</line>
<line>change the whole economic basis of life and the whole character of a</line>
<line>people.</line>

<line>The plan itself is not a new one. It was worked out at Hampton</line>
<line>Institute, but it was done at Hampton by white men. The plan had, in</line>
<line>fact, been many times theoretically laid down by thoughtful students of</line>
<line>Southern life. Handicrafts were taught in the days of slavery on most</line>
<line>well-managed plantations. But Tuskegee is, nevertheless, a brand-new</line>
<line>chapter in the history of the Negro, and in the history of the</line>
<line>knottiest problem we have ever faced. It not only makes “a carpenter of</line>
<line>a man; it makes a man of a carpenter.” In one sense, therefore, it is</line>
<line>of greater value than any other institution for the training of men and</line>
<line>women that we have, from Cambridge to Palo Alto. It is almost the only</line>
<line>one of which it may be said that it points the way to a new epoch in a</line>
<line>large area of our national life.</line>

<line>To work out the plan on paper, or at a distance—that is one thing. For</line>
<line>a white man to work it out—that too, is an easy thing. For a coloured</line>
<line>man to work it out in the South, where, in its constructive period, he</line>
<line>was necessarily misunderstood by his own people as well as by the</line>
<line>whites, and where he had to adjust it at every step to the strained</line>
<line>race relations—that is so very different and more difficult a thing</line>
<line>that the man who did it put the country under lasting obligations to</line>
<line>him.</line>

<line>It was not and is not a mere educational task. Anybody could teach boys</line>
<line>trades and give them an elementary education. Such tasks have been done</line>
<line>since the beginning of civilization. But this task had to be done with</line>
<line>the rawest of raw material, done within the civilization of the</line>
<line>dominant race, and so done as not to run across race lines and social</line>
<line>lines that are the strongest forces in the community. It had to be done</line>
<line>for the benefit of the whole community. It had to be done, moreover,</line>
<line>without local help, in the face of the direst poverty, done by begging,</line>
<line>and done in spite of the ignorance of one race and the prejudice of the</line>
<line>other.</line>

<line>No man living had a harder task, and a task that called for more wisdom</line>
<line>to do it right. The true measure of Mr. Washington’s success is, then,</line>
<line>not his teaching the pupils of Tuskegee, nor even gaining the support</line>
<line>of philanthropic persons at a distance, but this—that every Southern</line>
<line>white man of character and of wisdom has been won to a cordial</line>
<line>recognition of the value of the work, even men who held and still hold</line>
<line>to the conviction that a mere book education for the Southern blacks</line>
<line>under present conditions is a positive evil. This is a demonstration of</line>
<line>the efficiency of the Hampton-Tuskegee idea that stands like the</line>
<line>demonstration of the value of democratic institutions themselves—a</line>
<line>demonstration made so clear in spite of the greatest odds that it is no</line>
<line>longer open to argument.</line>

<line>Consider the change that has come in twenty years in the discussion of</line>
<line>the Negro problem. Two or three decades ago social philosophers and</line>
<line>statisticians and well-meaning philanthropists were still talking and</line>
<line>writing about the deportation of the Negroes, or about their settlement</line>
<line>within some restricted area, or about their settling in all parts of</line>
<line>the Union, or about their decline through their neglect of their</line>
<line>children, or about their rapid multiplication till they should expel</line>
<line>the whites from the South—of every sort of nonsense under heaven. All</line>
<line>this has given place to the simple plan of an indefinite extension</line>
<line>among the neglected classes of both races of the Hampton-Tuskegee</line>
<line>system of training. The “problem” in one sense has disappeared. The</line>
<line>future will have for the South swift or slow development of its masses</line>
<line>and of its soil in proportion to the swift or slow development of this</line>
<line>kind of training. This change of view is a true measure of Mr.</line>
<line>Washington’s work.</line>

<line>The literature of the Negro in America is colossal, from political</line>
<line>oratory through abolitionism to “Uncle Tom’s Cabin” and “Cotton is</line>
<line>King”—a vast mass of books which many men have read to the waste of</line>
<line>good years (and I among them); but the only books that I have read a</line>
<line>second time or ever care again to read in the whole list (most of them</line>
<line>by tiresome and unbalanced “reformers”) are “Uncle Remus” and “Up from</line>
<line>Slavery”; for these are the great literature of the subject. One has</line>
<line>all the best of the past, the other foreshadows a better future; and</line>
<line>the men who wrote them are the only men who have written of the subject</line>
<line>with that perfect frankness and perfect knowledge and perfect poise</line>
<line>whose other name is genius.</line>

<line>Mr. Washington has won a world-wide fame at an early age. His story of</line>
<line>his own life already has the distinction of translation into more</line>
<line>languages, I think, than any other American book; and I suppose that he</line>
<line>has as large a personal acquaintance among men of influence as any</line>
<line>private citizen now living.</line>

<line>His own teaching at Tuskegee is unique. He lectures to his advanced</line>
<line>students on the art of right living, not out of text-books, but</line>
<line>straight out of life. Then he sends them into the country to visit</line>
<line>Negro families. Such a student will come back with a minute report of</line>
<line>the way in which the family that he has seen lives, what their earnings</line>
<line>are, what they do well and what they do ill; and he will explain how</line>
<line>they might live better. He constructs a definite plan for the</line>
<line>betterment of that particular family out of the resources that they</line>
<line>have. Such a student, if he be bright, will profit more by an</line>
<line>experience like this than he could profit by all the books on sociology</line>
<line>and economics that ever were written. I talked with a boy at Tuskegee</line>
<line>who had made such a study as this, and I could not keep from</line>
<line>contrasting his knowledge and enthusiasm with what I heard in a class</line>
<line>room at a Negro university in one of the Southern cities, which is</line>
<line>conducted on the idea that a college course will save the soul. Here</line>
<line>the class was reciting a lesson from an abstruse text-book on</line>
<line>economics, reciting it by rote, with so obvious a failure to assimilate</line>
<line>it that the waste of labour was pitiful.</line>

<line>I asked Mr. Washington years ago what he regarded as the most important</line>
<line>result of his work, and he replied:</line>

<line>“I do not know which to put first, the effect of Tuskegee’s work on the</line>
<line>Negro, or the effect on the attitude of the white man to the Negro.”</line>

<line>The race divergence under the system of miseducation was fast getting</line>
<line>wider. Under the influence of the Hampton-Tuskegee idea the races are</line>
<line>coming into a closer sympathy and into an honourable and helpful</line>
<line>relation. As the Negro becomes economically independent, he becomes a</line>
<line>responsible part of the Southern life; and the whites so recognize him.</line>
<line>And this must be so from the nature of things. There is nothing</line>
<line>artificial about it. It is development in a perfectly natural way. And</line>
<line>the Southern whites not only so recognize it, but they are imitating it</line>
<line>in the teaching of the neglected masses of their own race. It has thus</line>
<line>come about that the school is taking a more direct and helpful hold on</line>
<line>life in the South than anywhere else in the country. Education is not a</line>
<line>thing apart from life—not a “system,” nor a philosophy; it is direct</line>
<line>teaching how to live and how to work.</line>

<line>To say that Mr. Washington has won the gratitude of all thoughtful</line>
<line>Southern white men, is to say that he has worked with the highest</line>
<line>practical wisdom at a large constructive task; for no plan for the</line>
<line>up-building of the freedman could succeed that ran counter to Southern</line>
<line>opinion. To win the support of Southern opinion and to shape it was a</line>
<line>necessary part of the task; and in this he has so well succeeded that</line>
<line>the South has a sincere and high regard for him. He once said to me</line>
<line>that he recalled the day, and remembered it thankfully, when he grew</line>
<line>large enough to regard a Southern white man as he regarded a Northern</line>
<line>one. It is well for our common country that the day is come when he and</line>
<line>his work are regarded as highly in the South as in any other part of</line>
<line>the Union. I think that no man of our generation has a more noteworthy</line>
<line>achievement to his credit than this; and it is an achievement of moral</line>
<line>earnestness of the strong character of a man who has done a great</line>
<line>national service.</line>

<line>Walter H. Page.</line>




<line>UP FROM SLAVERY</line>




</chapter>

<chapter>
<title>Chapter I. A Slave Among Slaves</title>


<line>I was born a slave on a plantation in Franklin County, Virginia. I am</line>
<line>not quite sure of the exact place or exact date of my birth, but at any</line>
<line>rate I suspect I must have been born somewhere and at some time. As</line>
<line>nearly as I have been able to learn, I was born near a cross-roads</line>
<line>post-office called Hale’s Ford, and the year was 1858 or 1859. I do not</line>
<line>know the month or the day. The earliest impressions I can now recall</line>
<line>are of the plantation and the slave quarters—the latter being the part</line>
<line>of the plantation where the slaves had their cabins.</line>

<line>My life had its beginning in the midst of the most miserable, desolate,</line>
<line>and discouraging surroundings. This was so, however, not because my</line>
<line>owners were especially cruel, for they were not, as compared with many</line>
<line>others. I was born in a typical log cabin, about fourteen by sixteen</line>
<line>feet square. In this cabin I lived with my mother and a brother and</line>
<line>sister till after the Civil War, when we were all declared free.</line>

<line>Of my ancestry I know almost nothing. In the slave quarters, and even</line>
<line>later, I heard whispered conversations among the coloured people of the</line>
<line>tortures which the slaves, including, no doubt, my ancestors on my</line>
<line>mother’s side, suffered in the middle passage of the slave ship while</line>
<line>being conveyed from Africa to America. I have been unsuccessful in</line>
<line>securing any information that would throw any accurate light upon the</line>
<line>history of my family beyond my mother. She, I remember, had a</line>
<line>half-brother and a half-sister. In the days of slavery not very much</line>
<line>attention was given to family history and family records—that is, black</line>
<line>family records. My mother, I suppose, attracted the attention of a</line>
<line>purchaser who was afterward my owner and hers. Her addition to the</line>
<line>slave family attracted about as much attention as the purchase of a new</line>
<line>horse or cow. Of my father I know even less than of my mother. I do not</line>
<line>even know his name. I have heard reports to the effect that he was a</line>
<line>white man who lived on one of the near-by plantations. Whoever he was,</line>
<line>I never heard of his taking the least interest in me or providing in</line>
<line>any way for my rearing. But I do not find especial fault with him. He</line>
<line>was simply another unfortunate victim of the institution which the</line>
<line>Nation unhappily had engrafted upon it at that time.</line>

<line>The cabin was not only our living-place, but was also used as the</line>
<line>kitchen for the plantation. My mother was the plantation cook. The</line>
<line>cabin was without glass windows; it had only openings in the side which</line>
<line>let in the light, and also the cold, chilly air of winter. There was a</line>
<line>door to the cabin—that is, something that was called a door—but the</line>
<line>uncertain hinges by which it was hung, and the large cracks in it, to</line>
<line>say nothing of the fact that it was too small, made the room a very</line>
<line>uncomfortable one. In addition to these openings there was, in the</line>
<line>lower right-hand corner of the room, the “cat-hole,”—a contrivance</line>
<line>which almost every mansion or cabin in Virginia possessed during the</line>
<line>ante-bellum period. The “cat-hole” was a square opening, about seven by</line>
<line>eight inches, provided for the purpose of letting the cat pass in and</line>
<line>out of the house at will during the night. In the case of our</line>
<line>particular cabin I could never understand the necessity for this</line>
<line>convenience, since there were at least a half-dozen other places in the</line>
<line>cabin that would have accommodated the cats. There was no wooden floor</line>
<line>in our cabin, the naked earth being used as a floor. In the centre of</line>
<line>the earthen floor there was a large, deep opening covered with boards,</line>
<line>which was used as a place in which to store sweet potatoes during the</line>
<line>winter. An impression of this potato-hole is very distinctly engraved</line>
<line>upon my memory, because I recall that during the process of putting the</line>
<line>potatoes in or taking them out I would often come into possession of</line>
<line>one or two, which I roasted and thoroughly enjoyed. There was no</line>
<line>cooking-stove on our plantation, and all the cooking for the whites and</line>
<line>slaves my mother had to do over an open fireplace, mostly in pots and</line>
<line>“skillets.” While the poorly built cabin caused us to suffer with cold</line>
<line>in the winter, the heat from the open fireplace in summer was equally</line>
<line>trying.</line>

<line>The early years of my life, which were spent in the little cabin, were</line>
<line>not very different from those of thousands of other slaves. My mother,</line>
<line>of course, had little time in which to give attention to the training</line>
<line>of her children during the day. She snatched a few moments for our care</line>
<line>in the early morning before her work began, and at night after the</line>
<line>day’s work was done. One of my earliest recollections is that of my</line>
<line>mother cooking a chicken late at night, and awakening her children for</line>
<line>the purpose of feeding them. How or where she got it I do not know. I</line>
<line>presume, however, it was procured from our owner’s farm. Some people</line>
<line>may call this theft. If such a thing were to happen now, I should</line>
<line>condemn it as theft myself. But taking place at the time it did, and</line>
<line>for the reason that it did, no one could ever make me believe that my</line>
<line>mother was guilty of thieving. She was simply a victim of the system of</line>
<line>slavery. I cannot remember having slept in a bed until after our family</line>
<line>was declared free by the Emancipation Proclamation. Three</line>
<line>children—John, my older brother, Amanda, my sister, and myself—had a</line>
<line>pallet on the dirt floor, or, to be more correct, we slept in and on a</line>
<line>bundle of filthy rags laid upon the dirt floor.</line>

<line>I was asked not long ago to tell something about the sports and</line>
<line>pastimes that I engaged in during my youth. Until that question was</line>
<line>asked it had never occurred to me that there was no period of my life</line>
<line>that was devoted to play. From the time that I can remember anything,</line>
<line>almost every day of my life had been occupied in some kind of labour;</line>
<line>though I think I would now be a more useful man if I had had time for</line>
<line>sports. During the period that I spent in slavery I was not large</line>
<line>enough to be of much service, still I was occupied most of the time in</line>
<line>cleaning the yards, carrying water to the men in the fields, or going</line>
<line>to the mill to which I used to take the corn, once a week, to be</line>
<line>ground. The mill was about three miles from the plantation. This work I</line>
<line>always dreaded. The heavy bag of corn would be thrown across the back</line>
<line>of the horse, and the corn divided about evenly on each side; but in</line>
<line>some way, almost without exception, on these trips, the corn would so</line>
<line>shift as to become unbalanced and would fall off the horse, and often I</line>
<line>would fall with it. As I was not strong enough to reload the corn upon</line>
<line>the horse, I would have to wait, sometimes for many hours, till a</line>
<line>chance passer-by came along who would help me out of my trouble. The</line>
<line>hours while waiting for some one were usually spent in crying. The time</line>
<line>consumed in this way made me late in reaching the mill, and by the time</line>
<line>I got my corn ground and reached home it would be far into the night.</line>
<line>The road was a lonely one, and often led through dense forests. I was</line>
<line>always frightened. The woods were said to be full of soldiers who had</line>
<line>deserted from the army, and I had been told that the first thing a</line>
<line>deserter did to a Negro boy when he found him alone was to cut off his</line>
<line>ears. Besides, when I was late in getting home I knew I would always</line>
<line>get a severe scolding or a flogging.</line>

<line>I had no schooling whatever while I was a slave, though I remember on</line>
<line>several occasions I went as far as the schoolhouse door with one of my</line>
<line>young mistresses to carry her books. The picture of several dozen boys</line>
<line>and girls in a schoolroom engaged in study made a deep impression upon</line>
<line>me, and I had the feeling that to get into a schoolhouse and study in</line>
<line>this way would be about the same as getting into paradise.</line>

<line>So far as I can now recall, the first knowledge that I got of the fact</line>
<line>that we were slaves, and that freedom of the slaves was being</line>
<line>discussed, was early one morning before day, when I was awakened by my</line>
<line>mother kneeling over her children and fervently praying that Lincoln</line>
<line>and his armies might be successful, and that one day she and her</line>
<line>children might be free. In this connection I have never been able to</line>
<line>understand how the slaves throughout the South, completely ignorant as</line>
<line>were the masses so far as books or newspapers were concerned, were able</line>
<line>to keep themselves so accurately and completely informed about the</line>
<line>great National questions that were agitating the country. From the time</line>
<line>that Garrison, Lovejoy, and others began to agitate for freedom, the</line>
<line>slaves throughout the South kept in close touch with the progress of</line>
<line>the movement. Though I was a mere child during the preparation for the</line>
<line>Civil War and during the war itself, I now recall the many</line>
<line>late-at-night whispered discussions that I heard my mother and the</line>
<line>other slaves on the plantation indulge in. These discussions showed</line>
<line>that they understood the situation, and that they kept themselves</line>
<line>informed of events by what was termed the “grape-vine” telegraph.</line>

<line>During the campaign when Lincoln was first a candidate for the</line>
<line>Presidency, the slaves on our far-off plantation, miles from any</line>
<line>railroad or large city or daily newspaper, knew what the issues</line>
<line>involved were. When war was begun between the North and the South,</line>
<line>every slave on our plantation felt and knew that, though other issues</line>
<line>were discussed, the primal one was that of slavery. Even the most</line>
<line>ignorant members of my race on the remote plantations felt in their</line>
<line>hearts, with a certainty that admitted of no doubt, that the freedom of</line>
<line>the slaves would be the one great result of the war, if the Northern</line>
<line>armies conquered. Every success of the Federal armies and every defeat</line>
<line>of the Confederate forces was watched with the keenest and most intense</line>
<line>interest. Often the slaves got knowledge of the results of great</line>
<line>battles before the white people received it. This news was usually</line>
<line>gotten from the coloured man who was sent to the post-office for the</line>
<line>mail. In our case the post-office was about three miles from the</line>
<line>plantation, and the mail came once or twice a week. The man who was</line>
<line>sent to the office would linger about the place long enough to get the</line>
<line>drift of the conversation from the group of white people who naturally</line>
<line>congregated there, after receiving their mail, to discuss the latest</line>
<line>news. The mail-carrier on his way back to our master’s house would as</line>
<line>naturally retail the news that he had secured among the slaves, and in</line>
<line>this way they often heard of important events before the white people</line>
<line>at the “big house,” as the master’s house was called.</line>

<line>I cannot remember a single instance during my childhood or early</line>
<line>boyhood when our entire family sat down to the table together, and</line>
<line>God’s blessing was asked, and the family ate a meal in a civilized</line>
<line>manner. On the plantation in Virginia, and even later, meals were</line>
<line>gotten by the children very much as dumb animals get theirs. It was a</line>
<line>piece of bread here and a scrap of meat there. It was a cup of milk at</line>
<line>one time and some potatoes at another. Sometimes a portion of our</line>
<line>family would eat out of the skillet or pot, while some one else would</line>
<line>eat from a tin plate held on the knees, and often using nothing but the</line>
<line>hands with which to hold the food. When I had grown to sufficient size,</line>
<line>I was required to go to the “big house” at meal-times to fan the flies</line>
<line>from the table by means of a large set of paper fans operated by a</line>
<line>pulley. Naturally much of the conversation of the white people turned</line>
<line>upon the subject of freedom and the war, and I absorbed a good deal of</line>
<line>it. I remember that at one time I saw two of my young mistresses and</line>
<line>some lady visitors eating ginger-cakes, in the yard. At that time those</line>
<line>cakes seemed to me to be absolutely the most tempting and desirable</line>
<line>things that I had ever seen; and I then and there resolved that, if I</line>
<line>ever got free, the height of my ambition would be reached if I could</line>
<line>get to the point where I could secure and eat ginger-cakes in the way</line>
<line>that I saw those ladies doing.</line>

<line>Of course as the war was prolonged the white people, in many cases,</line>
<line>often found it difficult to secure food for themselves. I think the</line>
<line>slaves felt the deprivation less than the whites, because the usual</line>
<line>diet for slaves was corn bread and pork, and these could be raised on</line>
<line>the plantation; but coffee, tea, sugar, and other articles which the</line>
<line>whites had been accustomed to use could not be raised on the</line>
<line>plantation, and the conditions brought about by the war frequently made</line>
<line>it impossible to secure these things. The whites were often in great</line>
<line>straits. Parched corn was used for coffee, and a kind of black molasses</line>
<line>was used instead of sugar. Many times nothing was used to sweeten the</line>
<line>so-called tea and coffee.</line>

<line>The first pair of shoes that I recall wearing were wooden ones. They</line>
<line>had rough leather on the top, but the bottoms, which were about an inch</line>
<line>thick, were of wood. When I walked they made a fearful noise, and</line>
<line>besides this they were very inconvenient, since there was no yielding</line>
<line>to the natural pressure of the foot. In wearing them one presented an</line>
<line>exceedingly awkward appearance. The most trying ordeal that I was</line>
<line>forced to endure as a slave boy, however, was the wearing of a flax</line>
<line>shirt. In the portion of Virginia where I lived it was common to use</line>
<line>flax as part of the clothing for the slaves. That part of the flax from</line>
<line>which our clothing was made was largely the refuse, which of course was</line>
<line>the cheapest and roughest part. I can scarcely imagine any torture,</line>
<line>except, perhaps, the pulling of a tooth, that is equal to that caused</line>
<line>by putting on a new flax shirt for the first time. It is almost equal</line>
<line>to the feeling that one would experience if he had a dozen or more</line>
<line>chestnut burrs, or a hundred small pin-points, in contact with his</line>
<line>flesh. Even to this day I can recall accurately the tortures that I</line>
<line>underwent when putting on one of these garments. The fact that my flesh</line>
<line>was soft and tender added to the pain. But I had no choice. I had to</line>
<line>wear the flax shirt or none; and had it been left to me to choose, I</line>
<line>should have chosen to wear no covering. In connection with the flax</line>
<line>shirt, my brother John, who is several years older than I am, performed</line>
<line>one of the most generous acts that I ever heard of one slave relative</line>
<line>doing for another. On several occasions when I was being forced to wear</line>
<line>a new flax shirt, he generously agreed to put it on in my stead and</line>
<line>wear it for several days, till it was “broken in.” Until I had grown to</line>
<line>be quite a youth this single garment was all that I wore.</line>

<line>One may get the idea, from what I have said, that there was bitter</line>
<line>feeling toward the white people on the part of my race, because of the</line>
<line>fact that most of the white population was away fighting in a war which</line>
<line>would result in keeping the Negro in slavery if the South was</line>
<line>successful. In the case of the slaves on our place this was not true,</line>
<line>and it was not true of any large portion of the slave population in the</line>
<line>South where the Negro was treated with anything like decency. During</line>
<line>the Civil War one of my young masters was killed, and two were severely</line>
<line>wounded. I recall the feeling of sorrow which existed among the slaves</line>
<line>when they heard of the death of “Mars’ Billy.” It was no sham sorrow,</line>
<line>but real. Some of the slaves had nursed “Mars’ Billy”; others had</line>
<line>played with him when he was a child. “Mars’ Billy” had begged for mercy</line>
<line>in the case of others when the overseer or master was thrashing them.</line>
<line>The sorrow in the slave quarter was only second to that in the “big</line>
<line>house.” When the two young masters were brought home wounded, the</line>
<line>sympathy of the slaves was shown in many ways. They were just as</line>
<line>anxious to assist in the nursing as the family relatives of the</line>
<line>wounded. Some of the slaves would even beg for the privilege of sitting</line>
<line>up at night to nurse their wounded masters. This tenderness and</line>
<line>sympathy on the part of those held in bondage was a result of their</line>
<line>kindly and generous nature. In order to defend and protect the women</line>
<line>and children who were left on the plantations when the white males went</line>
<line>to war, the slaves would have laid down their lives. The slave who was</line>
<line>selected to sleep in the “big house” during the absence of the males</line>
<line>was considered to have the place of honour. Any one attempting to harm</line>
<line>“young Mistress” or “old Mistress” during the night would have had to</line>
<line>cross the dead body of the slave to do so. I do not know how many have</line>
<line>noticed it, but I think that it will be found to be true that there are</line>
<line>few instances, either in slavery or freedom, in which a member of my</line>
<line>race has been known to betray a specific trust.</line>

<line>As a rule, not only did the members of my race entertain no feelings of</line>
<line>bitterness against the whites before and during the war, but there are</line>
<line>many instances of Negroes tenderly caring for their former masters and</line>
<line>mistresses who for some reason have become poor and dependent since the</line>
<line>war. I know of instances where the former masters of slaves have for</line>
<line>years been supplied with money by their former slaves to keep them from</line>
<line>suffering. I have known of still other cases in which the former slaves</line>
<line>have assisted in the education of the descendants of their former</line>
<line>owners. I know of a case on a large plantation in the South in which a</line>
<line>young white man, the son of the former owner of the estate, has become</line>
<line>so reduced in purse and self-control by reason of drink that he is a</line>
<line>pitiable creature; and yet, notwithstanding the poverty of the coloured</line>
<line>people themselves on this plantation, they have for years supplied this</line>
<line>young white man with the necessities of life. One sends him a little</line>
<line>coffee or sugar, another a little meat, and so on. Nothing that the</line>
<line>coloured people possess is too good for the son of “old Mars’ Tom,” who</line>
<line>will perhaps never be permitted to suffer while any remain on the place</line>
<line>who knew directly or indirectly of “old Mars’ Tom.”</line>

<line>I have said that there are few instances of a member of my race</line>
<line>betraying a specific trust. One of the best illustrations of this which</line>
<line>I know of is in the case of an ex-slave from Virginia whom I met not</line>
<line>long ago in a little town in the state of Ohio. I found that this man</line>
<line>had made a contract with his master, two or three years previous to the</line>
<line>Emancipation Proclamation, to the effect that the slave was to be</line>
<line>permitted to buy himself, by paying so much per year for his body; and</line>
<line>while he was paying for himself, he was to be permitted to labour where</line>
<line>and for whom he pleased. Finding that he could secure better wages in</line>
<line>Ohio, he went there. When freedom came, he was still in debt to his</line>
<line>master some three hundred dollars. Notwithstanding that the</line>
<line>Emancipation Proclamation freed him from any obligation to his master,</line>
<line>this black man walked the greater portion of the distance back to where</line>
<line>his old master lived in Virginia, and placed the last dollar, with</line>
<line>interest, in his hands. In talking to me about this, the man told me</line>
<line>that he knew that he did not have to pay the debt, but that he had</line>
<line>given his word to the master, and his word he had never broken. He felt</line>
<line>that he could not enjoy his freedom till he had fulfilled his promise.</line>

<line>From some things that I have said one may get the idea that some of the</line>
<line>slaves did not want freedom. This is not true. I have never seen one</line>
<line>who did not want to be free, or one who would return to slavery.</line>

<line>I pity from the bottom of my heart any nation or body of people that is</line>
<line>so unfortunate as to get entangled in the net of slavery. I have long</line>
<line>since ceased to cherish any spirit of bitterness against the Southern</line>
<line>white people on account of the enslavement of my race. No one section</line>
<line>of our country was wholly responsible for its introduction, and,</line>
<line>besides, it was recognized and protected for years by the General</line>
<line>Government. Having once got its tentacles fastened on to the economic</line>
<line>and social life of the Republic, it was no easy matter for the country</line>
<line>to relieve itself of the institution. Then, when we rid ourselves of</line>
<line>prejudice, or racial feeling, and look facts in the face, we must</line>
<line>acknowledge that, notwithstanding the cruelty and moral wrong of</line>
<line>slavery, the ten million Negroes inhabiting this country, who</line>
<line>themselves or whose ancestors went through the school of American</line>
<line>slavery, are in a stronger and more hopeful condition, materially,</line>
<line>intellectually, morally, and religiously, than is true of an equal</line>
<line>number of black people in any other portion of the globe. This is so to</line>
<line>such an extent that Negroes in this country, who themselves or whose</line>
<line>forefathers went through the school of slavery, are constantly</line>
<line>returning to Africa as missionaries to enlighten those who remained in</line>
<line>the fatherland. This I say, not to justify slavery—on the other hand, I</line>
<line>condemn it as an institution, as we all know that in America it was</line>
<line>established for selfish and financial reasons, and not from a</line>
<line>missionary motive—but to call attention to a fact, and to show how</line>
<line>Providence so often uses men and institutions to accomplish a purpose.</line>
<line>When persons ask me in these days how, in the midst of what sometimes</line>
<line>seem hopelessly discouraging conditions, I can have such faith in the</line>
<line>future of my race in this country, I remind them of the wilderness</line>
<line>through which and out of which, a good Providence has already led us.</line>

<line>Ever since I have been old enough to think for myself, I have</line>
<line>entertained the idea that, notwithstanding the cruel wrongs inflicted</line>
<line>upon us, the black man got nearly as much out of slavery as the white</line>
<line>man did. The hurtful influences of the institution were not by any</line>
<line>means confined to the Negro. This was fully illustrated by the life</line>
<line>upon our own plantation. The whole machinery of slavery was so</line>
<line>constructed as to cause labour, as a rule, to be looked upon as a badge</line>
<line>of degradation, of inferiority. Hence labour was something that both</line>
<line>races on the slave plantation sought to escape. The slave system on our</line>
<line>place, in a large measure, took the spirit of self-reliance and</line>
<line>self-help out of the white people. My old master had many boys and</line>
<line>girls, but not one, so far as I know, ever mastered a single trade or</line>
<line>special line of productive industry. The girls were not taught to cook,</line>
<line>sew, or to take care of the house. All of this was left to the slaves.</line>
<line>The slaves, of course, had little personal interest in the life of the</line>
<line>plantation, and their ignorance prevented them from learning how to do</line>
<line>things in the most improved and thorough manner. As a result of the</line>
<line>system, fences were out of repair, gates were hanging half off the</line>
<line>hinges, doors creaked, window-panes were out, plastering had fallen but</line>
<line>was not replaced, weeds grew in the yard. As a rule, there was food for</line>
<line>whites and blacks, but inside the house, and on the dining-room table,</line>
<line>there was wanting that delicacy and refinement of touch and finish</line>
<line>which can make a home the most convenient, comfortable, and attractive</line>
<line>place in the world. Withal there was a waste of food and other</line>
<line>materials which was sad. When freedom came, the slaves were almost as</line>
<line>well fitted to begin life anew as the master, except in the matter of</line>
<line>book-learning and ownership of property. The slave owner and his sons</line>
<line>had mastered no special industry. They unconsciously had imbibed the</line>
<line>feeling that manual labour was not the proper thing for them. On the</line>
<line>other hand, the slaves, in many cases, had mastered some handicraft,</line>
<line>and none were ashamed, and few unwilling, to labour.</line>

<line>Finally the war closed, and the day of freedom came. It was a momentous</line>
<line>and eventful day to all upon our plantation. We had been expecting it.</line>
<line>Freedom was in the air, and had been for months. Deserting soldiers</line>
<line>returning to their homes were to be seen every day. Others who had been</line>
<line>discharged, or whose regiments had been paroled, were constantly</line>
<line>passing near our place. The “grape-vine telegraph” was kept busy night</line>
<line>and day. The news and mutterings of great events were swiftly carried</line>
<line>from one plantation to another. In the fear of “Yankee” invasions, the</line>
<line>silverware and other valuables were taken from the “big house,” buried</line>
<line>in the woods, and guarded by trusted slaves. Woe be to any one who</line>
<line>would have attempted to disturb the buried treasure. The slaves would</line>
<line>give the Yankee soldiers food, drink, clothing—anything but that which</line>
<line>had been specifically intrusted to their care and honour. As the great</line>
<line>day drew nearer, there was more singing in the slave quarters than</line>
<line>usual. It was bolder, had more ring, and lasted later into the night.</line>
<line>Most of the verses of the plantation songs had some reference to</line>
<line>freedom. True, they had sung those same verses before, but they had</line>
<line>been careful to explain that the “freedom” in these songs referred to</line>
<line>the next world, and had no connection with life in this world. Now they</line>
<line>gradually threw off the mask, and were not afraid to let it be known</line>
<line>that the “freedom” in their songs meant freedom of the body in this</line>
<line>world. The night before the eventful day, word was sent to the slave</line>
<line>quarters to the effect that something unusual was going to take place</line>
<line>at the “big house” the next morning. There was little, if any, sleep</line>
<line>that night. All as excitement and expectancy. Early the next morning</line>
<line>word was sent to all the slaves, old and young, to gather at the house.</line>
<line>In company with my mother, brother, and sister, and a large number of</line>
<line>other slaves, I went to the master’s house. All of our master’s family</line>
<line>were either standing or seated on the veranda of the house, where they</line>
<line>could see what was to take place and hear what was said. There was a</line>
<line>feeling of deep interest, or perhaps sadness, on their faces, but not</line>
<line>bitterness. As I now recall the impression they made upon me, they did</line>
<line>not at the moment seem to be sad because of the loss of property, but</line>
<line>rather because of parting with those whom they had reared and who were</line>
<line>in many ways very close to them. The most distinct thing that I now</line>
<line>recall in connection with the scene was that some man who seemed to be</line>
<line>a stranger (a United States officer, I presume) made a little speech</line>
<line>and then read a rather long paper—the Emancipation Proclamation, I</line>
<line>think. After the reading we were told that we were all free, and could</line>
<line>go when and where we pleased. My mother, who was standing by my side,</line>
<line>leaned over and kissed her children, while tears of joy ran down her</line>
<line>cheeks. She explained to us what it all meant, that this was the day</line>
<line>for which she had been so long praying, but fearing that she would</line>
<line>never live to see.</line>

<line>For some minutes there was great rejoicing, and thanksgiving, and wild</line>
<line>scenes of ecstasy. But there was no feeling of bitterness. In fact,</line>
<line>there was pity among the slaves for our former owners. The wild</line>
<line>rejoicing on the part of the emancipated coloured people lasted but for</line>
<line>a brief period, for I noticed that by the time they returned to their</line>
<line>cabins there was a change in their feelings. The great responsibility</line>
<line>of being free, of having charge of themselves, of having to think and</line>
<line>plan for themselves and their children, seemed to take possession of</line>
<line>them. It was very much like suddenly turning a youth of ten or twelve</line>
<line>years out into the world to provide for himself. In a few hours the</line>
<line>great questions with which the Anglo-Saxon race had been grappling for</line>
<line>centuries had been thrown upon these people to be solved. These were</line>
<line>the questions of a home, a living, the rearing of children, education,</line>
<line>citizenship, and the establishment and support of churches. Was it any</line>
<line>wonder that within a few hours the wild rejoicing ceased and a feeling</line>
<line>of deep gloom seemed to pervade the slave quarters? To some it seemed</line>
<line>that, now that they were in actual possession of it, freedom was a more</line>
<line>serious thing than they had expected to find it. Some of the slaves</line>
<line>were seventy or eighty years old; their best days were gone. They had</line>
<line>no strength with which to earn a living in a strange place and among</line>
<line>strange people, even if they had been sure where to find a new place of</line>
<line>abode. To this class the problem seemed especially hard. Besides, deep</line>
<line>down in their hearts there was a strange and peculiar attachment to</line>
<line>“old Marster” and “old Missus,” and to their children, which they found</line>
<line>it hard to think of breaking off. With these they had spent in some</line>
<line>cases nearly a half-century, and it was no light thing to think of</line>
<line>parting. Gradually, one by one, stealthily at first, the older slaves</line>
<line>began to wander from the slave quarters back to the “big house” to have</line>
<line>a whispered conversation with their former owners as to the future.</line>




</chapter>

<chapter>
<title>Chapter II. Boyhood Days</title>


<line>After the coming of freedom there were two points upon which</line>
<line>practically all the people on our place were agreed, and I found that</line>
<line>this was generally true throughout the South: that they must change</line>
<line>their names, and that they must leave the old plantation for at least a</line>
<line>few days or weeks in order that they might really feel sure that they</line>
<line>were free.</line>

<line>In some way a feeling got among the coloured people that it was far</line>
<line>from proper for them to bear the surname of their former owners, and a</line>
<line>great many of them took other surnames. This was one of the first signs</line>
<line>of freedom. When they were slaves, a coloured person was simply called</line>
<line>“John” or “Susan.” There was seldom occasion for more than the use of</line>
<line>the one name. If “John” or “Susan” belonged to a white man by the name</line>
<line>of “Hatcher,” sometimes he was called “John Hatcher,” or as often</line>
<line>“Hatcher’s John.” But there was a feeling that “John Hatcher” or</line>
<line>“Hatcher’s John” was not the proper title by which to denote a freeman;</line>
<line>and so in many cases “John Hatcher” was changed to “John S. Lincoln” or</line>
<line>“John S. Sherman,” the initial “S” standing for no name, it being</line>
<line>simply a part of what the coloured man proudly called his “entitles.”</line>

<line>As I have stated, most of the coloured people left the old plantation</line>
<line>for a short while at least, so as to be sure, it seemed, that they</line>
<line>could leave and try their freedom on to see how it felt. After they had</line>
<line>remained away for a while, many of the older slaves, especially,</line>
<line>returned to their old homes and made some kind of contract with their</line>
<line>former owners by which they remained on the estate.</line>

<line>My mother’s husband, who was the stepfather of my brother John and</line>
<line>myself, did not belong to the same owners as did my mother. In fact, he</line>
<line>seldom came to our plantation. I remember seeing him there perhaps once</line>
<line>a year, that being about Christmas time. In some way, during the war,</line>
<line>by running away and following the Federal soldiers, it seems, he found</line>
<line>his way into the new state of West Virginia. As soon as freedom was</line>
<line>declared, he sent for my mother to come to the Kanawha Valley, in West</line>
<line>Virginia. At that time a journey from Virginia over the mountains to</line>
<line>West Virginia was rather a tedious and in some cases a painful</line>
<line>undertaking. What little clothing and few household goods we had were</line>
<line>placed in a cart, but the children walked the greater portion of the</line>
<line>distance, which was several hundred miles.</line>

<line>I do not think any of us ever had been very far from the plantation,</line>
<line>and the taking of a long journey into another state was quite an event.</line>
<line>The parting from our former owners and the members of our own race on</line>
<line>the plantation was a serious occasion. From the time of our parting</line>
<line>till their death we kept up a correspondence with the older members of</line>
<line>the family, and in later years we have kept in touch with those who</line>
<line>were the younger members. We were several weeks making the trip, and</line>
<line>most of the time we slept in the open air and did our cooking over a</line>
<line>log fire out-of-doors. One night I recall that we camped near an</line>
<line>abandoned log cabin, and my mother decided to build a fire in that for</line>
<line>cooking, and afterward to make a “pallet” on the floor for our</line>
<line>sleeping. Just as the fire had gotten well started a large black snake</line>
<line>fully a yard and a half long dropped down the chimney and ran out on</line>
<line>the floor. Of course we at once abandoned that cabin. Finally we</line>
<line>reached our destination—a little town called Malden, which is about</line>
<line>five miles from Charleston, the present capital of the state.</line>

<line>At that time salt-mining was the great industry in that part of West</line>
<line>Virginia, and the little town of Malden was right in the midst of the</line>
<line>salt-furnaces. My stepfather had already secured a job at a</line>
<line>salt-furnace, and he had also secured a little cabin for us to live in.</line>
<line>Our new house was no better than the one we had left on the old</line>
<line>plantation in Virginia. In fact, in one respect it was worse.</line>
<line>Notwithstanding the poor condition of our plantation cabin, we were at</line>
<line>all times sure of pure air. Our new home was in the midst of a cluster</line>
<line>of cabins crowded closely together, and as there were no sanitary</line>
<line>regulations, the filth about the cabins was often intolerable. Some of</line>
<line>our neighbours were coloured people, and some were the poorest and most</line>
<line>ignorant and degraded white people. It was a motley mixture. Drinking,</line>
<line>gambling, quarrels, fights, and shockingly immoral practices were</line>
<line>frequent. All who lived in the little town were in one way or another</line>
<line>connected with the salt business. Though I was a mere child, my</line>
<line>stepfather put me and my brother at work in one of the furnaces. Often</line>
<line>I began work as early as four o’clock in the morning.</line>

<line>The first thing I ever learned in the way of book knowledge was while</line>
<line>working in this salt-furnace. Each salt-packer had his barrels marked</line>
<line>with a certain number. The number allotted to my stepfather was “18.”</line>
<line>At the close of the day’s work the boss of the packers would come</line>
<line>around and put “18” on each of our barrels, and I soon learned to</line>
<line>recognize that figure wherever I saw it, and after a while got to the</line>
<line>point where I could make that figure, though I knew nothing about any</line>
<line>other figures or letters.</line>

<line>From the time that I can remember having any thoughts about anything, I</line>
<line>recall that I had an intense longing to learn to read. I determined,</line>
<line>when quite a small child, that, if I accomplished nothing else in life,</line>
<line>I would in some way get enough education to enable me to read common</line>
<line>books and newspapers. Soon after we got settled in some manner in our</line>
<line>new cabin in West Virginia, I induced my mother to get hold of a book</line>
<line>for me. How or where she got it I do not know, but in some way she</line>
<line>procured an old copy of Webster’s “blue-back” spelling-book, which</line>
<line>contained the alphabet, followed by such meaningless words as “ab,”</line>
<line>“ba,” “ca,” “da.” I began at once to devour this book, and I think that</line>
<line>it was the first one I ever had in my hands. I had learned from</line>
<line>somebody that the way to begin to read was to learn the alphabet, so I</line>
<line>tried in all the ways I could think of to learn it,—all of course</line>
<line>without a teacher, for I could find no one to teach me. At that time</line>
<line>there was not a single member of my race anywhere near us who could</line>
<line>read, and I was too timid to approach any of the white people. In some</line>
<line>way, within a few weeks, I mastered the greater portion of the</line>
<line>alphabet. In all my efforts to learn to read my mother shared fully my</line>
<line>ambition, and sympathized with me and aided me in every way that she</line>
<line>could. Though she was totally ignorant, she had high ambitions for her</line>
<line>children, and a large fund of good, hard, common sense, which seemed to</line>
<line>enable her to meet and master every situation. If I have done anything</line>
<line>in life worth attention, I feel sure that I inherited the disposition</line>
<line>from my mother.</line>

<line>In the midst of my struggles and longing for an education, a young</line>
<line>coloured boy who had learned to read in the state of Ohio came to</line>
<line>Malden. As soon as the coloured people found out that he could read, a</line>
<line>newspaper was secured, and at the close of nearly every day’s work this</line>
<line>young man would be surrounded by a group of men and women who were</line>
<line>anxious to hear him read the news contained in the papers. How I used</line>
<line>to envy this man! He seemed to me to be the one young man in all the</line>
<line>world who ought to be satisfied with his attainments.</line>

<line>About this time the question of having some kind of a school opened for</line>
<line>the coloured children in the village began to be discussed by members</line>
<line>of the race. As it would be the first school for Negro children that</line>
<line>had ever been opened in that part of Virginia, it was, of course, to be</line>
<line>a great event, and the discussion excited the wildest interest. The</line>
<line>most perplexing question was where to find a teacher. The young man</line>
<line>from Ohio who had learned to read the papers was considered, but his</line>
<line>age was against him. In the midst of the discussion about a teacher,</line>
<line>another young coloured man from Ohio, who had been a soldier, in some</line>
<line>way found his way into town. It was soon learned that he possessed</line>
<line>considerable education, and he was engaged by the coloured people to</line>
<line>teach their first school. As yet no free schools had been started for</line>
<line>coloured people in that section, hence each family agreed to pay a</line>
<line>certain amount per month, with the understanding that the teacher was</line>
<line>to “board ’round”—that is, spend a day with each family. This was not</line>
<line>bad for the teacher, for each family tried to provide the very best on</line>
<line>the day the teacher was to be its guest. I recall that I looked forward</line>
<line>with an anxious appetite to the “teacher’s day” at our little cabin.</line>

<line>This experience of a whole race beginning to go to school for the first</line>
<line>time, presents one of the most interesting studies that has ever</line>
<line>occurred in connection with the development of any race. Few people who</line>
<line>were not right in the midst of the scenes can form any exact idea of</line>
<line>the intense desire which the people of my race showed for an education.</line>
<line>As I have stated, it was a whole race trying to go to school. Few were</line>
<line>too young, and none too old, to make the attempt to learn. As fast as</line>
<line>any kind of teachers could be secured, not only were day-schools</line>
<line>filled, but night-schools as well. The great ambition of the older</line>
<line>people was to try to learn to read the Bible before they died. With</line>
<line>this end in view men and women who were fifty or seventy-five years old</line>
<line>would often be found in the night-school. Some day-schools were formed</line>
<line>soon after freedom, but the principal book studied in the Sunday-school</line>
<line>was the spelling-book. Day-school, night-school, Sunday-school, were</line>
<line>always crowded, and often many had to be turned away for want of room.</line>

<line>The opening of the school in the Kanawha Valley, however, brought to me</line>
<line>one of the keenest disappointments that I ever experienced. I had been</line>
<line>working in a salt-furnace for several months, and my stepfather had</line>
<line>discovered that I had a financial value, and so, when the school</line>
<line>opened, he decided that he could not spare me from my work. This</line>
<line>decision seemed to cloud my every ambition. The disappointment was made</line>
<line>all the more severe by reason of the fact that my place of work was</line>
<line>where I could see the happy children passing to and from school</line>
<line>mornings and afternoons. Despite this disappointment, however, I</line>
<line>determined that I would learn something, anyway. I applied myself with</line>
<line>greater earnestness than ever to the mastering of what was in the</line>
<line>“blue-back” speller.</line>

<line>My mother sympathized with me in my disappointment, and sought to</line>
<line>comfort me in all the ways she could, and to help me find a way to</line>
<line>learn. After a while I succeeded in making arrangements with the</line>
<line>teacher to give me some lessons at night, after the day’s work was</line>
<line>done. These night lessons were so welcome that I think I learned more</line>
<line>at night than the other children did during the day. My own experiences</line>
<line>in the night-school gave me faith in the night-school idea, with which,</line>
<line>in after years, I had to do both at Hampton and Tuskegee. But my boyish</line>
<line>heart was still set upon going to the day-school, and I let no</line>
<line>opportunity slip to push my case. Finally I won, and was permitted to</line>
<line>go to the school in the day for a few months, with the understanding</line>
<line>that I was to rise early in the morning and work in the furnace till</line>
<line>nine o’clock, and return immediately after school closed in the</line>
<line>afternoon for at least two more hours of work.</line>

<line>The schoolhouse was some distance from the furnace, and as I had to</line>
<line>work till nine o’clock, and the school opened at nine, I found myself</line>
<line>in a difficulty. School would always be begun before I reached it, and</line>
<line>sometimes my class had recited. To get around this difficulty I yielded</line>
<line>to a temptation for which most people, I suppose, will condemn me; but</line>
<line>since it is a fact, I might as well state it. I have great faith in the</line>
<line>power and influence of facts. It is seldom that anything is permanently</line>
<line>gained by holding back a fact. There was a large clock in a little</line>
<line>office in the furnace. This clock, of course, all the hundred or more</line>
<line>workmen depended upon to regulate their hours of beginning and ending</line>
<line>the day’s work. I got the idea that the way for me to reach school on</line>
<line>time was to move the clock hands from half-past eight up to the nine</line>
<line>o’clock mark. This I found myself doing morning after morning, till the</line>
<line>furnace “boss” discovered that something was wrong, and locked the</line>
<line>clock in a case. I did not mean to inconvenience anybody. I simply</line>
<line>meant to reach that schoolhouse in time.</line>

<line>When, however, I found myself at the school for the first time, I also</line>
<line>found myself confronted with two other difficulties. In the first</line>
<line>place, I found that all the other children wore hats or caps on their</line>
<line>heads, and I had neither hat nor cap. In fact, I do not remember that</line>
<line>up to the time of going to school I had ever worn any kind of covering</line>
<line>upon my head, nor do I recall that either I or anybody else had even</line>
<line>thought anything about the need of covering for my head. But, of</line>
<line>course, when I saw how all the other boys were dressed, I began to feel</line>
<line>quite uncomfortable. As usual, I put the case before my mother, and she</line>
<line>explained to me that she had no money with which to buy a “store hat,”</line>
<line>which was a rather new institution at that time among the members of my</line>
<line>race and was considered quite the thing for young and old to own, but</line>
<line>that she would find a way to help me out of the difficulty. She</line>
<line>accordingly got two pieces of “homespun” (jeans) and sewed them</line>
<line>together, and I was soon the proud possessor of my first cap.</line>

<line>The lesson that my mother taught me in this has always remained with</line>
<line>me, and I have tried as best as I could to teach it to others. I have</line>
<line>always felt proud, whenever I think of the incident, that my mother had</line>
<line>strength of character enough not to be led into the temptation of</line>
<line>seeming to be that which she was not—of trying to impress my</line>
<line>schoolmates and others with the fact that she was able to buy me a</line>
<line>“store hat” when she was not. I have always felt proud that she refused</line>
<line>to go into debt for that which she did not have the money to pay for.</line>
<line>Since that time I have owned many kinds of caps and hats, but never one</line>
<line>of which I have felt so proud as of the cap made of the two pieces of</line>
<line>cloth sewed together by my mother. I have noted the fact, but without</line>
<line>satisfaction, I need not add, that several of the boys who began their</line>
<line>careers with “store hats” and who were my schoolmates and used to join</line>
<line>in the sport that was made of me because I had only a “homespun” cap,</line>
<line>have ended their careers in the penitentiary, while others are not able</line>
<line>now to buy any kind of hat.</line>

<line>My second difficulty was with regard to my name, or rather _A_ name.</line>
<line>From the time when I could remember anything, I had been called simply</line>
<line>“Booker.” Before going to school it had never occurred to me that it</line>
<line>was needful or appropriate to have an additional name. When I heard the</line>
<line>school-roll called, I noticed that all of the children had at least two</line>
<line>names, and some of them indulged in what seemed to me the extravagance</line>
<line>of having three. I was in deep perplexity, because I knew that the</line>
<line>teacher would demand of me at least two names, and I had only one. By</line>
<line>the time the occasion came for the enrolling of my name, an idea</line>
<line>occurred to me which I thought would make me equal to the situation;</line>
<line>and so, when the teacher asked me what my full name was, I calmly told</line>
<line>him “Booker Washington,” as if I had been called by that name all my</line>
<line>life; and by that name I have since been known. Later in my life I</line>
<line>found that my mother had given me the name of “Booker Taliaferro” soon</line>
<line>after I was born, but in some way that part of my name seemed to</line>
<line>disappear and for a long while was forgotten, but as soon as I found</line>
<line>out about it I revived it, and made my full name “Booker Taliaferro</line>
<line>Washington.” I think there are not many men in our country who have had</line>
<line>the privilege of naming themselves in the way that I have.</line>

<line>More than once I have tried to picture myself in the position of a boy</line>
<line>or man with an honoured and distinguished ancestry which I could trace</line>
<line>back through a period of hundreds of years, and who had not only</line>
<line>inherited a name, but fortune and a proud family homestead; and yet I</line>
<line>have sometimes had the feeling that if I had inherited these, and had</line>
<line>been a member of a more popular race, I should have been inclined to</line>
<line>yield to the temptation of depending upon my ancestry and my colour to</line>
<line>do that for me which I should do for myself. Years ago I resolved that</line>
<line>because I had no ancestry myself I would leave a record of which my</line>
<line>children would be proud, and which might encourage them to still higher</line>
<line>effort.</line>

<line>The world should not pass judgment upon the Negro, and especially the</line>
<line>Negro youth, too quickly or too harshly. The Negro boy has obstacles,</line>
<line>discouragements, and temptations to battle with that are little known</line>
<line>to those not situated as he is. When a white boy undertakes a task, it</line>
<line>is taken for granted that he will succeed. On the other hand, people</line>
<line>are usually surprised if the Negro boy does not fail. In a word, the</line>
<line>Negro youth starts out with the presumption against him.</line>

<line>The influence of ancestry, however, is important in helping forward any</line>
<line>individual or race, if too much reliance is not placed upon it. Those</line>
<line>who constantly direct attention to the Negro youth’s moral weaknesses,</line>
<line>and compare his advancement with that of white youths, do not consider</line>
<line>the influence of the memories which cling about the old family</line>
<line>homesteads. I have no idea, as I have stated elsewhere, who my</line>
<line>grandmother was. I have, or have had, uncles and aunts and cousins, but</line>
<line>I have no knowledge as to where most of them are. My case will</line>
<line>illustrate that of hundreds of thousands of black people in every part</line>
<line>of our country. The very fact that the white boy is conscious that, if</line>
<line>he fails in life, he will disgrace the whole family record, extending</line>
<line>back through many generations, is of tremendous value in helping him to</line>
<line>resist temptations. The fact that the individual has behind and</line>
<line>surrounding him proud family history and connection serves as a</line>
<line>stimulus to help him to overcome obstacles when striving for success.</line>

<line>The time that I was permitted to attend school during the day was</line>
<line>short, and my attendance was irregular. It was not long before I had to</line>
<line>stop attending day-school altogether, and devote all of my time again</line>
<line>to work. I resorted to the night-school again. In fact, the greater</line>
<line>part of the education I secured in my boyhood was gathered through the</line>
<line>night-school after my day’s work was done. I had difficulty often in</line>
<line>securing a satisfactory teacher. Sometimes, after I had secured some</line>
<line>one to teach me at night, I would find, much to my disappointment, that</line>
<line>the teacher knew but little more than I did. Often I would have to walk</line>
<line>several miles at night in order to recite my night-school lessons.</line>
<line>There was never a time in my youth, no matter how dark and discouraging</line>
<line>the days might be, when one resolve did not continually remain with me,</line>
<line>and that was a determination to secure an education at any cost.</line>

<line>Soon after we moved to West Virginia, my mother adopted into our</line>
<line>family, notwithstanding our poverty, an orphan boy, to whom afterward</line>
<line>we gave the name of James B. Washington. He has ever since remained a</line>
<line>member of the family.</line>

<line>After I had worked in the salt-furnace for some time, work was secured</line>
<line>for me in a coal-mine which was operated mainly for the purpose of</line>
<line>securing fuel for the salt-furnace. Work in the coal-mine I always</line>
<line>dreaded. One reason for this was that any one who worked in a coal-mine</line>
<line>was always unclean, at least while at work, and it was a very hard job</line>
<line>to get one’s skin clean after the day’s work was over. Then it was</line>
<line>fully a mile from the opening of the coal-mine to the face of the coal,</line>
<line>and all, of course, was in the blackest darkness. I do not believe that</line>
<line>one ever experiences anywhere else such darkness as he does in a</line>
<line>coal-mine. The mine was divided into a large number of different</line>
<line>“rooms” or departments, and, as I never was able to learn the location</line>
<line>of all these “rooms,” I many times found myself lost in the mine. To</line>
<line>add to the horror of being lost, sometimes my light would go out, and</line>
<line>then, if I did not happen to have a match, I would wander about in the</line>
<line>darkness until by chance I found some one to give me a light. The work</line>
<line>was not only hard, but it was dangerous. There was always the danger of</line>
<line>being blown to pieces by a premature explosion of powder, or of being</line>
<line>crushed by falling slate. Accidents from one or the other of these</line>
<line>causes were frequently occurring, and this kept me in constant fear.</line>
<line>Many children of the tenderest years were compelled then, as is now</line>
<line>true I fear, in most coal-mining districts, to spend a large part of</line>
<line>their lives in these coal-mines, with little opportunity to get an</line>
<line>education; and, what is worse, I have often noted that, as a rule,</line>
<line>young boys who begin life in a coal-mine are often physically and</line>
<line>mentally dwarfed. They soon lose ambition to do anything else than to</line>
<line>continue as a coal-miner.</line>

<line>In those days, and later as a young man, I used to try to picture in my</line>
<line>imagination the feelings and ambitions of a white boy with absolutely</line>
<line>no limit placed upon his aspirations and activities. I used to envy the</line>
<line>white boy who had no obstacles placed in the way of his becoming a</line>
