<line>Congressman, Governor, Bishop, or President by reason of the accident</line>
<line>of his birth or race. I used to picture the way that I would act under</line>
<line>such circumstances; how I would begin at the bottom and keep rising</line>
<line>until I reached the highest round of success.</line>

<line>In later years, I confess that I do not envy the white boy as I once</line>
<line>did. I have learned that success is to be measured not so much by the</line>
<line>position that one has reached in life as by the obstacles which he has</line>
<line>overcome while trying to succeed. Looked at from this standpoint, I</line>
<line>almost reached the conclusion that often the Negro boy’s birth and</line>
<line>connection with an unpopular race is an advantage, so far as real life</line>
<line>is concerned. With few exceptions, the Negro youth must work harder and</line>
<line>must perform his tasks even better than a white youth in order to</line>
<line>secure recognition. But out of the hard and unusual struggle through</line>
<line>which he is compelled to pass, he gets a strength, a confidence, that</line>
<line>one misses whose pathway is comparatively smooth by reason of birth and</line>
<line>race.</line>

<line>From any point of view, I had rather be what I am, a member of the</line>
<line>Negro race, than be able to claim membership with the most favoured of</line>
<line>any other race. I have always been made sad when I have heard members</line>
<line>of any race claiming rights or privileges, or certain badges of</line>
<line>distinction, on the ground simply that they were members of this or</line>
<line>that race, regardless of their own individual worth or attainments. I</line>
<line>have been made to feel sad for such persons because I am conscious of</line>
<line>the fact that mere connection with what is known as a superior race</line>
<line>will not permanently carry an individual forward unless he has</line>
<line>individual worth, and mere connection with what is regarded as an</line>
<line>inferior race will not finally hold an individual back if he possesses</line>
<line>intrinsic, individual merit. Every persecuted individual and race</line>
<line>should get much consolation out of the great human law, which is</line>
<line>universal and eternal, that merit, no matter under what skin found, is,</line>
<line>in the long run, recognized and rewarded. This I have said here, not to</line>
<line>call attention to myself as an individual, but to the race to which I</line>
<line>am proud to belong.</line>




</chapter>

<chapter>
<title>Chapter III. The Struggle For An Education</title>


<line>One day, while at work in the coal-mine, I happened to overhear two</line>
<line>miners talking about a great school for coloured people somewhere in</line>
<line>Virginia. This was the first time that I had ever heard anything about</line>
<line>any kind of school or college that was more pretentious than the little</line>
<line>coloured school in our town.</line>

<line>In the darkness of the mine I noiselessly crept as close as I could to</line>
<line>the two men who were talking. I heard one tell the other that not only</line>
<line>was the school established for the members of any race, but the</line>
<line>opportunities that it provided by which poor but worthy students could</line>
<line>work out all or a part of the cost of a board, and at the same time be</line>
<line>taught some trade or industry.</line>

<line>As they went on describing the school, it seemed to me that it must be</line>
<line>the greatest place on earth, and not even Heaven presented more</line>
<line>attractions for me at that time than did the Hampton Normal and</line>
<line>Agricultural Institute in Virginia, about which these men were talking.</line>
<line>I resolved at once to go to that school, although I had no idea where</line>
<line>it was, or how many miles away, or how I was going to reach it; I</line>
<line>remembered only that I was on fire constantly with one ambition, and</line>
<line>that was to go to Hampton. This thought was with me day and night.</line>

<line>After hearing of the Hampton Institute, I continued to work for a few</line>
<line>months longer in the coal-mine. While at work there, I heard of a</line>
<line>vacant position in the household of General Lewis Ruffner, the owner of</line>
<line>the salt-furnace and coal-mine. Mrs. Viola Ruffner, the wife of General</line>
<line>Ruffner, was a “Yankee” woman from Vermont. Mrs. Ruffner had a</line>
<line>reputation all through the vicinity for being very strict with her</line>
<line>servants, and especially with the boys who tried to serve her. Few of</line>
<line>them remained with her more than two or three weeks. They all left with</line>
<line>the same excuse: she was too strict. I decided, however, that I would</line>
<line>rather try Mrs. Ruffner’s house than remain in the coal-mine, and so my</line>
<line>mother applied to her for the vacant position. I was hired at a salary</line>
<line>of $5 per month.</line>

<line>I had heard so much about Mrs. Ruffner’s severity that I was almost</line>
<line>afraid to see her, and trembled when I went into her presence. I had</line>
<line>not lived with her many weeks, however, before I began to understand</line>
<line>her. I soon began to learn that, first of all, she wanted everything</line>
<line>kept clean about her, that she wanted things done promptly and</line>
<line>systematically, and that at the bottom of everything she wanted</line>
<line>absolute honesty and frankness. Nothing must be sloven or slipshod;</line>
<line>every door, every fence, must be kept in repair.</line>

<line>I cannot now recall how long I lived with Mrs. Ruffner before going to</line>
<line>Hampton, but I think it must have been a year and a half. At any rate,</line>
<line>I here repeat what I have said more than once before, that the lessons</line>
<line>that I learned in the home of Mrs. Ruffner were as valuable to me as</line>
<line>any education I have ever gotten anywhere else. Even to this day I</line>
<line>never see bits of paper scattered around a house or in the street that</line>
<line>I do not want to pick them up at once. I never see a filthy yard that I</line>
<line>do not want to clean it, a paling off of a fence that I do not want to</line>
<line>put it on, an unpainted or unwhitewashed house that I do not want to</line>
<line>paint or whitewash it, or a button off one’s clothes, or a grease-spot</line>
<line>on them or on a floor, that I do not want to call attention to it.</line>

<line>From fearing Mrs. Ruffner I soon learned to look upon her as one of my</line>
<line>best friends. When she found that she could trust me she did so</line>
<line>implicitly. During the one or two winters that I was with her she gave</line>
<line>me an opportunity to go to school for an hour in the day during a</line>
<line>portion of the winter months, but most of my studying was done at</line>
<line>night, sometimes alone, sometimes under some one whom I could hire to</line>
<line>teach me. Mrs. Ruffner always encouraged and sympathized with me in all</line>
<line>my efforts to get an education. It was while living with her that I</line>
<line>began to get together my first library. I secured a dry-goods box,</line>
<line>knocked out one side of it, put some shelves in it, and began putting</line>
<line>into it every kind of book that I could get my hands upon, and called</line>
<line>it my “library.”</line>

<line>Notwithstanding my success at Mrs. Ruffner’s I did not give up the idea</line>
<line>of going to the Hampton Institute. In the fall of 1872 I determined to</line>
<line>make an effort to get there, although, as I have stated, I had no</line>
<line>definite idea of the direction in which Hampton was, or of what it</line>
<line>would cost to go there. I do not think that any one thoroughly</line>
<line>sympathized with me in my ambition to go to Hampton unless it was my</line>
<line>mother, and she was troubled with a grave fear that I was starting out</line>
<line>on a “wild-goose chase.” At any rate, I got only a half-hearted consent</line>
<line>from her that I might start. The small amount of money that I had</line>
<line>earned had been consumed by my stepfather and the remainder of the</line>
<line>family, with the exception of a very few dollars, and so I had very</line>
<line>little with which to buy clothes and pay my travelling expenses. My</line>
<line>brother John helped me all that he could, but of course that was not a</line>
<line>great deal, for his work was in the coal-mine, where he did not earn</line>
<line>much, and most of what he did earn went in the direction of paying the</line>
<line>household expenses.</line>

<line>Perhaps the thing that touched and pleased me most in connection with</line>
<line>my starting for Hampton was the interest that many of the older</line>
<line>coloured people took in the matter. They had spent the best days of</line>
<line>their lives in slavery, and hardly expected to live to see the time</line>
<line>when they would see a member of their race leave home to attend a</line>
<line>boarding-school. Some of these older people would give me a nickel,</line>
<line>others a quarter, or a handkerchief.</line>

<line>Finally the great day came, and I started for Hampton. I had only a</line>
<line>small, cheap satchel that contained a few articles of clothing I could</line>
<line>get. My mother at the time was rather weak and broken in health. I</line>
<line>hardly expected to see her again, and thus our parting was all the more</line>
<line>sad. She, however, was very brave through it all. At that time there</line>
<line>were no through trains connecting that part of West Virginia with</line>
<line>eastern Virginia. Trains ran only a portion of the way, and the</line>
<line>remainder of the distance was travelled by stage-coaches.</line>

<line>The distance from Malden to Hampton is about five hundred miles. I had</line>
<line>not been away from home many hours before it began to grow painfully</line>
<line>evident that I did not have enough money to pay my fare to Hampton. One</line>
<line>experience I shall long remember. I had been travelling over the</line>
<line>mountains most of the afternoon in an old-fashion stage-coach, when,</line>
<line>late in the evening, the coach stopped for the night at a common,</line>
<line>unpainted house called a hotel. All the other passengers except myself</line>
<line>were whites. In my ignorance I supposed that the little hotel existed</line>
<line>for the purpose of accommodating the passengers who travelled on the</line>
<line>stage-coach. The difference that the colour of one’s skin would make I</line>
<line>had not thought anything about. After all the other passengers had been</line>
<line>shown rooms and were getting ready for supper, I shyly presented myself</line>
<line>before the man at the desk. It is true I had practically no money in my</line>
<line>pocket with which to pay for bed or food, but I had hoped in some way</line>
<line>to beg my way into the good graces of the landlord, for at that season</line>
<line>in the mountains of Virginia the weather was cold, and I wanted to get</line>
<line>indoors for the night. Without asking as to whether I had any money,</line>
<line>the man at the desk firmly refused to even consider the matter of</line>
<line>providing me with food or lodging. This was my first experience in</line>
<line>finding out what the colour of my skin meant. In some way I managed to</line>
<line>keep warm by walking about, and so got through the night. My whole soul</line>
<line>was so bent upon reaching Hampton that I did not have time to cherish</line>
<line>any bitterness toward the hotel-keeper.</line>

<line>By walking, begging rides both in wagons and in the cars, in some way,</line>
<line>after a number of days, I reached the city of Richmond, Virginia, about</line>
<line>eighty-two miles from Hampton. When I reached there, tired, hungry, and</line>
<line>dirty, it was late in the night. I had never been in a large city, and</line>
<line>this rather added to my misery. When I reached Richmond, I was</line>
<line>completely out of money. I had not a single acquaintance in the place,</line>
<line>and, being unused to city ways, I did not know where to go. I applied</line>
<line>at several places for lodging, but they all wanted money, and that was</line>
<line>what I did not have. Knowing nothing else better to do, I walked the</line>
<line>streets. In doing this I passed by many food-stands where fried chicken</line>
<line>and half-moon apple pies were piled high and made to present a most</line>
<line>tempting appearance. At that time it seemed to me that I would have</line>
<line>promised all that I expected to possess in the future to have gotten</line>
<line>hold of one of those chicken legs or one of those pies. But I could not</line>
<line>get either of these, nor anything else to eat.</line>

<line>I must have walked the streets till after midnight. At last I became so</line>
<line>exhausted that I could walk no longer. I was tired, I was hungry, I was</line>
<line>everything but discouraged. Just about the time when I reached extreme</line>
<line>physical exhaustion, I came upon a portion of a street where the board</line>
<line>sidewalk was considerably elevated. I waited for a few minutes, till I</line>
<line>was sure that no passers-by could see me, and then crept under the</line>
<line>sidewalk and lay for the night upon the ground, with my satchel of</line>
<line>clothing for a pillow. Nearly all night I could hear the tramp of feet</line>
<line>over my head. The next morning I found myself somewhat refreshed, but I</line>
<line>was extremely hungry, because it had been a long time since I had had</line>
<line>sufficient food. As soon as it became light enough for me to see my</line>
<line>surroundings I noticed that I was near a large ship, and that this ship</line>
<line>seemed to be unloading a cargo of pig iron. I went at once to the</line>
<line>vessel and asked the captain to permit me to help unload the vessel in</line>
<line>order to get money for food. The captain, a white man, who seemed to be</line>
<line>kind-hearted, consented. I worked long enough to earn money for my</line>
<line>breakfast, and it seems to me, as I remember it now, to have been about</line>
<line>the best breakfast that I have ever eaten.</line>

<line>My work pleased the captain so well that he told me if I desired I</line>
<line>could continue working for a small amount per day. This I was very glad</line>
<line>to do. I continued working on this vessel for a number of days. After</line>
<line>buying food with the small wages I received there was not much left to</line>
<line>add on the amount I must get to pay my way to Hampton. In order to</line>
<line>economize in every way possible, so as to be sure to reach Hampton in a</line>
<line>reasonable time, I continued to sleep under the same sidewalk that gave</line>
<line>me shelter the first night I was in Richmond. Many years after that the</line>
<line>coloured citizens of Richmond very kindly tendered me a reception at</line>
<line>which there must have been two thousand people present. This reception</line>
<line>was held not far from the spot where I slept the first night I spent in</line>
<line>the city, and I must confess that my mind was more upon the sidewalk</line>
<line>that first gave me shelter than upon the recognition, agreeable and</line>
<line>cordial as it was.</line>

<line>When I had saved what I considered enough money with which to reach</line>
<line>Hampton, I thanked the captain of the vessel for his kindness, and</line>
<line>started again. Without any unusual occurrence I reached Hampton, with a</line>
<line>surplus of exactly fifty cents with which to begin my education. To me</line>
<line>it had been a long, eventful journey; but the first sight of the large,</line>
<line>three-story, brick school building seemed to have rewarded me for all</line>
<line>that I had undergone in order to reach the place. If the people who</line>
<line>gave the money to provide that building could appreciate the influence</line>
<line>the sight of it had upon me, as well as upon thousands of other youths,</line>
<line>they would feel all the more encouraged to make such gifts. It seemed</line>
<line>to me to be the largest and most beautiful building I had ever seen.</line>
<line>The sight of it seemed to give me new life. I felt that a new kind of</line>
<line>existence had now begun—that life would now have a new meaning. I felt</line>
<line>that I had reached the promised land, and I resolved to let no obstacle</line>
<line>prevent me from putting forth the highest effort to fit myself to</line>
<line>accomplish the most good in the world.</line>

<line>As soon as possible after reaching the grounds of the Hampton</line>
<line>Institute, I presented myself before the head teacher for an assignment</line>
<line>to a class. Having been so long without proper food, a bath, and a</line>
<line>change of clothing, I did not, of course, make a very favourable</line>
<line>impression upon her, and I could see at once that there were doubts in</line>
<line>her mind about the wisdom of admitting me as a student. I felt that I</line>
<line>could hardly blame her if she got the idea that I was a worthless</line>
<line>loafer or tramp. For some time she did not refuse to admit me, neither</line>
<line>did she decide in my favour, and I continued to linger about her, and</line>
<line>to impress her in all the ways I could with my worthiness. In the</line>
<line>meantime I saw her admitting other students, and that added greatly to</line>
<line>my discomfort, for I felt, deep down in my heart, that I could do as</line>
<line>well as they, if I could only get a chance to show what was in me.</line>

<line>After some hours had passed, the head teacher said to me: “The</line>
<line>adjoining recitation-room needs sweeping. Take the broom and sweep it.”</line>

<line>It occurred to me at once that here was my chance. Never did I receive</line>
<line>an order with more delight. I knew that I could sweep, for Mrs. Ruffner</line>
<line>had thoroughly taught me how to do that when I lived with her.</line>

<line>I swept the recitation-room three times. Then I got a dusting-cloth and</line>
<line>dusted it four times. All the woodwork around the walls, every bench,</line>
<line>table, and desk, I went over four times with my dusting-cloth. Besides,</line>
<line>every piece of furniture had been moved and every closet and corner in</line>
<line>the room had been thoroughly cleaned. I had the feeling that in a large</line>
<line>measure my future depended upon the impression I made upon the teacher</line>
<line>in the cleaning of that room. When I was through, I reported to the</line>
<line>head teacher. She was a “Yankee” woman who knew just where to look for</line>
<line>dirt. She went into the room and inspected the floor and closets; then</line>
<line>she took her handkerchief and rubbed it on the woodwork about the</line>
<line>walls, and over the table and benches. When she was unable to find one</line>
<line>bit of dirt on the floor, or a particle of dust on any of the</line>
<line>furniture, she quietly remarked, “I guess you will do to enter this</line>
<line>institution.”</line>

<line>I was one of the happiest souls on Earth. The sweeping of that room was</line>
<line>my college examination, and never did any youth pass an examination for</line>
<line>entrance into Harvard or Yale that gave him more genuine satisfaction.</line>
<line>I have passed several examinations since then, but I have always felt</line>
<line>that this was the best one I ever passed.</line>

<line>I have spoken of my own experience in entering the Hampton Institute.</line>
<line>Perhaps few, if any, had anything like the same experience that I had,</line>
<line>but about the same period there were hundreds who found their way to</line>
<line>Hampton and other institutions after experiencing something of the same</line>
<line>difficulties that I went through. The young men and women were</line>
<line>determined to secure an education at any cost.</line>

<line>The sweeping of the recitation-room in the manner that I did it seems</line>
<line>to have paved the way for me to get through Hampton. Miss Mary F.</line>
<line>Mackie, the head teacher, offered me a position as janitor. This, of</line>
<line>course, I gladly accepted, because it was a place where I could work</line>
<line>out nearly all the cost of my board. The work was hard and taxing but I</line>
<line>stuck to it. I had a large number of rooms to care for, and had to work</line>
<line>late into the night, while at the same time I had to rise by four</line>
<line>o’clock in the morning, in order to build the fires and have a little</line>
<line>time in which to prepare my lessons. In all my career at Hampton, and</line>
<line>ever since I have been out in the world, Miss Mary F. Mackie, the head</line>
<line>teacher to whom I have referred, proved one of my strongest and most</line>
<line>helpful friends. Her advice and encouragement were always helpful in</line>
<line>strengthening to me in the darkest hour.</line>

<line>I have spoken of the impression that was made upon me by the buildings</line>
<line>and general appearance of the Hampton Institute, but I have not spoken</line>
<line>of that which made the greatest and most lasting impression on me, and</line>
<line>that was a great man—the noblest, rarest human being that it has ever</line>
<line>been my privilege to meet. I refer to the late General Samuel C.</line>
<line>Armstrong.</line>

<line>It has been my fortune to meet personally many of what are called great</line>
<line>characters, both in Europe and America, but I do not hesitate to say</line>
<line>that I never met any man who, in my estimation, was the equal of</line>
<line>General Armstrong. Fresh from the degrading influences of the slave</line>
<line>plantation and the coal-mines, it was a rare privilege for me to be</line>
<line>permitted to come into direct contact with such a character as General</line>
<line>Armstrong. I shall always remember that the first time I went into his</line>
<line>presence he made the impression upon me of being a perfect man: I was</line>
<line>made to feel that there was something about him that was superhuman. It</line>
<line>was my privilege to know the General personally from the time I entered</line>
<line>Hampton till he died, and the more I saw of him the greater he grew in</line>
<line>my estimation. One might have removed from Hampton all the buildings,</line>
<line>class-rooms, teachers, and industries, and given the men and women</line>
<line>there the opportunity of coming into daily contact with General</line>
<line>Armstrong, and that alone would have been a liberal education. The</line>
<line>older I grow, the more I am convinced that there is no education which</line>
<line>one can get from books and costly apparatus that is equal to that which</line>
<line>can be gotten from contact with great men and women. Instead of</line>
<line>studying books so constantly, how I wish that our schools and colleges</line>
<line>might learn to study men and things!</line>

<line>General Armstrong spent two of the last six months of his life in my</line>
<line>home at Tuskegee. At that time he was paralyzed to the extent that he</line>
<line>had lost control of his body and voice in a very large degree.</line>
<line>Notwithstanding his affliction, he worked almost constantly night and</line>
<line>day for the cause to which he had given his life. I never saw a man who</line>
<line>so completely lost sight of himself. I do not believe he ever had a</line>
<line>selfish thought. He was just as happy in trying to assist some other</line>
<line>institution in the South as he was when working for Hampton. Although</line>
<line>he fought the Southern white man in the Civil War, I never heard him</line>
<line>utter a bitter word against him afterward. On the other hand, he was</line>
<line>constantly seeking to find ways by which he could be of service to the</line>
<line>Southern whites.</line>

<line>It would be difficult to describe the hold that he had upon the</line>
<line>students at Hampton, or the faith they had in him. In fact, he was</line>
<line>worshipped by his students. It never occurred to me that General</line>
<line>Armstrong could fail in anything that he undertook. There is almost no</line>
<line>request that he could have made that would not have been complied with.</line>
<line>When he was a guest at my home in Alabama, and was so badly paralyzed</line>
<line>that he had to be wheeled about in an invalid’s chair, I recall that</line>
<line>one of the General’s former students had occasion to push his chair up</line>
<line>a long, steep hill that taxed his strength to the utmost. When the top</line>
<line>of the hill was reached, the former pupil, with a glow of happiness on</line>
<line>his face, exclaimed, “I am so glad that I have been permitted to do</line>
<line>something that was real hard for the General before he dies!” While I</line>
<line>was a student at Hampton, the dormitories became so crowded that it was</line>
<line>impossible to find room for all who wanted to be admitted. In order to</line>
<line>help remedy the difficulty, the General conceived the plan of putting</line>
<line>up tents to be used as rooms. As soon as it became known that General</line>
<line>Armstrong would be pleased if some of the older students would live in</line>
<line>the tents during the winter, nearly every student in school volunteered</line>
<line>to go.</line>

<line>I was one of the volunteers. The winter that we spent in those tents</line>
<line>was an intensely cold one, and we suffered severely—how much I am sure</line>
<line>General Armstrong never knew, because we made no complaints. It was</line>
<line>enough for us to know that we were pleasing General Armstrong, and that</line>
<line>we were making it possible for an additional number of students to</line>
<line>secure an education. More than once, during a cold night, when a stiff</line>
<line>gale would be blowing, our tent was lifted bodily, and we would find</line>
<line>ourselves in the open air. The General would usually pay a visit to the</line>
<line>tents early in the morning, and his earnest, cheerful, encouraging</line>
<line>voice would dispel any feeling of despondency.</line>

<line>I have spoken of my admiration for General Armstrong, and yet he was</line>
<line>but a type of that Christlike body of men and women who went into the</line>
<line>Negro schools at the close of the war by the hundreds to assist in</line>
<line>lifting up my race. The history of the world fails to show a higher,</line>
<line>purer, and more unselfish class of men and women than those who found</line>
<line>their way into those Negro schools.</line>

<line>Life at Hampton was a constant revelation to me; was constantly taking</line>
<line>me into a new world. The matter of having meals at regular hours, of</line>
<line>eating on a tablecloth, using a napkin, the use of the bath-tub and of</line>
<line>the tooth-brush, as well as the use of sheets upon the bed, were all</line>
<line>new to me.</line>

<line>I sometimes feel that almost the most valuable lesson I got at the</line>
<line>Hampton Institute was in the use and value of the bath. I learned there</line>
<line>for the first time some of its value, not only in keeping the body</line>
<line>healthy, but in inspiring self-respect and promoting virtue. In all my</line>
<line>travels in the South and elsewhere since leaving Hampton I have always</line>
<line>in some way sought my daily bath. To get it sometimes when I have been</line>
<line>the guest of my own people in a single-roomed cabin has not always been</line>
<line>easy to do, except by slipping away to some stream in the woods. I have</line>
<line>always tried to teach my people that some provision for bathing should</line>
<line>be a part of every house.</line>

<line>For some time, while a student at Hampton, I possessed but a single</line>
<line>pair of socks, but when I had worn these till they became soiled, I</line>
<line>would wash them at night and hang them by the fire to dry, so that I</line>
<line>might wear them again the next morning.</line>

<line>The charge for my board at Hampton was ten dollars per month. I was</line>
<line>expected to pay a part of this in cash and to work out the remainder.</line>
<line>To meet this cash payment, as I have stated, I had just fifty cents</line>
<line>when I reached the institution. Aside from a very few dollars that my</line>
<line>brother John was able to send me once in a while, I had no money with</line>
<line>which to pay my board. I was determined from the first to make my work</line>
<line>as janitor so valuable that my services would be indispensable. This I</line>
<line>succeeded in doing to such an extent that I was soon informed that I</line>
<line>would be allowed the full cost of my board in return for my work. The</line>
<line>cost of tuition was seventy dollars a year. This, of course, was wholly</line>
<line>beyond my ability to provide. If I had been compelled to pay the</line>
<line>seventy dollars for tuition, in addition to providing for my board, I</line>
<line>would have been compelled to leave the Hampton school. General</line>
<line>Armstrong, however, very kindly got Mr. S. Griffitts Morgan, of New</line>
<line>Bedford, Mass., to defray the cost of my tuition during the whole time</line>
<line>that I was at Hampton. After I finished the course at Hampton and had</line>
<line>entered upon my lifework at Tuskegee, I had the pleasure of visiting</line>
<line>Mr. Morgan several times.</line>

<line>After having been for a while at Hampton, I found myself in difficulty</line>
<line>because I did not have books and clothing. Usually, however, I got</line>
<line>around the trouble about books by borrowing from those who were more</line>
<line>fortunate than myself. As to clothes, when I reached Hampton I had</line>
<line>practically nothing. Everything that I possessed was in a small hand</line>
<line>satchel. My anxiety about clothing was increased because of the fact</line>
<line>that General Armstrong made a personal inspection of the young men in</line>
<line>ranks, to see that their clothes were clean. Shoes had to be polished,</line>
<line>there must be no buttons off the clothing, and no grease-spots. To wear</line>
<line>one suit of clothes continually, while at work and in the schoolroom,</line>
<line>and at the same time keep it clean, was rather a hard problem for me to</line>
<line>solve. In some way I managed to get on till the teachers learned that I</line>
<line>was in earnest and meant to succeed, and then some of them were kind</line>
<line>enough to see that I was partly supplied with second-hand clothing that</line>
<line>had been sent in barrels from the North. These barrels proved a</line>
<line>blessing to hundreds of poor but deserving students. Without them I</line>
<line>question whether I should ever have gotten through Hampton.</line>

<line>When I first went to Hampton I do not recall that I had ever slept in a</line>
<line>bed that had two sheets on it. In those days there were not many</line>
<line>buildings there, and room was very precious. There were seven other</line>
<line>boys in the same room with me; most of them, however, students who had</line>
<line>been there for some time. The sheets were quite a puzzle to me. The</line>
<line>first night I slept under both of them, and the second night I slept on</line>
<line>top of them; but by watching the other boys I learned my lesson in</line>
<line>this, and have been trying to follow it ever since and to teach it to</line>
<line>others.</line>

<line>I was among the youngest of the students who were in Hampton at the</line>
<line>time. Most of the students were men and women—some as old as forty</line>
<line>years of age. As I now recall the scene of my first year, I do not</line>
<line>believe that one often has the opportunity of coming into contact with</line>
<line>three or four hundred men and women who were so tremendously in earnest</line>
<line>as these men and women were. Every hour was occupied in study or work.</line>
<line>Nearly all had had enough actual contact with the world to teach them</line>
<line>the need of education. Many of the older ones were, of course, too old</line>
<line>to master the text-books very thoroughly, and it was often sad to watch</line>
<line>their struggles; but they made up in earnest much of what they lacked</line>
<line>in books. Many of them were as poor as I was, and, besides having to</line>
<line>wrestle with their books, they had to struggle with a poverty which</line>
<line>prevented their having the necessities of life. Many of them had aged</line>
<line>parents who were dependent upon them, and some of them were men who had</line>
<line>wives whose support in some way they had to provide for.</line>

<line>The great and prevailing idea that seemed to take possession of every</line>
<line>one was to prepare himself to lift up the people at his home. No one</line>
<line>seemed to think of himself. And the officers and teachers, what a rare</line>
<line>set of human beings they were! They worked for the students night and</line>
<line>day, in seasons and out of season. They seemed happy only when they</line>
<line>were helping the students in some manner. Whenever it is written—and I</line>
<line>hope it will be—the part that the Yankee teachers played in the</line>
<line>education of the Negroes immediately after the war will make one of the</line>
<line>most thrilling parts of the history off this country. The time is not</line>
<line>far distant when the whole South will appreciate this service in a way</line>
<line>that it has not yet been able to do.</line>




</chapter>

<chapter>
<title>Chapter IV. Helping Others</title>


<line>At the end of my first year at Hampton I was confronted with another</line>
<line>difficulty. Most of the students went home to spend their vacation. I</line>
<line>had no money with which to go home, but I had to go somewhere. In those</line>
<line>days very few students were permitted to remain at the school during</line>
<line>vacation. It made me feel very sad and homesick to see the other</line>
<line>students preparing to leave and starting for home. I not only had no</line>
<line>money with which to go home, but I had none with which to go anywhere.</line>

<line>In some way, however, I had gotten hold of an extra, second-hand coat</line>
<line>which I thought was a pretty valuable coat. This I decided to sell, in</line>
<line>order to get a little money for travelling expenses. I had a good deal</line>
<line>of boyish pride, and I tried to hide, as far as I could, from the other</line>
<line>students the fact that I had no money and nowhere to go. I made it</line>
<line>known to a few people in the town of Hampton that I had this coat to</line>
<line>sell, and, after a good deal of persuading, one coloured man promised</line>
<line>to come to my room to look the coat over and consider the matter of</line>
<line>buying it. This cheered my drooping spirits considerably. Early the</line>
<line>next morning my prospective customer appeared. After looking the</line>
<line>garment over carefully, he asked me how much I wanted for it. I told</line>
<line>him I thought it was worth three dollars. He seemed to agree with me as</line>
<line>to price, but remarked in the most matter-of-fact way: “I tell you what</line>
<line>I will do; I will take the coat, and will pay you five cents, cash</line>
<line>down, and pay you the rest of the money just as soon as I can get it.”</line>
<line>It is not hard to imagine what my feelings were at the time.</line>

<line>With this disappointment I gave up all hope of getting out of the town</line>
<line>of Hampton for my vacation work. I wanted very much to go where I might</line>
<line>secure work that would at least pay me enough to purchase some</line>
<line>much-needed clothing and other necessities. In a few days practically</line>
<line>all the students and teachers had left for their homes, and this served</line>
<line>to depress my spirits even more.</line>

<line>After trying for several days in and near the town of Hampton, I</line>
<line>finally secured work in a restaurant at Fortress Monroe. The wages,</line>
<line>however, were very little more than my board. At night, and between</line>
<line>meals, I found considerable time for study and reading; and in this</line>
<line>direction I improved myself very much during the summer.</line>

<line>When I left school at the end of my first year, I owed the institution</line>
<line>sixteen dollars that I had not been able to work out. It was my</line>
<line>greatest ambition during the summer to save money enough with which to</line>
<line>pay this debt. I felt that this was a debt of honour, and that I could</line>
<line>hardly bring myself to the point of even trying to enter school again</line>
<line>till it was paid. I economized in every way that I could think of—did</line>
<line>my own washing, and went without necessary garments—but still I found</line>
<line>my summer vacation ending and I did not have the sixteen dollars.</line>

<line>One day, during the last week of my stay in the restaurant, I found</line>
<line>under one of the tables a crisp, new ten-dollar bill. I could hardly</line>
<line>contain myself, I was so happy. As it was not my place of business I</line>
<line>felt it to be the proper thing to show the money to the proprietor.</line>
<line>This I did. He seemed as glad as I was, but he coolly explained to me</line>
<line>that, as it was his place of business, he had a right to keep the</line>
<line>money, and he proceeded to do so. This, I confess, was another pretty</line>
<line>hard blow to me. I will not say that I became discouraged, for as I now</line>
<line>look back over my life I do not recall that I ever became discouraged</line>
<line>over anything that I set out to accomplish. I have begun everything</line>
<line>with the idea that I could succeed, and I never had much patience with</line>
<line>the multitudes of people who are always ready to explain why one cannot</line>
<line>succeed. I determined to face the situation just as it was. At the end</line>
<line>of the week I went to the treasurer of the Hampton Institute, General</line>
<line>J.F.B. Marshall, and told him frankly my condition. To my gratification</line>
<line>he told me that I could reenter the institution, and that he would</line>
<line>trust me to pay the debt when I could. During the second year I</line>
<line>continued to work as a janitor.</line>

<line>The education that I received at Hampton out of the text-books was but</line>
<line>a small part of what I learned there. One of the things that impressed</line>
<line>itself upon me deeply, the second year, was the unselfishness of the</line>
<line>teachers. It was hard for me to understand how any individuals could</line>
<line>bring themselves to the point where they could be so happy in working</line>
<line>for others. Before the end of the year, I think I began learning that</line>
<line>those who are happiest are those who do the most for others. This</line>
<line>lesson I have tried to carry with me ever since.</line>

<line>I also learned a valuable lesson at Hampton by coming into contact with</line>
<line>the best breeds of live stock and fowls. No student, I think, who has</line>
<line>had the opportunity of doing this could go out into the world and</line>
<line>content himself with the poorest grades.</line>

<line>Perhaps the most valuable thing that I got out of my second year was an</line>
<line>understanding of the use and value of the Bible. Miss Nathalie Lord,</line>
<line>one of the teachers, from Portland, Me., taught me how to use and love</line>
<line>the Bible. Before this I had never cared a great deal about it, but now</line>
<line>I learned to love to read the Bible, not only for the spiritual help</line>
<line>which it gives, but on account of it as literature. The lessons taught</line>
<line>me in this respect took such a hold upon me that at the present time,</line>
<line>when I am at home, no matter how busy I am, I always make it a rule to</line>
<line>read a chapter or a portion of a chapter in the morning, before</line>
<line>beginning the work of the day.</line>

<line>Whatever ability I may have as a public speaker I owe in a measure to</line>
<line>Miss Lord. When she found out that I had some inclination in this</line>
<line>direction, she gave me private lessons in the matter of breathing,</line>
<line>emphasis, and articulation. Simply to be able to talk in public for the</line>
<line>sake of talking has never had the least attraction to me. In fact, I</line>
<line>consider that there is nothing so empty and unsatisfactory as mere</line>
<line>abstract public speaking; but from my early childhood I have had a</line>
<line>desire to do something to make the world better, and then to be able to</line>
<line>speak to the world about that thing.</line>

<line>The debating societies at Hampton were a constant source of delight to</line>
<line>me. These were held on Saturday evening; and during my whole life at</line>
<line>Hampton I do not recall that I missed a single meeting. I not only</line>
<line>attended the weekly debating society, but was instrumental in</line>
<line>organizing an additional society. I noticed that between the time when</line>
<line>supper was over and the time to begin evening study there were about</line>
<line>twenty minutes which the young men usually spent in idle gossip. About</line>
<line>twenty of us formed a society for the purpose of utilizing this time in</line>
<line>debate or in practice in public speaking. Few persons ever derived more</line>
<line>happiness or benefit from the use of twenty minutes of time than we did</line>
<line>in this way.</line>

<line>At the end of my second year at Hampton, by the help of some money sent</line>
<line>me by my mother and brother John, supplemented by a small gift from one</line>
<line>of the teachers at Hampton, I was enabled to return to my home in</line>
<line>Malden, West Virginia, to spend my vacation. When I reached home I</line>
<line>found that the salt-furnaces were not running, and that the coal-mine</line>
<line>was not being operated on account of the miners being out on “strike.”</line>
<line>This was something which, it seemed, usually occurred whenever the men</line>
<line>got two or three months ahead in their savings. During the strike, of</line>
<line>course, they spent all that they had saved, and would often return to</line>
<line>work in debt at the same wages, or would move to another mine at</line>
<line>considerable expense. In either case, my observations convinced me that</line>
<line>the miners were worse off at the end of the strike. Before the days of</line>
<line>strikes in that section of the country, I knew miners who had</line>
<line>considerable money in the bank, but as soon as the professional labour</line>
<line>agitators got control, the savings of even the more thrifty ones began</line>
<line>disappearing.</line>

<line>My mother and the other members of my family were, of course, much</line>
<line>rejoiced to see me and to note the improvement that I had made during</line>
<line>my two years’ absence. The rejoicing on the part of all classes of the</line>
<line>coloured people, and especially the older ones, over my return, was</line>
<line>almost pathetic. I had to pay a visit to each family and take a meal</line>
<line>with each, and at each place tell the story of my experiences at</line>
<line>Hampton. In addition to this I had to speak before the church and</line>
<line>Sunday-school, and at various other places. The thing that I was most</line>
<line>in search of, though, work, I could not find. There was no work on</line>
<line>account of the strike. I spent nearly the whole of the first month of</line>
<line>my vacation in an effort to find something to do by which I could earn</line>
<line>money to pay my way back to Hampton and save a little money to use</line>
<line>after reaching there.</line>

<line>Toward the end of the first month, I went to a place a considerable</line>
<line>distance from my home, to try to find employment. I did not succeed,</line>
<line>and it was night before I got started on my return. When I had gotten</line>
<line>within a mile or so of my home I was so completely tired out that I</line>
<line>could not walk any farther, and I went into an old, abandoned house to</line>
<line>spend the remainder of the night. About three o’clock in the morning my</line>
<line>brother John found me asleep in this house, and broke to me, as gently</line>
<line>as he could, the sad news that our dear mother had died during the</line>
<line>night.</line>

<line>This seemed to me the saddest and blankest moment in my life. For</line>
<line>several years my mother had not been in good health, but I had no idea,</line>
<line>when I parted from her the previous day, that I should never see her</line>
<line>alive again. Besides that, I had always had an intense desire to be</line>
<line>with her when she did pass away. One of the chief ambitions which</line>
<line>spurred me on at Hampton was that I might be able to get to be in a</line>
<line>position in which I could better make my mother comfortable and happy.</line>
<line>She had so often expressed the wish that she might be permitted to live</line>
<line>to see her children educated and started out in the world.</line>

<line>In a very short time after the death of my mother our little home was</line>
<line>in confusion. My sister Amanda, although she tried to do the best she</line>
<line>could, was too young to know anything about keeping house, and my</line>
<line>stepfather was not able to hire a housekeeper. Sometimes we had food</line>
<line>cooked for us, and sometimes we did not. I remember that more than once</line>
<line>a can of tomatoes and some crackers constituted a meal. Our clothing</line>
<line>went uncared for, and everything about our home was soon in a</line>
<line>tumble-down condition. It seems to me that this was the most dismal</line>
<line>period of my life.</line>

<line>My good friend, Mrs. Ruffner, to whom I have already referred, always</line>
<line>made me welcome at her home, and assisted me in many ways during this</line>
<line>trying period. Before the end of the vacation she gave me some work,</line>
<line>and this, together with work in a coal-mine at some distance from my</line>
<line>home, enabled me to earn a little money.</line>

<line>At one time it looked as if I would have to give up the idea of</line>
<line>returning to Hampton, but my heart was so set on returning that I</line>
<line>determined not to give up going back without a struggle. I was very</line>
<line>anxious to secure some clothes for the winter, but in this I was</line>
<line>disappointed, except for a few garments which my brother John secured</line>
<line>for me. Notwithstanding my need of money and clothing, I was very happy</line>
<line>in the fact that I had secured enough money to pay my travelling</line>
<line>expenses back to Hampton. Once there, I knew that I could make myself</line>
<line>so useful as a janitor that I could in some way get through the school</line>
<line>year.</line>

<line>Three weeks before the time for the opening of the term at Hampton, I</line>
<line>was pleasantly surprised to receive a letter from my good friend Miss</line>
<line>Mary F. Mackie, the lady principal, asking me to return to Hampton two</line>
<line>weeks before the opening of the school, in order that I might assist</line>
<line>her in cleaning the buildings and getting things in order for the new</line>
<line>school year. This was just the opportunity I wanted. It gave me a</line>
<line>chance to secure a credit in the treasurer’s office. I started for</line>
<line>Hampton at once.</line>

<line>During these two weeks I was taught a lesson which I shall never</line>
<line>forget. Miss Mackie was a member of one of the oldest and most cultured</line>
<line>families of the North, and yet for two weeks she worked by my side</line>
<line>cleaning windows, dusting rooms, putting beds in order, and what not.</line>
<line>She felt that things would not be in condition for the opening of</line>
<line>school unless every window-pane was perfectly clean, and she took the</line>
<line>greatest satisfaction in helping to clean them herself. The work which</line>
<line>I have described she did every year that I was at Hampton.</line>

<line>It was hard for me at this time to understand how a woman of her</line>
<line>education and social standing could take such delight in performing</line>
<line>such service, in order to assist in the elevation of an unfortunate</line>
<line>race. Ever since then I have had no patience with any school for my</line>
<line>race in the South which did not teach its students the dignity of</line>
<line>labour.</line>

<line>During my last year at Hampton every minute of my time that was not</line>
<line>occupied with my duties as janitor was devoted to hard study. I was</line>
<line>determined, if possible, to make such a record in my class as would</line>
<line>cause me to be placed on the “honour roll” of Commencement speakers.</line>
<line>This I was successful in doing. It was June of 1875 when I finished the</line>
<line>regular course of study at Hampton. The greatest benefits that I got</line>
<line>out of my my life at the Hampton Institute, perhaps, may be classified</line>
<line>under two heads:—</line>

<line>First was contact with a great man, General S.C. Armstrong, who, I</line>
<line>repeat, was, in my opinion, the rarest, strongest, and most beautiful</line>
<line>character that it has ever been my privilege to meet.</line>

<line>Second, at Hampton, for the first time, I learned what education was</line>
<line>expected to do for an individual. Before going there I had a good deal</line>
<line>of the then rather prevalent idea among our people that to secure an</line>
<line>education meant to have a good, easy time, free from all necessity for</line>
<line>manual labour. At Hampton I not only learned that it was not a disgrace</line>
<line>to labour, but learned to love labour, not alone for its financial</line>
<line>value, but for labour’s own sake and for the independence and</line>
<line>self-reliance which the ability to do something which the world wants</line>
<line>done brings. At that institution I got my first taste of what it meant</line>
<line>to live a life of unselfishness, my first knowledge of the fact that</line>
<line>the happiest individuals are those who do the most to make others</line>
<line>useful and happy.</line>

<line>I was completely out of money when I graduated. In company with other</line>
<line>Hampton students, I secured a place as a table waiter in a summer hotel</line>
<line>in Connecticut, and managed to borrow enough money with which to get</line>
<line>there. I had not been in this hotel long before I found out that I knew</line>
<line>practically nothing about waiting on a hotel table. The head waiter,</line>
<line>however, supposed that I was an accomplished waiter. He soon gave me</line>
<line>charge of the table at which there sat four or five wealthy and rather</line>
<line>aristocratic people. My ignorance of how to wait upon them was so</line>
<line>apparent that they scolded me in such a severe manner that I became</line>
<line>frightened and left their table, leaving them sitting there without</line>
<line>food. As a result of this I was reduced from the position of waiter to</line>
<line>that of a dish-carrier.</line>

<line>But I determined to learn the business of waiting, and did so within a</line>
<line>few weeks and was restored to my former position. I have had the</line>
<line>satisfaction of being a guest in this hotel several times since I was a</line>
<line>waiter there.</line>

<line>At the close of the hotel season I returned to my former home in</line>
<line>Malden, and was elected to teach the coloured school at that place.</line>
<line>This was the beginning of one of the happiest periods of my life. I now</line>
<line>felt that I had the opportunity to help the people of my home town to a</line>
<line>higher life. I felt from the first that mere book education was not all</line>
<line>that the young people of that town needed. I began my work at eight</line>
<line>o’clock in the morning, and, as a rule, it did not end until ten</line>
<line>o’clock at night. In addition to the usual routine of teaching, I</line>
<line>taught the pupils to comb their hair, and to keep their hands and faces</line>
<line>clean, as well as their clothing. I gave special attention to teaching</line>
<line>them the proper use of the tooth-brush and the bath. In all my teaching</line>
<line>I have watched carefully the influence of the tooth-brush, and I am</line>
<line>convinced that there are few single agencies of civilization that are</line>
<line>more far-reaching.</line>

<line>There were so many of the older boys and girls in the town, as well as</line>
<line>men and women, who had to work in the daytime and still were craving an</line>
<line>opportunity for an education, that I soon opened a night-school. From</line>
<line>the first, this was crowded every night, being about as large as the</line>
<line>school that I taught in the day. The efforts of some of the men and</line>
<line>women, who in many cases were over fifty years of age, to learn, were</line>
<line>in some cases very pathetic.</line>

<line>My day and night school work was not all that I undertook. I</line>
<line>established a small reading-room and a debating society. On Sundays I</line>
<line>taught two Sunday-schools, one in the town of Malden in the afternoon,</line>
<line>and the other in the morning at a place three miles distant from</line>
<line>Malden. In addition to this, I gave private lessons to several young</line>
<line>men whom I was fitting to send to the Hampton Institute. Without regard</line>
<line>to pay and with little thought of it, I taught any one who wanted to</line>
<line>learn anything that I could teach him. I was supremely happy in the</line>
<line>opportunity of being able to assist somebody else. I did receive,</line>
<line>however, a small salary from the public fund, for my work as a</line>
<line>public-school teacher.</line>

<line>During the time that I was a student at Hampton my older brother, John,</line>
<line>not only assisted me all that he could, but worked all of the time in</line>
<line>the coal-mines in order to support the family. He willingly neglected</line>
<line>his own education that he might help me. It was my earnest wish to help</line>
<line>him to prepare to enter Hampton, and to save money to assist him in his</line>
<line>expenses there. Both of these objects I was successful in</line>
<line>accomplishing. In three years my brother finished the course at</line>
<line>Hampton, and he is now holding the important position of Superintendent</line>
<line>of Industries at Tuskegee. When he returned from Hampton, we both</line>
<line>combined our efforts and savings to send our adopted brother, James,</line>
<line>through the Hampton Institute. This we succeeded in doing, and he is</line>
<line>now the postmaster at the Tuskegee Institute. The year 1877, which was</line>
<line>my second year of teaching in Malden, I spent very much as I did the</line>
<line>first.</line>

<line>It was while my home was at Malden that what was known as the “Ku Klux</line>
<line>Klan” was in the height of its activity. The “Ku Klux” were bands of</line>
<line>men who had joined themselves together for the purpose of regulating</line>
<line>the conduct of the coloured people, especially with the object of</line>
<line>preventing the members of the race from exercising any influence in</line>
<line>politics. They corresponded somewhat to the “patrollers” of whom I used</line>
<line>to hear a great deal during the days of slavery, when I was a small</line>
<line>boy. The “patrollers” were bands of white men—usually young men—who</line>
<line>were organized largely for the purpose of regulating the conduct of the</line>
<line>slaves at night in such matters as preventing the slaves from going</line>
<line>from one plantation to another without passes, and for preventing them</line>
<line>from holding any kind of meetings without permission and without the</line>
<line>presence at these meetings of at least one white man.</line>

<line>Like the “patrollers” the “Ku Klux” operated almost wholly at night.</line>
<line>They were, however, more cruel than the “patrollers.” Their objects, in</line>
<line>the main, were to crush out the political aspirations of the Negroes,</line>
<line>but they did not confine themselves to this, because schoolhouses as</line>
<line>well as churches were burned by them, and many innocent persons were</line>
<line>made to suffer. During this period not a few coloured people lost their</line>
<line>lives.</line>

<line>As a young man, the acts of these lawless bands made a great impression</line>
<line>upon me. I saw one open battle take place at Malden between some of the</line>
<line>coloured and white people. There must have been not far from a hundred</line>
<line>persons engaged on each side; many on both sides were seriously</line>
<line>injured, among them General Lewis Ruffner, the husband of my friend</line>
<line>Mrs. Viola Ruffner. General Ruffner tried to defend the coloured</line>
<line>people, and for this he was knocked down and so seriously wounded that</line>
<line>he never completely recovered. It seemed to me as I watched this</line>
<line>struggle between members of the two races, that there was no hope for</line>
<line>our people in this country. The “Ku Klux” period was, I think, the</line>
<line>darkest part of the Reconstruction days.</line>

<line>I have referred to this unpleasant part of the history of the South</line>
<line>simply for the purpose of calling attention to the great change that</line>
<line>has taken place since the days of the “Ku Klux.” To-day there are no</line>
<line>such organizations in the South, and the fact that such ever existed is</line>
<line>almost forgotten by both races. There are few places in the South now</line>
<line>where public sentiment would permit such organizations to exist.</line>




</chapter>

<chapter>
<title>Chapter V. The Reconstruction Period</title>


<line>The years from 1867 to 1878 I think may be called the period of</line>
<line>Reconstruction. This included the time that I spent as a student at</line>
<line>Hampton and as a teacher in West Virginia. During the whole of the</line>
<line>Reconstruction period two ideas were constantly agitating in the minds</line>
<line>of the coloured people, or, at least, in the minds of a large part of</line>
<line>the race. One of these was the craze for Greek and Latin learning, and</line>
<line>the other was a desire to hold office.</line>

<line>It could not have been expected that a people who had spent generations</line>
<line>in slavery, and before that generations in the darkest heathenism,</line>
<line>could at first form any proper conception of what an education meant.</line>
<line>In every part of the South, during the Reconstruction period, schools,</line>
<line>both day and night, were filled to overflowing with people of all ages</line>
<line>and conditions, some being as far along in age as sixty and seventy</line>
<line>years. The ambition to secure an education was most praiseworthy and</line>
<line>encouraging. The idea, however, was too prevalent that, as soon as one</line>
<line>secured a little education, in some unexplainable way he would be free</line>
<line>from most of the hardships of the world, and, at any rate, could live</line>
<line>without manual labour. There was a further feeling that a knowledge,</line>
<line>however little, of the Greek and Latin languages would make one a very</line>
<line>superior human being, something bordering almost on the supernatural. I</line>
<line>remember that the first coloured man whom I saw who knew something</line>
<line>about foreign languages impressed me at the time as being a man of all</line>
<line>others to be envied.</line>

<line>Naturally, most of our people who received some little education became</line>
<line>teachers or preachers. While among those two classes there were many</line>
<line>capable, earnest, godly men and women, still a large proportion took up</line>
<line>teaching or preaching as an easy way to make a living. Many became</line>
<line>teachers who could do little more than write their names. I remember</line>
<line>there came into our neighbourhood one of this class, who was in search</line>
<line>of a school to teach, and the question arose while he was there as to</line>
<line>the shape of the earth and how he could teach the children concerning</line>
<line>the subject. He explained his position in the matter by saying that he</line>
<line>was prepared to teach that the earth was either flat or round,</line>
<line>according to the preference of a majority of his patrons.</line>

<line>The ministry was the profession that suffered most—and still suffers,</line>
<line>though there has been great improvement—on account of not only ignorant</line>
<line>but in many cases immoral men who claimed that they were “called to</line>
<line>preach.” In the earlier days of freedom almost every coloured man who</line>
<line>learned to read would receive “a call to preach” within a few days</line>
<line>after he began reading. At my home in West Virginia the process of</line>
<line>being called to the ministry was a very interesting one. Usually the</line>
<line>“call” came when the individual was sitting in church. Without warning</line>
<line>the one called would fall upon the floor as if struck by a bullet, and</line>
<line>would lie there for hours, speechless and motionless. Then the news</line>
<line>would spread all through the neighborhood that this individual had</line>
<line>received a “call.” If he were inclined to resist the summons, he would</line>
<line>fall or be made to fall a second or third time. In the end he always</line>
<line>yielded to the call. While I wanted an education badly, I confess that</line>
<line>in my youth I had a fear that when I had learned to read and write very</line>
<line>well I would receive one of these “calls”; but, for some reason, my</line>
<line>call never came.</line>

<line>When we add the number of wholly ignorant men who preached or</line>
<line>“exhorted” to that of those who possessed something of an education, it</line>
<line>can be seen at a glance that the supply of ministers was large. In</line>
<line>fact, some time ago I knew a certain church that had a total membership</line>
<line>of about two hundred, and eighteen of that number were ministers. But,</line>
<line>I repeat, in many communities in the South the character of the</line>
<line>ministry is being improved, and I believe that within the next two or</line>
<line>three decades a very large proportion of the unworthy ones will have</line>
<line>disappeared. The “calls” to preach, I am glad to say, are not nearly so</line>
<line>numerous now as they were formerly, and the calls to some industrial</line>
<line>occupation are growing more numerous. The improvement that has taken</line>
<line>place in the character of the teachers is even more marked than in the</line>
<line>case of the ministers.</line>

<line>During the whole of the Reconstruction period our people throughout the</line>
<line>South looked to the Federal Government for everything, very much as a</line>
<line>child looks to its mother. This was not unnatural. The central</line>
<line>government gave them freedom, and the whole Nation had been enriched</line>
<line>for more than two centuries by the labour of the Negro. Even as a</line>
<line>youth, and later in manhood, I had the feeling that it was cruelly</line>
<line>wrong in the central government, at the beginning of our freedom, to</line>
<line>fail to make some provision for the general education of our people in</line>
<line>addition to what the states might do, so that the people would be the</line>
<line>better prepared for the duties of citizenship.</line>

<line>It is easy to find fault, to remark what might have been done, and</line>
<line>perhaps, after all, and under all the circumstances, those in charge of</line>
<line>the conduct of affairs did the only thing that could be done at the</line>
<line>time. Still, as I look back now over the entire period of our freedom,</line>
<line>I cannot help feeling that it would have been wiser if some plan could</line>
<line>have been put in operation which would have made the possession of a</line>
<line>certain amount of education or property, or both, a test for the</line>
<line>exercise of the franchise, and a way provided by which this test should</line>
<line>be made to apply honestly and squarely to both the white and black</line>
<line>races.</line>

<line>Though I was but little more than a youth during the period of</line>
<line>Reconstruction, I had the feeling that mistakes were being made, and</line>
<line>that things could not remain in the condition that they were in then</line>
<line>very long. I felt that the Reconstruction policy, so far as it related</line>
<line>to my race, was in a large measure on a false foundation, was</line>
<line>artificial and forced. In many cases it seemed to me that the ignorance</line>
<line>of my race was being used as a tool with which to help white men into</line>
<line>office, and that there was an element in the North which wanted to</line>
<line>punish the Southern white men by forcing the Negro into positions over</line>
<line>the heads of the Southern whites. I felt that the Negro would be the</line>
<line>one to suffer for this in the end. Besides, the general political</line>
<line>agitation drew the attention of our people away from the more</line>
<line>fundamental matters of perfecting themselves in the industries at their</line>
<line>doors and in securing property.</line>

<line>The temptations to enter political life were so alluring that I came</line>
<line>very near yielding to them at one time, but I was kept from doing so by</line>
<line>the feeling that I would be helping in a more substantial way by</line>
<line>assisting in the laying of the foundation of the race through a</line>
<line>generous education of the hand, head, and heart. I saw coloured men who</line>
<line>were members of the state legislatures, and county officers, who, in</line>
<line>some cases, could not read or write, and whose morals were as weak as</line>
<line>their education. Not long ago, when passing through the streets of a</line>
<line>certain city in the South, I heard some brick-masons calling out, from</line>
<line>the top of a two-story brick building on which they were working, for</line>
<line>the “Governor” to “hurry up and bring up some more bricks.” Several</line>
<line>times I heard the command, “Hurry up, Governor!” “Hurry up, Governor!”</line>
<line>My curiosity was aroused to such an extent that I made inquiry as to</line>
<line>who the “Governor” was, and soon found that he was a coloured man who</line>
<line>at one time had held the position of Lieutenant-Governor of his state.</line>

<line>But not all the coloured people who were in office during</line>
<line>Reconstruction were unworthy of their positions, by any means. Some of</line>
<line>them, like the late Senator B.K. Bruce, Governor Pinchback, and many</line>
<line>others, were strong, upright, useful men. Neither were all the class</line>
<line>designated as carpetbaggers dishonourable men. Some of them, like</line>
<line>ex-Governor Bullock, of Georgia, were men of high character and</line>
<line>usefulness.</line>

<line>Of course the coloured people, so largely without education, and wholly</line>
<line>without experience in government, made tremendous mistakes, just as</line>
<line>many people similarly situated would have done. Many of the Southern</line>
<line>whites have a feeling that, if the Negro is permitted to exercise his</line>
<line>political rights now to any degree, the mistakes of the Reconstruction</line>
<line>period will repeat themselves. I do not think this would be true,</line>
<line>because the Negro is a much stronger and wiser man than he was</line>
<line>thirty-five years ago, and he is fast learning the lesson that he</line>
<line>cannot afford to act in a manner that will alienate his Southern white</line>
<line>neighbours from him. More and more I am convinced that the final</line>
<line>solution of the political end of our race problem will be for each</line>
<line>state that finds it necessary to change the law bearing upon the</line>
<line>franchise to make the law apply with absolute honesty, and without</line>
<line>opportunity for double dealing or evasion, to both races alike. Any</line>
<line>other course my daily observation in the South convinces me, will be</line>
<line>unjust to the Negro, unjust to the white man, and unfair to the rest of</line>
<line>the state in the Union, and will be, like slavery, a sin that at some</line>
<line>time we shall have to pay for.</line>

<line>In the fall of 1878, after having taught school in Malden for two</line>
<line>years, and after I had succeeded in preparing several of the young men</line>
<line>and women, besides my two brothers, to enter the Hampton Institute, I</line>
<line>decided to spend some months in study at Washington, D.C. I remained</line>
<line>there for eight months. I derived a great deal of benefit from the</line>
<line>studies which I pursued, and I came into contact with some strong men</line>
<line>and women. At the institution I attended there was no industrial</line>
<line>training given to the students, and I had an opportunity of comparing</line>
<line>the influence of an institution with no industrial training with that</line>
<line>of one like the Hampton Institute, that emphasizes the industries. At</line>
<line>this school I found the students, in most cases, had more money, were</line>
<line>better dressed, wore the latest style of all manner of clothing, and in</line>
<line>some cases were more brilliant mentally. At Hampton it was a standing</line>
<line>rule that, while the institution would be responsible for securing some</line>
<line>one to pay the tuition for the students, the men and women themselves</line>
<line>must provide for their own board, books, clothing, and room wholly by</line>
<line>work, or partly by work and partly in cash. At the institution at which</line>
<line>I now was, I found that a large portion of the students by some means</line>
<line>had their personal expenses paid for them. At Hampton the student was</line>
<line>constantly making the effort through the industries to help himself,</line>
<line>and that very effort was of immense value in character-building. The</line>
<line>students at the other school seemed to be less self-dependent. They</line>
<line>seemed to give more attention to mere outward appearances. In a word,</line>
<line>they did not appear to me to be beginning at the bottom, on a real,</line>
<line>solid foundation, to the extent that they were at Hampton. They knew</line>
<line>more about Latin and Greek when they left school, but they seemed to</line>
<line>know less about life and its conditions as they would meet it at their</line>
<line>homes. Having lived for a number of years in the midst of comfortable</line>
<line>surroundings, they were not as much inclined as the Hampton students to</line>
<line>go into the country districts of the South, where there was little of</line>
<line>comfort, to take up work for our people, and they were more inclined to</line>
<line>yield to the temptation to become hotel waiters and Pullman-car porters</line>
<line>as their life-work.</line>

<line>During the time I was a student at Washington the city was crowded with</line>
<line>coloured people, many of whom had recently come from the South. A large</line>
<line>proportion of these people had been drawn to Washington because they</line>
<line>felt that they could lead a life of ease there. Others had secured</line>
<line>minor government positions, and still another large class was there in</line>
<line>the hope of securing Federal positions. A number of coloured men—some</line>
<line>of them very strong and brilliant—were in the House of Representatives</line>
<line>at that time, and one, the Hon. B.K. Bruce, was in the Senate. All this</line>
<line>tended to make Washington an attractive place for members of the</line>
<line>coloured race. Then, too, they knew that at all times they could have</line>
<line>the protection of the law in the District of Columbia. The public</line>
<line>schools in Washington for coloured people were better then than they</line>
<line>were elsewhere. I took great interest in studying the life of our</line>
<line>people there closely at that time. I found that while among them there</line>
<line>was a large element of substantial, worthy citizens, there was also a</line>
<line>superficiality about the life of a large class that greatly alarmed me.</line>
<line>I saw young coloured men who were not earning more than four dollars a</line>
<line>week spend two dollars or more for a buggy on Sunday to ride up and</line>
<line>down Pennsylvania Avenue in, in order that they might try to convince</line>
<line>the world that they were worth thousands. I saw other young men who</line>
<line>received seventy-five or one hundred dollars per month from the</line>
<line>Government, who were in debt at the end of every month. I saw men who</line>
<line>but a few months previous were members of Congress, then without</line>
<line>employment and in poverty. Among a large class there seemed to be a</line>
<line>dependence upon the Government for every conceivable thing. The members</line>
<line>of this class had little ambition to create a position for themselves,</line>
<line>but wanted the Federal officials to create one for them. How many times</line>
<line>I wished then, and have often wished since, that by some power of magic</line>
<line>I might remove the great bulk of these people into the county districts</line>
<line>and plant them upon the soil, upon the solid and never deceptive</line>
<line>foundation of Mother Nature, where all nations and races that have ever</line>
<line>succeeded have gotten their start,—a start that at first may be slow</line>
<line>and toilsome, but one that nevertheless is real.</line>

<line>In Washington I saw girls whose mothers were earning their living by</line>
<line>laundrying. These girls were taught by their mothers, in rather a crude</line>
<line>way it is true, the industry of laundrying. Later, these girls entered</line>
<line>the public schools and remained there perhaps six or eight years. When</line>
<line>the public school course was finally finished, they wanted more costly</line>
<line>dresses, more costly hats and shoes. In a word, while their wants have</line>
<line>been increased, their ability to supply their wants had not been</line>
<line>increased in the same degree. On the other hand, their six or eight</line>
<line>years of book education had weaned them away from the occupation of</line>
<line>their mothers. The result of this was in too many cases that the girls</line>
<line>went to the bad. I often thought how much wiser it would have been to</line>
<line>give these girls the same amount of maternal training—and I favour any</line>
<line>kind of training, whether in the languages or mathematics, that gives</line>
<line>strength and culture to the mind—but at the same time to give them the</line>
<line>most thorough training in the latest and best methods of laundrying and</line>
<line>other kindred occupations.</line>




</chapter>

<chapter>
<title>Chapter VI. Black Race And Red Race</title>


<line>During the year that I spent in Washington, and for some little time</line>
<line>before this, there had been considerable agitation in the state of West</line>
<line>Virginia over the question of moving the capital of the state from</line>
<line>Wheeling to some other central point. As a result of this, the</line>
<line>Legislature designated three cities to be voted upon by the citizens of</line>
<line>the state as the permanent seat of government. Among these cities was</line>
<line>Charleston, only five miles from Malden, my home. At the close of my</line>
<line>school year in Washington I was very pleasantly surprised to receive,</line>
<line>from a committee of three white people in Charleston, an invitation to</line>
<line>canvass the state in the interests of that city. This invitation I</line>
<line>accepted, and spent nearly three months in speaking in various parts of</line>
<line>the state. Charleston was successful in winning the prize, and is now</line>
<line>the permanent seat of government.</line>

<line>The reputation that I made as a speaker during this campaign induced a</line>
<line>number of persons to make an earnest effort to get me to enter</line>
<line>political life, but I refused, still believing that I could find other</line>
<line>service which would prove of more permanent value to my race. Even then</line>
<line>I had a strong feeling that what our people most needed was to get a</line>
<line>foundation in education, industry, and property, and for this I felt</line>
<line>that they could better afford to strive than for political preferment.</line>
<line>As for my individual self, it appeared to me to be reasonably certain</line>
<line>that I could succeed in political life, but I had a feeling that it</line>
<line>would be a rather selfish kind of success—individual success at the</line>
<line>cost of failing to do my duty in assisting in laying a foundation for</line>
<line>the masses.</line>

<line>At this period in the progress of our race a very large proportion of</line>
<line>the young men who went to school or to college did so with the</line>
<line>expressed determination to prepare themselves to be great lawyers, or</line>
<line>Congressmen, and many of the women planned to become music teachers;</line>
<line>but I had a reasonably fixed idea, even at that early period in my</line>
<line>life, that there was a need for something to be done to prepare the way</line>
<line>for successful lawyers, Congressmen, and music teachers.</line>

<line>I felt that the conditions were a good deal like those of an old</line>
<line>coloured man, during the days of slavery, who wanted to learn how to</line>
<line>play on the guitar. In his desire to take guitar lessons he applied to</line>
<line>one of his young masters to teach him, but the young man, not having</line>
<line>much faith in the ability of the slave to master the guitar at his age,</line>
<line>sought to discourage him by telling him: “Uncle Jake, I will give you</line>
<line>guitar lessons; but, Jake, I will have to charge you three dollars for</line>
