<line>the first lesson, two dollars for the second lesson, and one dollar for</line>
<line>the third lesson. But I will charge you only twenty-five cents for the</line>
<line>last lesson.”</line>

<line>Uncle Jake answered: “All right, boss, I hires you on dem terms. But,</line>
<line>boss! I wants yer to be sure an’ give me dat las’ lesson first.”</line>

<line>Soon after my work in connection with the removal of the capital was</line>
<line>finished, I received an invitation which gave me great joy and which at</line>
<line>the same time was a very pleasant surprise. This was a letter from</line>
<line>General Armstrong, inviting me to return to Hampton at the next</line>
<line>Commencement to deliver what was called the “post-graduate address.”</line>
<line>This was an honour which I had not dreamed of receiving. With much care</line>
<line>I prepared the best address that I was capable of. I chose for my</line>
<line>subject “The Force That Wins.”</line>

<line>As I returned to Hampton for the purpose of delivering this address, I</line>
<line>went over much of the same ground—now, however, covered entirely by</line>
<line>railroad—that I had traversed nearly six years before, when I first</line>
<line>sought entrance into Hampton Institute as a student. Now I was able to</line>
<line>ride the whole distance in the train. I was constantly contrasting this</line>
<line>with my first journey to Hampton. I think I may say, without seeming</line>
<line>egotism, that it is seldom that five years have wrought such a change</line>
<line>in the life and aspirations of an individual.</line>

<line>At Hampton I received a warm welcome from teachers and students. I</line>
<line>found that during my absence from Hampton the institute each year had</line>
<line>been getting closer to the real needs and conditions of our people;</line>
<line>that the industrial teaching, as well as that of the academic</line>
<line>department, had greatly improved. The plan of the school was not</line>
<line>modelled after that of any other institution then in existence, but</line>
<line>every improvement was made under the magnificent leadership of General</line>
<line>Armstrong solely with the view of meeting and helping the needs of our</line>
<line>people as they presented themselves at the time. Too often, it seems to</line>
<line>me, in missionary and educational work among underdeveloped races,</line>
<line>people yield to the temptation of doing that which was done a hundred</line>
<line>years before, or is being done in other communities a thousand miles</line>
<line>away. The temptation often is to run each individual through a certain</line>
<line>educational mould, regardless of the condition of the subject or the</line>
<line>end to be accomplished. This was not so at Hampton Institute.</line>

<line>The address which I delivered on Commencement Day seems to have pleased</line>
<line>every one, and many kind and encouraging words were spoken to me</line>
<line>regarding it. Soon after my return to my home in West Virginia, where I</line>
<line>had planned to continue teaching, I was again surprised to receive a</line>
<line>letter from General Armstrong, asking me to return to Hampton partly as</line>
<line>a teacher and partly to pursue some supplementary studies. This was in</line>
<line>the summer of 1879. Soon after I began my first teaching in West</line>
<line>Virginia I had picked out four of the brightest and most promising of</line>
<line>my pupils, in addition to my two brothers, to whom I have already</line>
<line>referred, and had given them special attention, with the view of having</line>
<line>them go to Hampton. They had gone there, and in each case the teachers</line>
<line>had found them so well prepared that they entered advanced classes.</line>
<line>This fact, it seems, led to my being called back to Hampton as a</line>
<line>teacher. One of the young men that I sent to Hampton in this way is now</line>
<line>Dr. Samuel E. Courtney, a successful physician in Boston, and a member</line>
<line>of the School Board of that city.</line>

<line>About this time the experiment was being tried for the first time, by</line>
<line>General Armstrong, of educating Indians at Hampton. Few people then had</line>
<line>any confidence in the ability of the Indians to receive education and</line>
<line>to profit by it. General Armstrong was anxious to try the experiment</line>
<line>systematically on a large scale. He secured from the reservations in</line>
<line>the Western states over one hundred wild and for the most part</line>
<line>perfectly ignorant Indians, the greater proportion of whom were young</line>
<line>men. The special work which the General desired me to do was to be a</line>
<line>sort of “house father” to the Indian young men—that is, I was to live</line>
<line>in the building with them and have the charge of their discipline,</line>
<line>clothing, rooms, and so on. This was a very tempting offer, but I had</line>
<line>become so much absorbed in my work in West Virginia that I dreaded to</line>
<line>give it up. However, I tore myself away from it. I did not know how to</line>
<line>refuse to perform any service that General Armstrong desired of me.</line>

<line>On going to Hampton, I took up my residence in a building with about</line>
<line>seventy-five Indian youths. I was the only person in the building who</line>
<line>was not a member of their race. At first I had a good deal of doubt</line>
<line>about my ability to succeed. I knew that the average Indian felt</line>
<line>himself above the white man, and, of course, he felt himself far above</line>
<line>the Negro, largely on account of the fact of the Negro having submitted</line>
<line>to slavery—a thing which the Indian would never do. The Indians, in the</line>
<line>Indian Territory, owned a large number of slaves during the days of</line>
<line>slavery. Aside from this, there was a general feeling that the attempt</line>
<line>to educate and civilize the red men at Hampton would be a failure. All</line>
<line>this made me proceed very cautiously, for I felt keenly the great</line>
<line>responsibility. But I was determined to succeed. It was not long before</line>
<line>I had the complete confidence of the Indians, and not only this, but I</line>
<line>think I am safe in saying that I had their love and respect. I found</line>
<line>that they were about like any other human beings; that they responded</line>
<line>to kind treatment and resented ill-treatment. They were continually</line>
<line>planning to do something that would add to my happiness and comfort.</line>
<line>The things that they disliked most, I think, were to have their long</line>
<line>hair cut, to give up wearing their blankets, and to cease smoking; but</line>
<line>no white American ever thinks that any other race is wholly civilized</line>
<line>until he wears the white man’s clothes, eats the white man’s food,</line>
<line>speaks the white man’s language, and professes the white man’s</line>
<line>religion.</line>

<line>When the difficulty of learning the English language was subtracted, I</line>
<line>found that in the matter of learning trades and in mastering academic</line>
<line>studies there was little difference between the coloured and Indian</line>
<line>students. It was a constant delight to me to note the interest which</line>
<line>the coloured students took in trying to help the Indians in every way</line>
<line>possible. There were a few of the coloured students who felt that the</line>
<line>Indians ought not to be admitted to Hampton, but these were in the</line>
<line>minority. Whenever they were asked to do so, the Negro students gladly</line>
<line>took the Indians as room-mates, in order that they might teach them to</line>
<line>speak English and to acquire civilized habits.</line>

<line>I have often wondered if there was a white institution in this country</line>
<line>whose students would have welcomed the incoming of more than a hundred</line>
<line>companions of another race in the cordial way that these black students</line>
<line>at Hampton welcomed the red ones. How often I have wanted to say to</line>
<line>white students that they lift themselves up in proportion as they help</line>
<line>to lift others, and the more unfortunate the race, and the lower in the</line>
<line>scale of civilization, the more does one raise one’s self by giving the</line>
<line>assistance.</line>

<line>This reminds me of a conversation which I once had with the Hon.</line>
<line>Frederick Douglass. At one time Mr. Douglass was travelling in the</line>
<line>state of Pennsylvania, and was forced, on account of his colour, to</line>
<line>ride in the baggage-car, in spite of the fact that he had paid the same</line>
<line>price for his passage that the other passengers had paid. When some of</line>
<line>the white passengers went into the baggage-car to console Mr. Douglass,</line>
<line>and one of them said to him: “I am sorry, Mr. Douglass, that you have</line>
<line>been degraded in this manner,” Mr. Douglass straightened himself up on</line>
<line>the box upon which he was sitting, and replied: “They cannot degrade</line>
<line>Frederick Douglass. The soul that is within me no man can degrade. I am</line>
<line>not the one that is being degraded on account of this treatment, but</line>
<line>those who are inflicting it upon me.”</line>

<line>In one part of the country, where the law demands the separation of the</line>
<line>races on the railroad trains, I saw at one time a rather amusing</line>
<line>instance which showed how difficult it sometimes is to know where the</line>
<line>black begins and the white ends.</line>

<line>There was a man who was well known in his community as a Negro, but who</line>
<line>was so white that even an expert would have hard work to classify him</line>
<line>as a black man. This man was riding in the part of the train set aside</line>
<line>for the coloured passengers. When the train conductor reached him, he</line>
<line>showed at once that he was perplexed. If the man was a Negro, the</line>
<line>conductor did not want to send him to the white people’s coach; at the</line>
<line>same time, if he was a white man, the conductor did not want to insult</line>
<line>him by asking him if he was a Negro. The official looked him over</line>
<line>carefully, examining his hair, eyes, nose, and hands, but still seemed</line>
<line>puzzled. Finally, to solve the difficulty, he stooped over and peeped</line>
<line>at the man’s feet. When I saw the conductor examining the feet of the</line>
<line>man in question, I said to myself, “That will settle it;” and so it</line>
<line>did, for the trainman promptly decided that the passenger was a Negro,</line>
<line>and let him remain where he was. I congratulated myself that my race</line>
<line>was fortunate in not losing one of its members.</line>

<line>My experience has been that the time to test a true gentleman is to</line>
<line>observe him when he is in contact with individuals of a race that is</line>
<line>less fortunate than his own. This is illustrated in no better way than</line>
<line>by observing the conduct of the old-school type of Southern gentleman</line>
<line>when he is in contact with his former slaves or their descendants.</line>

<line>An example of what I mean is shown in a story told of George</line>
<line>Washington, who, meeting a coloured man in the road once, who politely</line>
<line>lifted his hat, lifted his own in return. Some of his white friends who</line>
<line>saw the incident criticised Washington for his action. In reply to</line>
<line>their criticism George Washington said: “Do you suppose that I am going</line>
<line>to permit a poor, ignorant, coloured man to be more polite than I am?”</line>

<line>While I was in charge of the Indian boys at Hampton, I had one or two</line>
<line>experiences which illustrate the curious workings of caste in America.</line>
<line>One of the Indian boys was taken ill, and it became my duty to take him</line>
<line>to Washington, deliver him over to the Secretary of the Interior, and</line>
<line>get a receipt for him, in order that he might be returned to his</line>
<line>Western reservation. At that time I was rather ignorant of the ways of</line>
<line>the world. During my journey to Washington, on a steamboat, when the</line>
<line>bell rang for dinner, I was careful to wait and not enter the dining</line>
<line>room until after the greater part of the passengers had finished their</line>
<line>meal. Then, with my charge, I went to the dining saloon. The man in</line>
<line>charge politely informed me that the Indian could be served, but that I</line>
<line>could not. I never could understand how he knew just where to draw the</line>
<line>colour line, since the Indian and I were of about the same complexion.</line>
<line>The steward, however, seemed to be an expert in this manner. I had been</line>
<line>directed by the authorities at Hampton to stop at a certain hotel in</line>
<line>Washington with my charge, but when I went to this hotel the clerk</line>
<line>stated that he would be glad to receive the Indian into the house, but</line>
<line>said that he could not accommodate me.</line>

<line>An illustration of something of this same feeling came under my</line>
<line>observation afterward. I happened to find myself in a town in which so</line>
<line>much excitement and indignation were being expressed that it seemed</line>
<line>likely for a time that there would be a lynching. The occasion of the</line>
<line>trouble was that a dark-skinned man had stopped at the local hotel.</line>
<line>Investigation, however, developed the fact that this individual was a</line>
<line>citizen of Morocco, and that while travelling in this country he spoke</line>
<line>the English language. As soon as it was learned that he was not an</line>
<line>American Negro, all the signs of indignation disappeared. The man who</line>
<line>was the innocent cause of the excitement, though, found it prudent</line>
<line>after that not to speak English.</line>

<line>At the end of my first year with the Indians there came another opening</line>
<line>for me at Hampton, which, as I look back over my life now, seems to</line>
<line>have come providentially, to help to prepare me for my work at Tuskegee</line>
<line>later. General Armstrong had found out that there was quite a number of</line>
<line>young coloured men and women who were intensely in earnest in wishing</line>
<line>to get an education, but who were prevented from entering Hampton</line>
<line>Institute because they were too poor to be able to pay any portion of</line>
<line>the cost of their board, or even to supply themselves with books. He</line>
<line>conceived the idea of starting a night-school in connection with the</line>
<line>Institute, into which a limited number of the most promising of these</line>
<line>young men and women would be received, on condition that they were to</line>
<line>work for ten hours during the day, and attend school for two hours at</line>
<line>night. They were to be paid something above the cost of their board for</line>
<line>their work. The greater part of their earnings was to be reserved in</line>
<line>the school’s treasury as a fund to be drawn on to pay their board when</line>
<line>they had become students in the day-school, after they had spent one or</line>
<line>two years in the night-school. In this way they would obtain a start in</line>
<line>their books and a knowledge of some trade or industry, in addition to</line>
<line>the other far-reaching benefits of the institution.</line>

<line>General Armstrong asked me to take charge of the night-school, and I</line>
<line>did so. At the beginning of this school there were about twelve strong,</line>
<line>earnest men and women who entered the class. During the day the greater</line>
<line>part of the young men worked in the school’s sawmill, and the young</line>
<line>women worked in the laundry. The work was not easy in either place, but</line>
<line>in all my teaching I never taught pupils who gave me much genuine</line>
<line>satisfaction as these did. They were good students, and mastered their</line>
<line>work thoroughly. They were so much in earnest that only the ringing of</line>
<line>the retiring-bell would make them stop studying, and often they would</line>
<line>urge me to continue the lessons after the usual hour for going to bed</line>
<line>had come.</line>

<line>These students showed so much earnestness, both in their hard work</line>
<line>during the day, as well as in their application to their studies at</line>
<line>night, that I gave them the name of “The Plucky Class”—a name which</line>
<line>soon grew popular and spread throughout the institution. After a</line>
<line>student had been in the night-school long enough to prove what was in</line>
<line>him, I gave him a printed certificate which read something like this:—</line>

<line>“This is to certify that James Smith is a member of The Plucky Class of</line>
<line>the Hampton Institute, and is in good and regular standing.”</line>

<line>The students prized these certificates highly, and they added greatly</line>
<line>to the popularity of the night-school. Within a few weeks this</line>
<line>department had grown to such an extent that there were about</line>
<line>twenty-five students in attendance. I have followed the course of many</line>
<line>of these twenty-five men and women ever since then, and they are now</line>
<line>holding important and useful positions in nearly every part of the</line>
<line>South. The night-school at Hampton, which started with only twelve</line>
<line>students, now numbers between three and four hundred, and is one of the</line>
<line>permanent and most important features of the institution.</line>




</chapter>

<chapter>
<title>Chapter VII. Early Days At Tuskegee</title>


<line>During the time that I had charge of the Indians and the night-school</line>
<line>at Hampton, I pursued some studies myself, under the direction of the</line>
<line>instructors there. One of these instructors was the Rev. Dr. H.B.</line>
<line>Frissell, the present Principal of the Hampton Institute, General</line>
<line>Armstrong’s successor.</line>

<line>In May, 1881, near the close of my first year in teaching the</line>
<line>night-school, in a way that I had not dared expect, the opportunity</line>
<line>opened for me to begin my life-work. One night in the chapel, after the</line>
<line>usual chapel exercises were over, General Armstrong referred to the</line>
<line>fact that he had received a letter from some gentlemen in Alabama</line>
<line>asking him to recommend some one to take charge of what was to be a</line>
<line>normal school for the coloured people in the little town of Tuskegee in</line>
<line>that state. These gentlemen seemed to take it for granted that no</line>
<line>coloured man suitable for the position could be secured, and they were</line>
<line>expecting the General to recommend a white man for the place. The next</line>
<line>day General Armstrong sent for me to come to his office, and, much to</line>
<line>my surprise, asked me if I thought I could fill the position in</line>
<line>Alabama. I told him that I would be willing to try. Accordingly, he</line>
<line>wrote to the people who had applied to him for the information, that he</line>
<line>did not know of any white man to suggest, but if they would be willing</line>
<line>to take a coloured man, he had one whom he could recommend. In this</line>
<line>letter he gave them my name.</line>

<line>Several days passed before anything more was heard about the matter.</line>
<line>Some time afterward, one Sunday evening during the chapel exercises, a</line>
<line>messenger came in and handed the general a telegram. At the end of the</line>
<line>exercises he read the telegram to the school. In substance, these were</line>
<line>its words: “Booker T. Washington will suit us. Send him at once.”</line>

<line>There was a great deal of joy expressed among the students and</line>
<line>teachers, and I received very hearty congratulations. I began to get</line>
<line>ready at once to go to Tuskegee. I went by way of my old home in West</line>
<line>Virginia, where I remained for several days, after which I proceeded to</line>
<line>Tuskegee. I found Tuskegee to be a town of about two thousand</line>
<line>inhabitants, nearly one-half of whom were coloured. It was in what was</line>
<line>known as the Black Belt of the South. In the county in which Tuskegee</line>
<line>is situated the coloured people outnumbered the whites by about three</line>
<line>to one. In some of the adjoining and near-by counties the proportion</line>
<line>was not far from six coloured persons to one white.</line>

<line>I have often been asked to define the term “Black Belt.” So far as I</line>
<line>can learn, the term was first used to designate a part of the country</line>
<line>which was distinguished by the colour of the soil. The part of the</line>
<line>country possessing this thick, dark, and naturally rich soil was, of</line>
<line>course, the part of the South where the slaves were most profitable,</line>
<line>and consequently they were taken there in the largest numbers. Later,</line>
<line>and especially since the war, the term seems to be used wholly in a</line>
<line>political sense—that is, to designate the counties where the black</line>
<line>people outnumber the white.</line>

<line>Before going to Tuskegee I had expected to find there a building and</line>
<line>all the necessary apparatus ready for me to begin teaching. To my</line>
<line>disappointment, I found nothing of the kind. I did find, though, that</line>
<line>which no costly building and apparatus can supply,—hundreds of hungry,</line>
<line>earnest souls who wanted to secure knowledge.</line>

<line>Tuskegee seemed an ideal place for the school. It was in the midst of</line>
<line>the great bulk of the Negro population, and was rather secluded, being</line>
<line>five miles from the main line of railroad, with which it was connected</line>
<line>by a short line. During the days of slavery, and since, the town had</line>
<line>been a centre for the education of the white people. This was an added</line>
<line>advantage, for the reason that I found the white people possessing a</line>
<line>degree of culture and education that is not surpassed by many</line>
<line>localities. While the coloured people were ignorant, they had not, as a</line>
<line>rule, degraded and weakened their bodies by vices such as are common to</line>
<line>the lower class of people in the large cities. In general, I found the</line>
<line>relations between the two races pleasant. For example, the largest, and</line>
<line>I think at that time the only hardware store in the town was owned and</line>
<line>operated jointly by a coloured man and a white man. This copartnership</line>
<line>continued until the death of the white partner.</line>

<line>I found that about a year previous to my going to Tuskegee some of the</line>
<line>coloured people who had heard something of the work of education being</line>
<line>done at Hampton had applied to the state Legislature, through their</line>
<line>representatives, for a small appropriation to be used in starting a</line>
<line>normal school in Tuskegee. This request the Legislature had complied</line>
<line>with to the extent of granting an annual appropriation of two thousand</line>
<line>dollars. I soon learned, however, that this money could be used only</line>
<line>for the payment of the salaries of the instructors, and that there was</line>
<line>no provision for securing land, buildings, or apparatus. The task</line>
<line>before me did not seem a very encouraging one. It seemed much like</line>
<line>making bricks without straw. The coloured people were overjoyed, and</line>
<line>were constantly offering their services in any way in which they could</line>
<line>be of assistance in getting the school started.</line>

<line>My first task was to find a place in which to open the school. After</line>
<line>looking the town over with some care, the most suitable place that</line>
<line>could be secured seemed to be a rather dilapidated shanty near the</line>
<line>coloured Methodist church, together with the church itself as a sort of</line>
<line>assembly-room. Both the church and the shanty were in about as bad</line>
<line>condition as was possible. I recall that during the first months of</line>
<line>school that I taught in this building it was in such poor repair that,</line>
<line>whenever it rained, one of the older students would very kindly leave</line>
<line>his lessons and hold an umbrella over me while I heard the recitations</line>
<line>of the others. I remember, also, that on more than one occasion my</line>
<line>landlady held an umbrella over me while I ate breakfast.</line>

<line>At the time I went to Alabama the coloured people were taking</line>
<line>considerable interest in politics, and they were very anxious that I</line>
<line>should become one of them politically, in every respect. They seemed to</line>
<line>have a little distrust of strangers in this regard. I recall that one</line>
<line>man, who seemed to have been designated by the others to look after my</line>
<line>political destiny, came to me on several occasions and said, with a</line>
<line>good deal of earnestness: “We wants you to be sure to vote jes’ like we</line>
<line>votes. We can’t read de newspapers very much, but we knows how to vote,</line>
<line>an’ we wants you to vote jes’ like we votes.” He added: “We watches de</line>
<line>white man, and we keeps watching de white man till we finds out which</line>
<line>way de white man’s gwine to vote; an’ when we finds out which way de</line>
<line>white man’s gwine to vote, den we votes ’xactly de other way. Den we</line>
<line>knows we’s right.”</line>

<line>I am glad to add, however, that at the present time the disposition to</line>
<line>vote against the white man merely because he is white is largely</line>
<line>disappearing, and the race is learning to vote from principle, for what</line>
<line>the voter considers to be for the best interests of both races.</line>

<line>I reached Tuskegee, as I have said, early in June, 1881. The first</line>
<line>month I spent in finding accommodations for the school, and in</line>
<line>travelling through Alabama, examining into the actual life of the</line>
<line>people, especially in the court districts, and in getting the school</line>
<line>advertised among the class of people that I wanted to have attend it.</line>
<line>The most of my travelling was done over the country roads, with a mule</line>
<line>and a cart or a mule and a buggy wagon for conveyance. I ate and slept</line>
<line>with the people, in their little cabins. I saw their farms, their</line>
<line>schools, their churches. Since, in the case of the most of these</line>
<line>visits, there had been no notice given in advance that a stranger was</line>
<line>expected, I had the advantage of seeing the real, everyday life of the</line>
<line>people.</line>

<line>In the plantation districts I found that, as a rule, the whole family</line>
<line>slept in one room, and that in addition to the immediate family there</line>
<line>sometimes were relatives, or others not related to the family, who</line>
<line>slept in the same room. On more than one occasion I went outside the</line>
<line>house to get ready for bed, or to wait until the family had gone to</line>
<line>bed. They usually contrived some kind of a place for me to sleep,</line>
<line>either on the floor or in a special part of another’s bed. Rarely was</line>
<line>there any place provided in the cabin where one could bathe even the</line>
<line>face and hands, but usually some provision was made for this outside</line>
<line>the house, in the yard.</line>

<line>The common diet of the people was fat pork and corn bread. At times I</line>
<line>have eaten in cabins where they had only corn bread and “black-eye</line>
<line>peas” cooked in plain water. The people seemed to have no other idea</line>
<line>than to live on this fat meat and corn bread,—the meat, and the meal of</line>
<line>which the bread was made, having been bought at a high price at a store</line>
<line>in town, notwithstanding the fact that the land all about the cabin</line>
<line>homes could easily have been made to produce nearly every kind of</line>
<line>garden vegetable that is raised anywhere in the country. Their one</line>
<line>object seemed to be to plant nothing but cotton; and in many cases</line>
<line>cotton was planted up to the very door of the cabin.</line>

<line>In these cabin homes I often found sewing-machines which had been</line>
<line>bought, or were being bought, on instalments, frequently at a cost of</line>
<line>as much as sixty dollars, or showy clocks for which the occupants of</line>
<line>the cabins had paid twelve or fourteen dollars. I remember that on one</line>
<line>occasion when I went into one of these cabins for dinner, when I sat</line>
<line>down to the table for a meal with the four members of the family, I</line>
<line>noticed that, while there were five of us at the table, there was but</line>
<line>one fork for the five of us to use. Naturally there was an awkward</line>
<line>pause on my part. In the opposite corner of that same cabin was an</line>
<line>organ for which the people told me they were paying sixty dollars in</line>
<line>monthly instalments. One fork, and a sixty-dollar organ!</line>

<line>In most cases the sewing-machine was not used, the clocks were so</line>
<line>worthless that they did not keep correct time—and if they had, in nine</line>
<line>cases out of ten there would have been no one in the family who could</line>
<line>have told the time of day—while the organ, of course, was rarely used</line>
<line>for want of a person who could play upon it.</line>

<line>In the case to which I have referred, where the family sat down to the</line>
<line>table for the meal at which I was their guest, I could see plainly that</line>
<line>this was an awkward and unusual proceeding, and was done in my honour.</line>
<line>In most cases, when the family got up in the morning, for example, the</line>
<line>wife would put a piece of meat in a frying-pan and put a lump of dough</line>
<line>in a “skillet,” as they called it. These utensils would be placed on</line>
<line>the fire, and in ten or fifteen minutes breakfast would be ready.</line>
<line>Frequently the husband would take his bread and meat in his hand and</line>
<line>start for the field, eating as he walked. The mother would sit down in</line>
<line>a corner and eat her breakfast, perhaps from a plate and perhaps</line>
<line>directly from the “skillet” or frying-pan, while the children would eat</line>
<line>their portion of the bread and meat while running about the yard. At</line>
<line>certain seasons of the year, when meat was scarce, it was rarely that</line>
<line>the children who were not old enough or strong enough to work in the</line>
<line>fields would have the luxury of meat.</line>

<line>The breakfast over, and with practically no attention given to the</line>
<line>house, the whole family would, as a general thing, proceed to the</line>
<line>cotton-field. Every child that was large enough to carry a hoe was put</line>
<line>to work, and the baby—for usually there was at least one baby—would be</line>
<line>laid down at the end of the cotton row, so that its mother could give</line>
<line>it a certain amount of attention when she had finished chopping her</line>
<line>row. The noon meal and the supper were taken in much the same way as</line>
<line>the breakfast.</line>

<line>All the days of the family would be spent after much this same routine,</line>
<line>except Saturday and Sunday. On Saturday the whole family would spent at</line>
<line>least half a day, and often a whole day, in town. The idea in going to</line>
<line>town was, I suppose, to do shopping, but all the shopping that the</line>
<line>whole family had money for could have been attended to in ten minutes</line>
<line>by one person. Still, the whole family remained in town for most of the</line>
<line>day, spending the greater part of the time in standing on the streets,</line>
<line>the women, too often, sitting about somewhere smoking or dipping snuff.</line>
<line>Sunday was usually spent in going to some big meeting. With few</line>
<line>exceptions, I found that the crops were mortgaged in the counties where</line>
<line>I went, and that the most of the coloured farmers were in debt. The</line>
<line>state had not been able to build schoolhouses in the country districts,</line>
<line>and, as a rule, the schools were taught in churches or in log cabins.</line>
<line>More than once, while on my journeys, I found that there was no</line>
<line>provision made in the house used for school purposes for heating the</line>
<line>building during the winter, and consequently a fire had to be built in</line>
<line>the yard, and teacher and pupils passed in and out of the house as they</line>
<line>got cold or warm. With few exceptions, I found the teachers in these</line>
<line>country schools to be miserably poor in preparation for their work, and</line>
<line>poor in moral character. The schools were in session from three to five</line>
<line>months. There was practically no apparatus in the schoolhouses, except</line>
<line>that occasionally there was a rough blackboard. I recall that one day I</line>
<line>went into a schoolhouse—or rather into an abandoned log cabin that was</line>
<line>being used as a schoolhouse—and found five pupils who were studying a</line>
<line>lesson from one book. Two of these, on the front seat, were using the</line>
<line>book between them; behind these were two others peeping over the</line>
<line>shoulders of the first two, and behind the four was a fifth little</line>
<line>fellow who was peeping over the shoulders of all four.</line>

<line>What I have said concerning the character of the schoolhouses and</line>
<line>teachers will also apply quite accurately as a description of the</line>
<line>church buildings and the ministers.</line>

<line>I met some very interesting characters during my travels. As</line>
<line>illustrating the peculiar mental processes of the country people, I</line>
<line>remember that I asked one coloured man, who was about sixty years old,</line>
<line>to tell me something of his history. He said that he had been born in</line>
<line>Virginia, and sold into Alabama in 1845. I asked him how many were sold</line>
<line>at the same time. He said, “There were five of us; myself and brother</line>
<line>and three mules.”</line>

<line>In giving all these descriptions of what I saw during my month of</line>
<line>travel in the country around Tuskegee, I wish my readers to keep in</line>
<line>mind the fact that there were many encouraging exceptions to the</line>
<line>conditions which I have described. I have stated in such plain words</line>
<line>what I saw, mainly for the reason that later I want to emphasize the</line>
<line>encouraging changes that have taken place in the community, not wholly</line>
<line>by the work of the Tuskegee school, but by that of other institutions</line>
<line>as well.</line>




</chapter>

<chapter>
<title>Chapter VIII. Teaching School In A Stable And A Hen-House</title>


<line>I confess that what I saw during my month of travel and investigation</line>
<line>left me with a very heavy heart. The work to be done in order to lift</line>
<line>these people up seemed almost beyond accomplishing. I was only one</line>
<line>person, and it seemed to me that the little effort which I could put</line>
<line>forth could go such a short distance toward bringing about results. I</line>
<line>wondered if I could accomplish anything, and if it were worth while for</line>
<line>me to try.</line>

<line>Of one thing I felt more strongly convinced than ever, after spending</line>
<line>this month in seeing the actual life of the coloured people, and that</line>
<line>was that, in order to lift them up, something must be done more than</line>
<line>merely to imitate New England education as it then existed. I saw more</line>
<line>clearly than ever the wisdom of the system which General Armstrong had</line>
<line>inaugurated at Hampton. To take the children of such people as I had</line>
<line>been among for a month, and each day give them a few hours of mere book</line>
<line>education, I felt would be almost a waste of time.</line>

<line>After consultation with the citizens of Tuskegee, I set July 4, 1881,</line>
<line>as the day for the opening of the school in the little shanty and</line>
<line>church which had been secured for its accommodation. The white people,</line>
<line>as well as the coloured, were greatly interested in the starting of the</line>
<line>new school, and the opening day was looked forward to with much earnest</line>
<line>discussion. There were not a few white people in the vicinity of</line>
<line>Tuskegee who looked with some disfavour upon the project. They</line>
<line>questioned its value to the coloured people, and had a fear that it</line>
<line>might result in bringing about trouble between the races. Some had the</line>
<line>feeling that in proportion as the Negro received education, in the same</line>
<line>proportion would his value decrease as an economic factor in the state.</line>
<line>These people feared the result of education would be that the Negroes</line>
<line>would leave the farms, and that it would be difficult to secure them</line>
<line>for domestic service.</line>

<line>The white people who questioned the wisdom of starting this new school</line>
<line>had in their minds pictures of what was called an educated Negro, with</line>
<line>a high hat, imitation gold eye-glasses, a showy walking-stick, kid</line>
<line>gloves, fancy boots, and what not—in a word, a man who was determined</line>
<line>to live by his wits. It was difficult for these people to see how</line>
<line>education would produce any other kind of a coloured man.</line>

<line>In the midst of all the difficulties which I encountered in getting the</line>
<line>little school started, and since then through a period of nineteen</line>
<line>years, there are two men among all the many friends of the school in</line>
<line>Tuskegee upon whom I have depended constantly for advice and guidance;</line>
<line>and the success of the undertaking is largely due to these men, from</line>
<line>whom I have never sought anything in vain. I mention them simply as</line>
<line>types. One is a white man and an ex-slaveholder, Mr. George W.</line>
<line>Campbell; the other is a black man and an ex-slave, Mr. Lewis Adams.</line>
<line>These were the men who wrote to General Armstrong for a teacher.</line>

<line>Mr. Campbell is a merchant and banker, and had had little experience in</line>
<line>dealing with matters pertaining to education. Mr. Adams was a mechanic,</line>
<line>and had learned the trades of shoemaking, harness-making, and</line>
<line>tinsmithing during the days of slavery. He had never been to school a</line>
<line>day in his life, but in some way he had learned to read and write while</line>
<line>a slave. From the first, these two men saw clearly what my plan of</line>
<line>education was, sympathized with me, and supported me in every effort.</line>
<line>In the days which were darkest financially for the school, Mr. Campbell</line>
<line>was never appealed to when he was not willing to extend all the aid in</line>
<line>his power. I do not know two men, one an ex-slaveholder, one an</line>
<line>ex-slave, whose advice and judgment I would feel more like following in</line>
<line>everything which concerns the life and development of the school at</line>
<line>Tuskegee than those of these two men.</line>

<line>I have always felt that Mr. Adams, in a large degree, derived his</line>
<line>unusual power of mind from the training given his hands in the process</line>
<line>of mastering well three trades during the days of slavery. If one goes</line>
<line>to-day into any Southern town, and asks for the leading and most</line>
<line>reliable coloured man in the community, I believe that in five cases</line>
<line>out of ten he will be directed to a Negro who learned a trade during</line>
<line>the days of slavery.</line>

<line>On the morning that the school opened, thirty students reported for</line>
<line>admission. I was the only teacher. The students were about equally</line>
<line>divided between the sexes. Most of them lived in Macon County, the</line>
<line>county in which Tuskegee is situated, and of which it is the</line>
<line>county-seat. A great many more students wanted to enter the school, but</line>
<line>it had been decided to receive only those who were above fifteen years</line>
<line>of age, and who had previously received some education. The greater</line>
<line>part of the thirty were public-school teachers, and some of them were</line>
<line>nearly forty years of age. With the teachers came some of their former</line>
<line>pupils, and when they were examined it was amusing to note that in</line>
<line>several cases the pupil entered a higher class than did his former</line>
<line>teacher. It was also interesting to note how many big books some of</line>
<line>them had studied, and how many high-sounding subjects some of them</line>
<line>claimed to have mastered. The bigger the book and the longer the name</line>
<line>of the subject, the prouder they felt of their accomplishment. Some had</line>
<line>studied Latin, and one or two Greek. This they thought entitled them to</line>
<line>special distinction.</line>

<line>In fact, one of the saddest things I saw during the month of travel</line>
<line>which I have described was a young man, who had attended some high</line>
<line>school, sitting down in a one-room cabin, with grease on his clothing,</line>
<line>filth all around him, and weeds in the yard and garden, engaged in</line>
<line>studying a French grammar.</line>

<line>The students who came first seemed to be fond of memorizing long and</line>
<line>complicated “rules” in grammar and mathematics, but had little thought</line>
<line>or knowledge of applying these rules to their everyday affairs of their</line>
<line>life. One subject which they liked to talk about, and tell me that they</line>
<line>had mastered, in arithmetic, was “banking and discount,” but I soon</line>
<line>found out that neither they nor almost any one in the neighbourhood in</line>
<line>which they had lived had ever had a bank account. In registering the</line>
<line>names of the students, I found that almost every one of them had one or</line>
<line>more middle initials. When I asked what the “J” stood for, in the name</line>
<line>of John J. Jones, it was explained to me that this was a part of his</line>
<line>“entitles.” Most of the students wanted to get an education because</line>
<line>they thought it would enable them to earn more money as</line>
<line>school-teachers.</line>

<line>Notwithstanding what I have said about them in these respects, I have</line>
<line>never seen a more earnest and willing company of young men and women</line>
<line>than these students were. They were all willing to learn the right</line>
<line>thing as soon as it was shown them what was right. I was determined to</line>
<line>start them off on a solid and thorough foundation, so far as their</line>
<line>books were concerned. I soon learned that most of them had the merest</line>
<line>smattering of the high-sounding things that they had studied. While</line>
<line>they could locate the Desert of Sahara or the capital of China on an</line>
<line>artificial globe, I found out that the girls could not locate the</line>
<line>proper places for the knives and forks on an actual dinner-table, or</line>
<line>the places on which the bread and meat should be set.</line>

<line>I had to summon a good deal of courage to take a student who had been</line>
<line>studying cube root and “banking and discount,” and explain to him that</line>
<line>the wisest thing for him to do first was thoroughly master the</line>
<line>multiplication table.</line>

<line>The number of pupils increased each week, until by the end of the first</line>
<line>month there were nearly fifty. Many of them, however, said that, as</line>
<line>they could remain only for two or three months, they wanted to enter a</line>
<line>high class and get a diploma the first year if possible.</line>

<line>At the end of the first six weeks a new and rare face entered the</line>
<line>school as a co-teacher. This was Miss Olivia A. Davidson, who later</line>
<line>became my wife. Miss Davidson was born in Ohio, and received her</line>
<line>preparatory education in the public schools of that state. When little</line>
<line>more than a girl, she heard of the need of teachers in the South. She</line>
<line>went to the state of Mississippi and began teaching there. Later she</line>
<line>taught in the city of Memphis. While teaching in Mississippi, one of</line>
<line>her pupils became ill with smallpox. Every one in the community was so</line>
<line>frightened that no one would nurse the boy. Miss Davidson closed her</line>
<line>school and remained by the bedside of the boy night and day until he</line>
<line>recovered. While she was at her Ohio home on her vacation, the worst</line>
<line>epidemic of yellow fever broke out in Memphis, Tenn., that perhaps has</line>
<line>ever occurred in the South. When she heard of this, she at once</line>
<line>telegraphed the Mayor of Memphis, offering her services as a</line>
<line>yellow-fever nurse, although she had never had the disease.</line>

<line>Miss Davidon’s experience in the South showed her that the people</line>
<line>needed something more than mere book-learning. She heard of the Hampton</line>
<line>system of education, and decided that this was what she wanted in order</line>
<line>to prepare herself for better work in the South. The attention of Mrs.</line>
<line>Mary Hemenway, of Boston, was attracted to her rare ability. Through</line>
<line>Mrs. Hemenway’s kindness and generosity, Miss Davidson, after</line>
<line>graduating at Hampton, received an opportunity to complete a two years’</line>
<line>course of training at the Massachusetts State Normal School at</line>
<line>Framingham.</line>

<line>Before she went to Framingham, some one suggested to Miss Davidson</line>
<line>that, since she was so very light in colour, she might find it more</line>
<line>comfortable not to be known as a coloured women in this school in</line>
<line>Massachusetts. She at once replied that under no circumstances and for</line>
<line>no considerations would she consent to deceive any one in regard to her</line>
<line>racial identity.</line>

<line>Soon after her graduation from the Framingham institution, Miss</line>
<line>Davidson came to Tuskegee, bringing into the school many valuable and</line>
<line>fresh ideas as to the best methods of teaching, as well as a rare moral</line>
<line>character and a life of unselfishness that I think has seldom been</line>
<line>equalled. No single individual did more toward laying the foundations</line>
<line>of the Tuskegee Institute so as to insure the successful work that has</line>
<line>been done there than Olivia A. Davidson.</line>

<line>Miss Davidson and I began consulting as to the future of the school</line>
<line>from the first. The students were making progress in learning books and</line>
<line>in developing their minds; but it became apparent at once that, if we</line>
<line>were to make any permanent impression upon those who had come to us for</line>
<line>training we must do something besides teach them mere books. The</line>
<line>students had come from homes where they had had no opportunities for</line>
<line>lessons which would teach them how to care for their bodies. With few</line>
<line>exceptions, the homes in Tuskegee in which the students boarded were</line>
<line>but little improvement upon those from which they had come. We wanted</line>
<line>to teach the students how to bathe; how to care for their teeth and</line>
<line>clothing. We wanted to teach them what to eat, and how to eat it</line>
<line>properly, and how to care for their rooms. Aside from this, we wanted</line>
<line>to give them such a practical knowledge of some one industry, together</line>
<line>with the spirit of industry, thrift, and economy, that they would be</line>
<line>sure of knowing how to make a living after they had left us. We wanted</line>
<line>to teach them to study actual things instead of mere books alone.</line>

<line>We found that the most of our students came from the country districts,</line>
<line>where agriculture in some form or other was the main dependence of the</line>
<line>people. We learned that about eighty-five per cent of the coloured</line>
<line>people in the Gulf states depended upon agriculture for their living.</line>
<line>Since this was true, we wanted to be careful not to educate our</line>
<line>students out of sympathy with agricultural life, so that they would be</line>
<line>attracted from the country to the cities, and yield to the temptation</line>
<line>of trying to live by their wits. We wanted to give them such an</line>
<line>education as would fit a large proportion of them to be teachers, and</line>
<line>at the same time cause them to return to the plantation districts and</line>
<line>show the people there how to put new energy and new ideas into farming,</line>
<line>as well as into the intellectual and moral and religious life of the</line>
<line>people.</line>

<line>All these ideas and needs crowded themselves upon us with a seriousness</line>
<line>that seemed well-nigh overwhelming. What were we to do? We had only the</line>
<line>little old shanty and the abandoned church which the good coloured</line>
<line>people of the town of Tuskegee had kindly loaned us for the</line>
<line>accommodation of the classes. The number of students was increasing</line>
<line>daily. The more we saw of them, and the more we travelled through the</line>
<line>country districts, the more we saw that our efforts were reaching, to</line>
<line>only a partial degree, the actual needs of the people whom we wanted to</line>
<line>lift up through the medium of the students whom we should educate and</line>
<line>send out as leaders.</line>

<line>The more we talked with the students, who were then coming to us from</line>
<line>several parts of the state, the more we found that the chief ambition</line>
<line>among a large proportion of them was to get an education so that they</line>
<line>would not have to work any longer with their hands.</line>

<line>This is illustrated by a story told of a coloured man in Alabama, who,</line>
<line>one hot day in July, while he was at work in a cotton-field, suddenly</line>
<line>stopped, and, looking toward the skies, said: “O Lawd, de cotton am so</line>
<line>grassy, de work am so hard, and the sun am so hot dat I b’lieve dis</line>
<line>darky am called to preach!”</line>

<line>About three months after the opening of the school, and at the time</line>
<line>when we were in the greatest anxiety about our work, there came into</line>
<line>market for sale an old and abandoned plantation which was situated</line>
<line>about a mile from the town of Tuskegee. The mansion house—or “big</line>
<line>house,” as it would have been called—which had been occupied by the</line>
<line>owners during slavery, had been burned. After making a careful</line>
<line>examination of the place, it seemed to be just the location that we</line>
<line>wanted in order to make our work effective and permanent.</line>

<line>But how were we to get it? The price asked for it was very little—only</line>
<line>five hundred dollars—but we had no money, and we were strangers in the</line>
<line>town and had no credit. The owner of the land agreed to let us occupy</line>
<line>the place if we could make a payment of two hundred and fifty dollars</line>
<line>down, with the understanding that the remaining two hundred and fifty</line>
<line>dollars must be paid within a year. Although five hundred dollars was</line>
<line>cheap for the land, it was a large sum when one did not have any part</line>
<line>of it.</line>

<line>In the midst of the difficulty I summoned a great deal of courage and</line>
<line>wrote to my friend General J.F.B. Marshall, the Treasurer of the</line>
<line>Hampton Institute, putting the situation before him and beseeching him</line>
<line>to lend me the two hundred and fifty dollars on my own personal</line>
<line>responsibility. Within a few days a reply came to the effect that he</line>
<line>had no authority to lend me the money belonging to the Hampton</line>
<line>Institute, but that he would gladly lend me the amount needed from his</line>
<line>own personal funds.</line>

<line>I confess that the securing of this money in this way was a great</line>
<line>surprise to me, as well as a source of gratification. Up to that time I</line>
<line>never had had in my possession so much money as one hundred dollars at</line>
<line>a time, and the loan which I had asked General Marshall for seemed a</line>
<line>tremendously large sum to me. The fact of my being responsible for the</line>
<line>repaying of such a large amount of money weighed very heavily upon me.</line>

<line>I lost no time in getting ready to move the school on to the new farm.</line>
<line>At the time we occupied the place there were standing upon it a cabin,</line>
<line>formerly used as a dining room, an old kitchen, a stable, and an old</line>
<line>hen-house. Within a few weeks we had all of these structures in use.</line>
<line>The stable was repaired and used as a recitation-room, and very</line>
<line>presently the hen-house was utilized for the same purpose.</line>

<line>I recall that one morning, when I told an old coloured man who lived</line>
<line>near, and who sometimes helped me, that our school had grown so large</line>
<line>that it would be necessary for us to use the hen-house for school</line>
<line>purposes, and that I wanted him to help me give it a thorough cleaning</line>
<line>out the next day, he replied, in the most earnest manner: “What you</line>
<line>mean, boss? You sholy ain’t gwine clean out de hen-house in de</line>
<line>day-time?”</line>

<line>Nearly all the work of getting the new location ready for school</line>
<line>purposes was done by the students after school was over in the</line>
<line>afternoon. As soon as we got the cabins in condition to be used, I</line>
<line>determined to clear up some land so that we could plant a crop. When I</line>
<line>explained my plan to the young men, I noticed that they did not seem to</line>
<line>take to it very kindly. It was hard for them to see the connection</line>
<line>between clearing land and an education. Besides, many of them had been</line>
<line>school-teachers, and they questioned whether or not clearing land would</line>
<line>be in keeping with their dignity. In order to relieve them from any</line>
<line>embarrassment, each afternoon after school I took my axe and led the</line>
<line>way to the woods. When they saw that I was not afraid or ashamed to</line>
<line>work, they began to assist with more enthusiasm. We kept at the work</line>
<line>each afternoon, until we had cleared about twenty acres and had planted</line>
<line>a crop.</line>

<line>In the meantime Miss Davidson was devising plans to repay the loan. Her</line>
<line>first effort was made by holding festivals, or “suppers.” She made a</line>
<line>personal canvass among the white and coloured families in the town of</line>
<line>Tuskegee, and got them to agree to give something, like a cake, a</line>
<line>chicken, bread, or pies, that could be sold at the festival. Of course</line>
<line>the coloured people were glad to give anything that they could spare,</line>
<line>but I want to add that Miss Davidson did not apply to a single white</line>
<line>family, so far as I now remember, that failed to donate something; and</line>
<line>in many ways the white families showed their interest in the school.</line>

<line>Several of these festivals were held, and quite a little sum of money</line>
<line>was raised. A canvass was also made among the people of both races for</line>
<line>direct gifts of money, and most of those applied to gave small sums. It</line>
<line>was often pathetic to note the gifts of the older coloured people, most</line>
<line>of whom had spent their best days in slavery. Sometimes they would give</line>
<line>five cents, sometimes twenty-five cents. Sometimes the contribution was</line>
<line>a quilt, or a quantity of sugarcane. I recall one old coloured women</line>
<line>who was about seventy years of age, who came to see me when we were</line>
<line>raising money to pay for the farm. She hobbled into the room where I</line>
<line>was, leaning on a cane. She was clad in rags; but they were clean. She</line>
<line>said: “Mr. Washin’ton, God knows I spent de bes’ days of my life in</line>
<line>slavery. God knows I’s ignorant an’ poor; but,” she added, “I knows</line>
<line>what you an’ Miss Davidson is tryin’ to do. I knows you is tryin’ to</line>
<line>make better men an’ better women for de coloured race. I ain’t got no</line>
<line>money, but I wants you to take dese six eggs, what I’s been savin’ up,</line>
<line>an’ I wants you to put dese six eggs into the eddication of dese boys</line>
<line>an’ gals.”</line>

<line>Since the work at Tuskegee started, it has been my privilege to receive</line>
<line>many gifts for the benefit of the institution, but never any, I think,</line>
<line>that touched me so deeply as this one.</line>




</chapter>

<chapter>
<title>Chapter IX. Anxious Days And Sleepless Nights</title>


<line>The coming of Christmas, that first year of our residence in Alabama,</line>
<line>gave us an opportunity to get a farther insight into the real life of</line>
<line>the people. The first thing that reminded us that Christmas had arrived</line>
<line>was the “foreday” visits of scores of children rapping at our doors,</line>
<line>asking for “Chris’mus gifts! Chris’mus gifts!” Between the hours of two</line>
<line>o’clock and five o’clock in the morning I presume that we must have had</line>
<line>a half-hundred such calls. This custom prevails throughout this portion</line>
<line>of the South to-day.</line>

<line>During the days of slavery it was a custom quite generally observed</line>
<line>throughout all the Southern states to give the coloured people a week</line>
<line>of holiday at Christmas, or to allow the holiday to continue as long as</line>
<line>the “yule log” lasted. The male members of the race, and often the</line>
<line>female members, were expected to get drunk. We found that for a whole</line>
<line>week the coloured people in and around Tuskegee dropped work the day</line>
<line>before Christmas, and that it was difficult for any one to perform any</line>
<line>service from the time they stopped work until after the New Year.</line>
<line>Persons who at other times did not use strong drink thought it quite</line>
<line>the proper thing to indulge in it rather freely during the Christmas</line>
<line>week. There was a widespread hilarity, and a free use of guns, pistols,</line>
<line>and gunpowder generally. The sacredness of the season seemed to have</line>
<line>been almost wholly lost sight of.</line>

<line>During this first Christmas vacation I went some distance from the town</line>
<line>to visit the people on one of the large plantations. In their poverty</line>
<line>and ignorance it was pathetic to see their attempts to get joy out of</line>
<line>the season that in most parts of the country is so sacred and so dear</line>
<line>to the heart. In one cabin I notice that all that the five children had</line>
<line>to remind them of the coming of Christ was a single bunch of</line>
<line>firecrackers, which they had divided among them. In another cabin,</line>
<line>where there were at least a half-dozen persons, they had only ten</line>
<line>cents’ worth of ginger-cakes, which had been bought in the store the</line>
<line>day before. In another family they had only a few pieces of sugarcane.</line>
<line>In still another cabin I found nothing but a new jug of cheap, mean</line>
<line>whiskey, which the husband and wife were making free use of,</line>
<line>notwithstanding the fact that the husband was one of the local</line>
<line>ministers. In a few instances I found that the people had gotten hold</line>
<line>of some bright-coloured cards that had been designed for advertising</line>
<line>purposes, and were making the most of these. In other homes some member</line>
<line>of the family had bought a new pistol. In the majority of cases there</line>
<line>was nothing to be seen in the cabin to remind one of the coming of the</line>
<line>Saviour, except that the people had ceased work in the fields and were</line>
<line>lounging about their homes. At night, during Christmas week, they</line>
<line>usually had what they called a “frolic,” in some cabin on the</line>
<line>plantation. That meant a kind of rough dance, where there was likely to</line>
<line>be a good deal of whiskey used, and where there might be some shooting</line>
<line>or cutting with razors.</line>

<line>While I was making this Christmas visit I met an old coloured man who</line>
<line>was one of the numerous local preachers, who tried to convince me, from</line>
<line>the experience Adam had in the Garden of Eden, that God had cursed all</line>
<line>labour, and that, therefore, it was a sin for any man to work. For that</line>
<line>reason this man sought to do as little work as possible. He seemed at</line>
<line>that time to be supremely happy, because he was living, as he expressed</line>
<line>it, through one week that was free from sin.</line>

<line>In the school we made a special effort to teach our students the</line>
<line>meaning of Christmas, and to give them lessons in its proper</line>
<line>observance. In this we have been successful to a degree that makes me</line>
<line>feel safe in saying that the season now has a new meaning, not only</line>
<line>through all that immediate region, but, in a measure, wherever our</line>
<line>graduates have gone.</line>

<line>At the present time one of the most satisfactory features of the</line>
<line>Christmas and Thanksgiving season at Tuskegee is the unselfish and</line>
<line>beautiful way in which our graduates and students spend their time in</line>
<line>administering to the comfort and happiness of others, especially the</line>
<line>unfortunate. Not long ago some of our young men spent a holiday in</line>
<line>rebuilding a cabin for a helpless coloured women who was about</line>
<line>seventy-five years old. At another time I remember that I made it known</line>
<line>in chapel, one night, that a very poor student was suffering from cold,</line>
<line>because he needed a coat. The next morning two coats were sent to my</line>
<line>office for him.</line>

<line>I have referred to the disposition on the part of the white people in</line>
<line>the town of Tuskegee and vicinity to help the school. From the first, I</line>
<line>resolved to make the school a real part of the community in which it</line>
<line>was located. I was determined that no one should have the feeling that</line>
<line>it was a foreign institution, dropped down in the midst of the people,</line>
<line>for which they had no responsibility and in which they had no interest.</line>
<line>I noticed that the very fact that they had been asking to contribute</line>
<line>toward the purchase of the land made them begin to feel as if it was</line>
<line>going to be their school, to a large degree. I noted that just in</line>
<line>proportion as we made the white people feel that the institution was a</line>
<line>part of the life of the community, and that, while we wanted to make</line>
<line>friends in Boston, for example, we also wanted to make white friends in</line>
<line>Tuskegee, and that we wanted to make the school of real service to all</line>
<line>the people, their attitude toward the school became favourable.</line>

<line>Perhaps I might add right here, what I hope to demonstrate later, that,</line>
<line>so far as I know, the Tuskegee school at the present time has no warmer</line>
<line>and more enthusiastic friends anywhere than it has among the white</line>
<line>citizens of Tuskegee and throughout the state of Alabama and the entire</line>
<line>South. From the first, I have advised our people in the South to make</line>
<line>friends in every straightforward, manly way with their next-door</line>
<line>neighbour, whether he be a black man or a white man. I have also</line>
<line>advised them, where no principle is at stake, to consult the interests</line>
<line>of their local communities, and to advise with their friends in regard</line>
<line>to their voting.</line>

<line>For several months the work of securing the money with which to pay for</line>
<line>the farm went on without ceasing. At the end of three months enough was</line>
<line>secured to repay the loan of two hundred and fifty dollars to General</line>
<line>Marshall, and within two months more we had secured the entire five</line>
<line>hundred dollars and had received a deed of the one hundred acres of</line>
<line>land. This gave us a great deal of satisfaction. It was not only a</line>
<line>source of satisfaction to secure a permanent location for the school,</line>
<line>but it was equally satisfactory to know that the greater part of the</line>
<line>money with which it was paid for had been gotten from the white and</line>
<line>coloured people in the town of Tuskegee. The most of this money was</line>
<line>obtained by holding festivals and concerts, and from small individual</line>
<line>donations.</line>

<line>Our next effort was in the direction of increasing the cultivation of</line>
<line>the land, so as to secure some return from it, and at the same time</line>
<line>give the students training in agriculture. All the industries at</line>
<line>Tuskegee have been started in natural and logical order, growing out of</line>
<line>the needs of a community settlement. We began with farming, because we</line>
<line>wanted something to eat.</line>

<line>Many of the students, also, were able to remain in school but a few</line>
<line>weeks at a time, because they had so little money with which to pay</line>
<line>their board. Thus another object which made it desirable to get an</line>
<line>industrial system started was in order to make it available as a means</line>
<line>of helping the students to earn money enough so that they might be able</line>
<line>to remain in school during the nine months’ session of the school year.</line>

<line>The first animal that the school came into possession of was an old</line>
<line>blind horse given us by one of the white citizens of Tuskegee. Perhaps</line>
<line>I may add here that at the present time the school owns over two</line>
<line>hundred horses, colts, mules, cows, calves, and oxen, and about seven</line>
<line>hundred hogs and pigs, as well as a large number of sheep and goats.</line>

<line>The school was constantly growing in numbers, so much so that, after we</line>
<line>had got the farm paid for, the cultivation of the land begun, and the</line>
<line>old cabins which we had found on the place somewhat repaired, we turned</line>
<line>our attention toward providing a large, substantial building. After</line>
<line>having given a good deal of thought to the subject, we finally had the</line>
<line>plans drawn for a building that was estimated to cost about six</line>
<line>thousand dollars. This seemed to us a tremendous sum, but we knew that</line>
<line>the school must go backward or forward, and that our work would mean</line>
<line>little unless we could get hold of the students in their home life.</line>

<line>One incident which occurred about this time gave me a great deal of</line>
<line>satisfaction as well as surprise. When it became known in the town that</line>
<line>we were discussing the plans for a new, large building, a Southern</line>
<line>white man who was operating a sawmill not far from Tuskegee came to me</line>
<line>and said that he would gladly put all the lumber necessary to erect the</line>
<line>building on the grounds, with no other guarantee for payment than my</line>
<line>word that it would be paid for when we secured some money. I told the</line>
<line>man frankly that at the time we did not have in our hands one dollar of</line>
<line>the money needed. Notwithstanding this, he insisted on being allowed to</line>
<line>put the lumber on the grounds. After we had secured some portion of the</line>
<line>money we permitted him to do this.</line>

<line>Miss Davidson again began the work of securing in various ways small</line>
<line>contributions for the new building from the white and coloured people</line>
<line>in and near Tuskegee. I think I never saw a community of people so</line>
<line>happy over anything as were the coloured people over the prospect of</line>
<line>this new building. One day, when we were holding a meeting to secure</line>
<line>funds for its erection, an old, ante-bellum coloured man came a</line>
<line>distance of twelve miles and brought in his ox-cart a large hog. When</line>
<line>the meeting was in progress, he rose in the midst of the company and</line>
<line>said that he had no money which he could give, but he had raised two</line>
<line>fine hogs, and that he had brought one of them as a contribution toward</line>
<line>the expenses of the building. He closed his announcement by saying:</line>
<line>“Any nigger that’s got any love for his race, or any respect for</line>
<line>himself, will bring a hog to the next meeting.” Quite a number of men</line>
<line>in the community also volunteered to give several days’ work, each,</line>
<line>toward the erection of the building.</line>

<line>After we had secured all the help that we could in Tuskegee, Miss</line>
<line>Davidson decided to go North for the purpose of securing additional</line>
<line>funds. For weeks she visited individuals and spoke in churches and</line>
<line>before Sunday schools and other organizations. She found this work</line>
<line>quite trying, and often embarrassing. The school was not known, but she</line>
<line>was not long in winning her way into the confidence of the best people</line>
<line>in the North.</line>

<line>The first gift from any Northern person was received from a New York</line>
<line>lady whom Miss Davidson met on the boat that was bringing her North.</line>
<line>They fell into a conversation, and the Northern lady became so much</line>
<line>interested in the effort being made at Tuskegee that before they parted</line>
<line>Miss Davidson was handed a check for fifty dollars. For some time</line>
<line>before our marriage, and also after it, Miss Davidson kept up the work</line>
<line>of securing money in the North and in the South by interesting people</line>
<line>by personal visits and through correspondence. At the same time she</line>
<line>kept in close touch with the work at Tuskegee, as lady principal and</line>
<line>classroom teacher. In addition to this, she worked among the older</line>
<line>people in and near Tuskegee, and taught a Sunday school class in the</line>
<line>town. She was never very strong, but never seemed happy unless she was</line>
<line>giving all of her strength to the cause which she loved. Often, at</line>
<line>night, after spending the day in going from door to door trying to</line>
<line>interest persons in the work at Tuskegee, she would be so exhausted</line>
<line>that she could not undress herself. A lady upon whom she called, in</line>
<line>Boston, afterward told me that at one time when Miss Davidson called</line>
<line>her to see and send up her card the lady was detained a little before</line>
<line>she could see Miss Davidson, and when she entered the parlour she found</line>
<line>Miss Davidson so exhausted that she had fallen asleep.</line>

<line>While putting up our first building, which was named Porter Hall, after</line>
<line>Mr. A.H. Porter, of Brooklyn, N.Y., who gave a generous sum toward its</line>
<line>erection, the need for money became acute. I had given one of our</line>
<line>creditors a promise that upon a certain day he should be paid four</line>
<line>hundred dollars. On the morning of that day we did not have a dollar.</line>
<line>The mail arrived at the school at ten o’clock, and in this mail there</line>
<line>was a check sent by Miss Davidson for exactly four hundred dollars. I</line>
<line>could relate many instances of almost the same character. This four</line>
<line>hundred dollars was given by two ladies in Boston. Two years later,</line>
<line>when the work at Tuskegee had grown considerably, and when we were in</line>
<line>the midst of a season when we were so much in need of money that the</line>
<line>future looked doubtful and gloomy, the same two Boston ladies sent us</line>
<line>six thousand dollars. Words cannot describe our surprise, or the</line>
<line>encouragement that the gift brought to us. Perhaps I might add here</line>
<line>that for fourteen years these same friends have sent us six thousand</line>
<line>dollars a year.</line>

<line>As soon as the plans were drawn for the new building, the students</line>
<line>began digging out the earth where the foundations were to be laid,</line>
<line>working after the regular classes were over. They had not fully</line>
<line>outgrown the idea that it was hardly the proper thing for them to use</line>
<line>their hands, since they had come there, as one of them expressed it,</line>
<line>“to be educated, and not to work.” Gradually, though, I noted with</line>
<line>satisfaction that a sentiment in favour of work was gaining ground.</line>
<line>After a few weeks of hard work the foundations were ready, and a day</line>
<line>was appointed for the laying of the corner-stone.</line>

<line>When it is considered that the laying of this corner-stone took place</line>
<line>in the heart of the South, in the “Black Belt,” in the centre of that</line>
<line>part of our country that was most devoted to slavery; that at that time</line>
<line>slavery had been abolished only about sixteen years; that only sixteen</line>
<line>years before no Negro could be taught from books without the teacher</line>
<line>receiving the condemnation of the law or of public sentiment—when all</line>
<line>this is considered, the scene that was witnessed on that spring day at</line>
<line>Tuskegee was a remarkable one. I believe there are few places in the</line>
<line>world where it could have taken place.</line>

<line>The principal address was delivered by the Hon. Waddy Thompson, the</line>
<line>Superintendent of Education for the county. About the corner-stone were</line>
<line>gathered the teachers, the students, their parents and friends, the</line>
<line>county officials—who were white—and all the leading white men in that</line>
<line>vicinity, together with many of the black men and women whom the same</line>
<line>white people but a few years before had held a title to as property.</line>
<line>The members of both races were anxious to exercise the privilege of</line>
<line>placing under the corner-stone some momento.</line>

<line>Before the building was completed we passed through some very trying</line>
<line>seasons. More than once our hearts were made to bleed, as it were,</line>
<line>because bills were falling due that we did not have the money to meet.</line>
<line>Perhaps no one who has not gone through the experience, month after</line>
<line>month, of trying to erect buildings and provide equipment for a school</line>
<line>when no one knew where the money was to come from, can properly</line>
<line>appreciate the difficulties under which we laboured. During the first</line>
<line>years at Tuskegee I recall that night after night I would roll and toss</line>
<line>on my bed, without sleep, because of the anxiety and uncertainty which</line>
<line>we were in regarding money. I knew that, in a large degree, we were</line>
<line>trying an experiment—that of testing whether or not it was possible for</line>
<line>Negroes to build up and control the affairs of a large education</line>
<line>institution. I knew that if we failed it would injure the whole race. I</line>
<line>knew that the presumption was against us. I knew that in the case of</line>
<line>white people beginning such an enterprise it would be taken for granted</line>
<line>that they were going to succeed, but in our case I felt that people</line>
<line>would be surprised if we succeeded. All this made a burden which</line>
<line>pressed down on us, sometimes, it seemed, at the rate of a thousand</line>
<line>pounds to the square inch.</line>

<line>In all our difficulties and anxieties, however, I never went to a white</line>
<line>or a black person in the town of Tuskegee for any assistance that was</line>
<line>in their power to render, without being helped according to their</line>
<line>means. More than a dozen times, when bills figuring up into the</line>
<line>hundreds of dollars were falling due, I applied to the white men of</line>
<line>Tuskegee for small loans, often borrowing small amounts from as many as</line>
<line>a half-dozen persons, to meet our obligations. One thing I was</line>
<line>determined to do from the first, and that was to keep the credit of the</line>
<line>school high; and this, I think I can say without boasting, we have done</line>
<line>all through these years.</line>

<line>I shall always remember a bit of advice given me by Mr. George W.</line>
<line>Campbell, the white man to whom I have referred to as the one who</line>
<line>induced General Armstrong to send me to Tuskegee. Soon after I entered</line>
<line>upon the work Mr. Campbell said to me, in his fatherly way:</line>
<line>“Washington, always remember that credit is capital.”</line>

<line>At one time when we were in the greatest distress for money that we</line>
<line>ever experienced, I placed the situation frankly before General</line>
<line>Armstrong. Without hesitation he gave me his personal check for all the</line>
<line>money which he had saved for his own use. This was not the only time</line>
<line>that General Armstrong helped Tuskegee in this way. I do not think I</line>
<line>have ever made this fact public before.</line>

<line>During the summer of 1882, at the end of the first year’s work of the</line>
<line>school, I was married to Miss Fannie N. Smith, of Malden, W. Va. We</line>
<line>began keeping house in Tuskegee early in the fall. This made a home for</line>
<line>our teachers, who now had been increased to four in number. My wife was</line>
<line>also a graduate of the Hampton Institute. After earnest and constant</line>
