<line>work in the interests of the school, together with her housekeeping</line>
<line>duties, my wife passed away in May, 1884. One child, Portia M.</line>
<line>Washington, was born during our marriage.</line>

<line>From the first, my wife most earnestly devoted her thoughts and time to</line>
<line>the work of the school, and was completely one with me in every</line>
<line>interest and ambition. She passed away, however, before she had an</line>
<line>opportunity of seeing what the school was designed to be.</line>




</chapter>

<chapter>
<title>Chapter X. A Harder Task Than Making Bricks Without Straw</title>


<line>From the very beginning, at Tuskegee, I was determined to have the</line>
<line>students do not only the agricultural and domestic work, but to have</line>
<line>them erect their own buildings. My plan was to have them, while</line>
<line>performing this service, taught the latest and best methods of labour,</line>
<line>so that the school would not only get the benefit of their efforts, but</line>
<line>the students themselves would be taught to see not only utility in</line>
<line>labour, but beauty and dignity; would be taught, in fact, how to lift</line>
<line>labour up from mere drudgery and toil, and would learn to love work for</line>
<line>its own sake. My plan was not to teach them to work in the old way, but</line>
<line>to show them how to make the forces of nature—air, water, steam,</line>
<line>electricity, horse-power—assist them in their labour.</line>

<line>At first many advised against the experiment of having the buildings</line>
<line>erected by the labour of the students, but I was determined to stick to</line>
<line>it. I told those who doubted the wisdom of the plan that I knew that</line>
<line>our first buildings would not be so comfortable or so complete in their</line>
<line>finish as buildings erected by the experienced hands of outside</line>
<line>workmen, but that in the teaching of civilization, self-help, and</line>
<line>self-reliance, the erection of buildings by the students themselves</line>
<line>would more than compensate for any lack of comfort or fine finish.</line>

<line>I further told those who doubted the wisdom of this plan, that the</line>
<line>majority of our students came to us in poverty, from the cabins of the</line>
<line>cotton, sugar, and rice plantations of the South, and that while I knew</line>
<line>it would please the students very much to place them at once in finely</line>
<line>constructed buildings, I felt that it would be following out a more</line>
<line>natural process of development to teach them how to construct their own</line>
<line>buildings. Mistakes I knew would be made, but these mistakes would</line>
<line>teach us valuable lessons for the future.</line>

<line>During the now nineteen years’ existence of the Tuskegee school, the</line>
<line>plan of having the buildings erected by student labour has been adhered</line>
<line>to. In this time forty buildings, counting small and large, have been</line>
<line>built, and all except four are almost wholly the product of student</line>
<line>labour. As an additional result, hundreds of men are now scattered</line>
<line>throughout the South who received their knowledge of mechanics while</line>
<line>being taught how to erect these buildings. Skill and knowledge are now</line>
<line>handed down from one set of students to another in this way, until at</line>
<line>the present time a building of any description or size can be</line>
<line>constructed wholly by our instructors and students, from the drawing of</line>
<line>the plans to the putting in of the electric fixtures, without going off</line>
<line>the grounds for a single workman.</line>

<line>Not a few times, when a new student has been led into the temptation of</line>
<line>marring the looks of some building by leadpencil marks or by the cuts</line>
<line>of a jack-knife, I have heard an old student remind him: “Don’t do</line>
<line>that. That is our building. I helped put it up.”</line>

<line>In the early days of the school I think my most trying experience was</line>
<line>in the matter of brickmaking. As soon as we got the farm work</line>
<line>reasonably well started, we directed our next efforts toward the</line>
<line>industry of making bricks. We needed these for use in connection with</line>
<line>the erection of our own buildings; but there was also another reason</line>
<line>for establishing this industry. There was no brickyard in the town, and</line>
<line>in addition to our own needs there was a demand for bricks in the</line>
<line>general market.</line>

<line>I had always sympathized with the “Children of Israel,” in their task</line>
<line>of “making bricks without straw,” but ours was the task of making</line>
<line>bricks with no money and no experience.</line>

<line>In the first place, the work was hard and dirty, and it was difficult</line>
<line>to get the students to help. When it came to brickmaking, their</line>
<line>distaste for manual labour in connection with book education became</line>
<line>especially manifest. It was not a pleasant task for one to stand in the</line>
<line>mud-pit for hours, with the mud up to his knees. More than one man</line>
<line>became disgusted and left the school.</line>

<line>We tried several locations before we opened up a pit that furnished</line>
<line>brick clay. I had always supposed that brickmaking was very simple, but</line>
<line>I soon found out by bitter experience that it required special skill</line>
<line>and knowledge, particularly in the burning of the bricks. After a good</line>
<line>deal of effort we moulded about twenty-five thousand bricks, and put</line>
<line>them into a kiln to be burned. This kiln turned out to be a failure,</line>
<line>because it was not properly constructed or properly burned. We began at</line>
<line>once, however, on a second kiln. This, for some reason, also proved a</line>
<line>failure. The failure of this kiln made it still more difficult to get</line>
<line>the students to take part in the work. Several of the teachers,</line>
<line>however, who had been trained in the industries at Hampton, volunteered</line>
<line>their services, and in some way we succeeded in getting a third kiln</line>
<line>ready for burning. The burning of a kiln required about a week. Toward</line>
<line>the latter part of the week, when it seemed as if we were going to have</line>
<line>a good many thousand bricks in a few hours, in the middle of the night</line>
<line>the kiln fell. For the third time we had failed.</line>

<line>The failure of this last kiln left me without a single dollar with</line>
<line>which to make another experiment. Most of the teachers advised the</line>
<line>abandoning of the effort to make bricks. In the midst of my troubles I</line>
<line>thought of a watch which had come into my possession years before. I</line>
<line>took the watch to the city of Montgomery, which was not far distant,</line>
<line>and placed it in a pawn-shop. I secured cash upon it to the amount of</line>
<line>fifteen dollars, with which to renew the brickmaking experiment. I</line>
<line>returned to Tuskegee, and, with the help of the fifteen dollars,</line>
<line>rallied our rather demoralized and discouraged forces and began a</line>
<line>fourth attempt to make bricks. This time, I am glad to say, we were</line>
<line>successful. Before I got hold of any money, the time-limit on my watch</line>
<line>had expired, and I have never seen it since; but I have never regretted</line>
<line>the loss of it.</line>

<line>Brickmaking has now become such an important industry at the school</line>
<line>that last season our students manufactured twelve hundred thousand of</line>
<line>first-class bricks, of a quality suitable to be sold in any market.</line>
<line>Aside from this, scores of young men have mastered the brickmaking</line>
<line>trade—both the making of bricks by hand and by machinery—and are now</line>
<line>engaged in this industry in many parts of the South.</line>

<line>The making of these bricks taught me an important lesson in regard to</line>
<line>the relations of the two races in the South. Many white people who had</line>
<line>had no contact with the school, and perhaps no sympathy with it, came</line>
<line>to us to buy bricks because they found out that ours were good bricks.</line>
<line>They discovered that we were supplying a real want in the community.</line>
<line>The making of these bricks caused many of the white residents of the</line>
<line>neighbourhood to begin to feel that the education of the Negro was not</line>
<line>making him worthless, but that in educating our students we were adding</line>
<line>something to the wealth and comfort of the community. As the people of</line>
<line>the neighbourhood came to us to buy bricks, we got acquainted with</line>
<line>them; they traded with us and we with them. Our business interests</line>
<line>became intermingled. We had something which they wanted; they had</line>
<line>something which we wanted. This, in a large measure, helped to lay the</line>
<line>foundation for the pleasant relations that have continued to exist</line>
<line>between us and the white people in that section, and which now extend</line>
<line>throughout the South.</line>

<line>Wherever one of our brickmakers has gone in the South, we find that he</line>
<line>has something to contribute to the well-being of the community into</line>
<line>which he has gone; something that has made the community feel that, in</line>
<line>a degree, it is indebted to him, and perhaps, to a certain extent,</line>
<line>dependent upon him. In this way pleasant relations between the races</line>
<line>have been stimulated.</line>

<line>My experience is that there is something in human nature which always</line>
<line>makes an individual recognize and reward merit, no matter under what</line>
<line>colour of skin merit is found. I have found, too, that it is the</line>
<line>visible, the tangible, that goes a long ways in softening prejudices.</line>
<line>The actual sight of a first-class house that a Negro has built is ten</line>
<line>times more potent than pages of discussion about a house that he ought</line>
<line>to build, or perhaps could build.</line>

<line>The same principle of industrial education has been carried out in the</line>
<line>building of our own wagons, carts, and buggies, from the first. We now</line>
<line>own and use on our farm and about the school dozens of these vehicles,</line>
<line>and every one of them has been built by the hands of the students.</line>
<line>Aside from this, we help supply the local market with these vehicles.</line>
<line>The supplying of them to the people in the community has had the same</line>
<line>effect as the supplying of bricks, and the man who learns at Tuskegee</line>
<line>to build and repair wagons and carts is regarded as a benefactor by</line>
<line>both races in the community where he goes. The people with whom he</line>
<line>lives and works are going to think twice before they part with such a</line>
<line>man.</line>

<line>The individual who can do something that the world wants done will, in</line>
<line>the end, make his way regardless of race. One man may go into a</line>
<line>community prepared to supply the people there with an analysis of Greek</line>
<line>sentences. The community may not at the time be prepared for, or feel</line>
<line>the need of, Greek analysis, but it may feel its need of bricks and</line>
<line>houses and wagons. If the man can supply the need for those, then, it</line>
<line>will lead eventually to a demand for the first product, and with the</line>
<line>demand will come the ability to appreciate it and to profit by it.</line>

<line>About the time that we succeeded in burning our first kiln of bricks we</line>
<line>began facing in an emphasized form the objection of the students to</line>
<line>being taught to work. By this time it had gotten to be pretty well</line>
<line>advertised throughout the state that every student who came to</line>
<line>Tuskegee, no matter what his financial ability might be, must learn</line>
<line>some industry. Quite a number of letters came from parents protesting</line>
<line>against their children engaging in labour while they were in the</line>
<line>school. Other parents came to the school to protest in person. Most of</line>
<line>the new students brought a written or a verbal request from their</line>
<line>parents to the effect that they wanted their children taught nothing</line>
<line>but books. The more books, the larger they were, and the longer the</line>
<line>titles printed upon them, the better pleased the students and their</line>
<line>parents seemed to be.</line>

<line>I gave little heed to these protests, except that I lost no opportunity</line>
<line>to go into as many parts of the state as I could, for the purpose of</line>
<line>speaking to the parents, and showing them the value of industrial</line>
<line>education. Besides, I talked to the students constantly on the subject.</line>
<line>Notwithstanding the unpopularity of industrial work, the school</line>
<line>continued to increase in numbers to such an extent that by the middle</line>
<line>of the second year there was an attendance of about one hundred and</line>
<line>fifty, representing almost all parts of the state of Alabama, and</line>
<line>including a few from other states.</line>

<line>In the summer of 1882 Miss Davidson and I both went North and engaged</line>
<line>in the work of raising funds for the completion of our new building. On</line>
<line>my way North I stopped in New York to try to get a letter of</line>
<line>recommendation from an officer of a missionary organization who had</line>
<line>become somewhat acquainted with me a few years previous. This man not</line>
<line>only refused to give me the letter, but advised me most earnestly to go</line>
<line>back home at once, and not make any attempt to get money, for he was</line>
<line>quite sure that I would never get more than enough to pay my travelling</line>
<line>expenses. I thanked him for his advice, and proceeded on my journey.</line>

<line>The first place I went to in the North, was Northampton, Mass., where I</line>
<line>spent nearly a half-day in looking for a coloured family with whom I</line>
<line>could board, never dreaming that any hotel would admit me. I was</line>
<line>greatly surprised when I found that I would have no trouble in being</line>
<line>accommodated at a hotel.</line>

<line>We were successful in getting money enough so that on Thanksgiving Day</line>
<line>of that year we held our first service in the chapel of Porter Hall,</line>
<line>although the building was not completed.</line>

<line>In looking about for some one to preach the Thanksgiving sermon, I</line>
<line>found one of the rarest men that it has ever been my privilege to know.</line>
<line>This was the Rev. Robert C. Bedford, a white man from Wisconsin, who</line>
<line>was then pastor of a little coloured Congregational church in</line>
<line>Montgomery, Ala. Before going to Montgomery to look for some one to</line>
<line>preach this sermon I had never heard of Mr. Bedford. He had never heard</line>
<line>of me. He gladly consented to come to Tuskegee and hold the</line>
<line>Thanksgiving service. It was the first service of the kind that the</line>
<line>coloured people there had ever observed, and what a deep interest they</line>
<line>manifested in it! The sight of the new building made it a day of</line>
<line>Thanksgiving for them never to be forgotten.</line>

<line>Mr. Bedford consented to become one of the trustees of the school, and</line>
<line>in that capacity, and as a worker for it, he has been connected with it</line>
<line>for eighteen years. During this time he has borne the school upon his</line>
<line>heart night and day, and is never so happy as when he is performing</line>
<line>some service, no matter how humble, for it. He completely obliterates</line>
<line>himself in everything, and looks only for permission to serve where</line>
<line>service is most disagreeable, and where others would not be attracted.</line>
<line>In all my relations with him he has seemed to me to approach as nearly</line>
<line>to the spirit of the Master as almost any man I ever met.</line>

<line>A little later there came into the service of the school another man,</line>
<line>quite young at the time, and fresh from Hampton, without whose service</line>
<line>the school never could have become what it is. This was Mr. Warren</line>
<line>Logan, who now for seventeen years has been the treasurer of the</line>
<line>Institute, and the acting principal during my absence. He has always</line>
<line>shown a degree of unselfishness and an amount of business tact, coupled</line>
<line>with a clear judgment, that has kept the school in good condition no</line>
<line>matter how long I have been absent from it. During all the financial</line>
<line>stress through which the school has passed, his patience and faith in</line>
<line>our ultimate success have not left him.</line>

<line>As soon as our first building was near enough to completion so that we</line>
<line>could occupy a portion of it—which was near the middle of the second</line>
<line>year of the school—we opened a boarding department. Students had begun</line>
<line>coming from quite a distance, and in such increasing numbers that we</line>
<line>felt more and more that we were merely skimming over the surface, in</line>
<line>that we were not getting hold of the students in their home life.</line>

<line>We had nothing but the students and their appetites with which to begin</line>
<line>a boarding department. No provision had been made in the new building</line>
<line>for a kitchen and dining room; but we discovered that by digging out a</line>
<line>large amount of earth from under the building we could make a partially</line>
<line>lighted basement room that could be used for a kitchen and dining room.</line>
<line>Again I called on the students to volunteer for work, this time to</line>
<line>assist in digging out the basement. This they did, and in a few weeks</line>
<line>we had a place to cook and eat in, although it was very rough and</line>
<line>uncomfortable. Any one seeing the place now would never believe that it</line>
<line>was once used for a dining room.</line>

<line>The most serious problem, though, was to get the boarding department</line>
<line>started off in running order, with nothing to do with in the way of</line>
<line>furniture, and with no money with which to buy anything. The merchants</line>
<line>in the town would let us have what food we wanted on credit. In fact,</line>
<line>in those earlier years I was constantly embarrassed because people</line>
<line>seemed to have more faith in me than I had in myself. It was pretty</line>
<line>hard to cook, however, without stoves, and awkward to eat without</line>
<line>dishes. At first the cooking was done out-of-doors, in the</line>
<line>old-fashioned, primitive style, in pots and skillets placed over a</line>
<line>fire. Some of the carpenters’ benches that had been used in the</line>
<line>construction of the building were utilized for tables. As for dishes,</line>
<line>there were too few to make it worth while to spend time in describing</line>
<line>them.</line>

<line>No one connected with the boarding department seemed to have any idea</line>
<line>that meals must be served at certain fixed and regular hours, and this</line>
<line>was a source of great worry. Everything was so out of joint and so</line>
<line>inconvenient that I feel safe in saying that for the first two weeks</line>
<line>something was wrong at every meal. Either the meat was not done or had</line>
<line>been burnt, or the salt had been left out of the bread, or the tea had</line>
<line>been forgotten.</line>

<line>Early one morning I was standing near the dining-room door listening to</line>
<line>the complaints of the students. The complaints that morning were</line>
<line>especially emphatic and numerous, because the whole breakfast had been</line>
<line>a failure. One of the girls who had failed to get any breakfast came</line>
<line>out and went to the well to draw some water to drink and take the place</line>
<line>of the breakfast which she had not been able to get. When she reached</line>
<line>the well, she found that the rope was broken and that she could get no</line>
<line>water. She turned from the well and said, in the most discouraged tone,</line>
<line>not knowing that I was where I could hear her, “We can’t even get water</line>
<line>to drink at this school.” I think no one remark ever came so near</line>
<line>discouraging me as that one.</line>

<line>At another time, when Mr. Bedford—whom I have already spoken of as one</line>
<line>of our trustees, and a devoted friend of the institution—was visiting</line>
<line>the school, he was given a bedroom immediately over the dining room.</line>
<line>Early in the morning he was awakened by a rather animated discussion</line>
<line>between two boys in the dining room below. The discussion was over the</line>
<line>question as to whose turn it was to use the coffee-cup that morning.</line>
<line>One boy won the case by proving that for three mornings he had not had</line>
<line>an opportunity to use the cup at all.</line>

<line>But gradually, with patience and hard work, we brought order out of</line>
<line>chaos, just as will be true of any problem if we stick to it with</line>
<line>patience and wisdom and earnest effort.</line>

<line>As I look back now over that part of our struggle, I am glad to see</line>
<line>that we had it. I am glad that we endured all those discomforts and</line>
<line>inconveniences. I am glad that our students had to dig out the place</line>
<line>for their kitchen and dining room. I am glad that our first</line>
<line>boarding-place was in the dismal, ill-lighted, and damp basement. Had</line>
<line>we started in a fine, attractive, convenient room, I fear we would have</line>
<line>“lost our heads” and become “stuck up.” It means a great deal, I think,</line>
<line>to start off on a foundation which one has made for one’s self.</line>

<line>When our old students return to Tuskegee now, as they often do, and go</line>
<line>into our large, beautiful, well-ventilated, and well-lighted dining</line>
<line>room, and see tempting, well-cooked food—largely grown by the students</line>
<line>themselves—and see tables, neat tablecloths and napkins, and vases of</line>
<line>flowers upon the tables, and hear singing birds, and note that each</line>
<line>meal is served exactly upon the minute, with no disorder, and with</line>
<line>almost no complaint coming from the hundreds that now fill our dining</line>
<line>room, they, too, often say to me that they are glad that we started as</line>
<line>we did, and built ourselves up year by year, by a slow and natural</line>
<line>process of growth.</line>




</chapter>

<chapter>
<title>Chapter XI. Making Their Beds Before They Could Lie On Them</title>


<line>A little later in the history of the school we had a visit from General</line>
<line>J.F.B. Marshall, the Treasurer of the Hampton Institute, who had had</line>
<line>faith enough to lend us the first two hundred and fifty dollars with</line>
<line>which to make a payment down on the farm. He remained with us a week,</line>
<line>and made a careful inspection of everything. He seemed well pleased</line>
<line>with our progress, and wrote back interesting and encouraging reports</line>
<line>to Hampton. A little later Miss Mary F. Mackie, the teacher who had</line>
<line>given me the “sweeping” examination when I entered Hampton, came to see</line>
<line>us, and still later General Armstrong himself came.</line>

<line>At the time of the visits of these Hampton friends the number of</line>
<line>teachers at Tuskegee had increased considerably, and the most of the</line>
<line>new teachers were graduates of the Hampton Institute. We gave our</line>
<line>Hampton friends, especially General Armstrong, a cordial welcome. They</line>
<line>were all surprised and pleased at the rapid progress that the school</line>
<line>had made within so short a time. The coloured people from miles around</line>
<line>came to the school to get a look at General Armstrong, about whom they</line>
<line>had heard so much. The General was not only welcomed by the members of</line>
<line>my own race, but by the Southern white people as well.</line>

<line>This first visit which General Armstrong made to Tuskegee gave me an</line>
<line>opportunity to get an insight into his character such as I had not</line>
<line>before had. I refer to his interest in the Southern white people.</line>
<line>Before this I had had the thought that General Armstrong, having fought</line>
<line>the Southern white man, rather cherished a feeling of bitterness toward</line>
<line>the white South, and was interested in helping only the coloured man</line>
<line>there. But this visit convinced me that I did not know the greatness</line>
<line>and the generosity of the man. I soon learned, by his visits to the</line>
<line>Southern white people, and from his conversations with them, that he</line>
<line>was as anxious about the prosperity and the happiness of the white race</line>
<line>as the black. He cherished no bitterness against the South, and was</line>
<line>happy when an opportunity offered for manifesting his sympathy. In all</line>
<line>my acquaintance with General Armstrong I never heard him speak, in</line>
<line>public or in private, a single bitter word against the white man in the</line>
<line>South. From his example in this respect I learned the lesson that great</line>
<line>men cultivate love, and that only little men cherish a spirit of</line>
<line>hatred. I learned that assistance given to the weak makes the one who</line>
<line>gives it strong; and that oppression of the unfortunate makes one weak.</line>

<line>It is now long ago that I learned this lesson from General Armstrong,</line>
<line>and resolved that I would permit no man, no matter what his colour</line>
<line>might be, to narrow and degrade my soul by making me hate him. With</line>
<line>God’s help, I believe that I have completely rid myself of any ill</line>
<line>feeling toward the Southern white man for any wrong that he may have</line>
<line>inflicted upon my race. I am made to feel just as happy now when I am</line>
<line>rendering service to Southern white men as when the service is rendered</line>
<line>to a member of my own race. I pity from the bottom of my heart any</line>
<line>individual who is so unfortunate as to get into the habit of holding</line>
<line>race prejudice.</line>

<line>The more I consider the subject, the more strongly I am convinced that</line>
<line>the most harmful effect of the practice to which the people in certain</line>
<line>sections of the South have felt themselves compelled to resort, in</line>
<line>order to get rid of the force of the Negroes’ ballot, is not wholly in</line>
<line>the wrong done to the Negro, but in the permanent injury to the morals</line>
<line>of the white man. The wrong to the Negro is temporary, but to the</line>
<line>morals of the white man the injury is permanent. I have noted time and</line>
<line>time again that when an individual perjures himself in order to break</line>
<line>the force of the black man’s ballot, he soon learns to practise</line>
<line>dishonesty in other relations of life, not only where the Negro is</line>
<line>concerned, but equally so where a white man is concerned. The white man</line>
<line>who begins by cheating a Negro usually ends by cheating a white man.</line>
<line>The white man who begins to break the law by lynching a Negro soon</line>
<line>yields to the temptation to lynch a white man. All this, it seems to</line>
<line>me, makes it important that the whole Nation lend a hand in trying to</line>
<line>lift the burden of ignorance from the South.</line>

<line>Another thing that is becoming more apparent each year in the</line>
<line>development of education in the South is the influence of General</line>
<line>Armstrong’s idea of education; and this not upon the blacks alone, but</line>
<line>upon the whites also. At the present time there is almost no Southern</line>
<line>state that is not putting forth efforts in the direction of securing</line>
<line>industrial education for its white boys and girls, and in most cases it</line>
<line>is easy to trace the history of these efforts back to General</line>
<line>Armstrong.</line>

<line>Soon after the opening of our humble boarding department students began</line>
<line>coming to us in still larger numbers. For weeks we not only had to</line>
<line>contend with the difficulty of providing board, with no money, but also</line>
<line>with that of providing sleeping accommodations. For this purpose we</line>
<line>rented a number of cabins near the school. These cabins were in a</line>
<line>dilapidated condition, and during the winter months the students who</line>
<line>occupied them necessarily suffered from the cold. We charge the</line>
<line>students eight dollars a month—all they were able to pay—for their</line>
<line>board. This included, besides board, room, fuel, and washing. We also</line>
<line>gave the students credit on their board bills for all the work which</line>
<line>they did for the school which was of any value to the institution. The</line>
<line>cost of tuition, which was fifty dollars a year for each student, we</line>
<line>had to secure then, as now, wherever we could.</line>

<line>This small charge in cash gave us no capital with which to start a</line>
<line>boarding department. The weather during the second winter of our work</line>
<line>was very cold. We were not able to provide enough bed-clothes to keep</line>
<line>the students warm. In fact, for some time we were not able to provide,</line>
<line>except in a few cases, bedsteads and mattresses of any kind. During the</line>
<line>coldest nights I was so troubled about the discomfort of the students</line>
<line>that I could not sleep myself. I recall that on several occasions I</line>
<line>went in the middle of the night to the shanties occupied by the young</line>
<line>men, for the purpose of confronting them. Often I found some of them</line>
<line>sitting huddled around a fire, with the one blanket which we had been</line>
<line>able to provide wrapped around them, trying in this way to keep warm.</line>
<line>During the whole night some of them did not attempt to lie down. One</line>
<line>morning, when the night previous had been unusually cold, I asked those</line>
<line>of the students in the chapel who thought that they had been</line>
<line>frostbitten during the night to raise their hands. Three hands went up.</line>
<line>Notwithstanding these experiences, there was almost no complaining on</line>
<line>the part of the students. They knew that we were doing the best that we</line>
<line>could for them. They were happy in the privilege of being permitted to</line>
<line>enjoy any kind of opportunity that would enable them to improve their</line>
<line>condition. They were constantly asking what they might do to lighten</line>
<line>the burdens of the teachers.</line>

<line>I have heard it stated more than once, both in the North and in the</line>
<line>South, that coloured people would not obey and respect each other when</line>
<line>one member of the race is placed in a position of authority over</line>
<line>others. In regard to this general belief and these statements, I can</line>
<line>say that during the nineteen years of my experience at Tuskegee I</line>
<line>never, either by word or act, have been treated with disrespect by any</line>
<line>student or officer connected with the institution. On the other hand, I</line>
<line>am constantly embarrassed by the many acts of thoughtful kindness. The</line>
<line>students do not seem to want to see me carry a large book or a satchel</line>
<line>or any kind of a burden through the grounds. In such cases more than</line>
<line>one always offers to relieve me. I almost never go out of my office</line>
<line>when the rain is falling that some student does not come to my side</line>
<line>with an umbrella and ask to be allowed to hold it over me.</line>

<line>While writing upon this subject, it is a pleasure for me to add that in</line>
<line>all my contact with the white people of the South I have never received</line>
<line>a single personal insult. The white people in and near Tuskegee, to an</line>
<line>especial degree, seem to count it as a privilege to show me all the</line>
<line>respect within their power, and often go out of their way to do this.</line>

<line>Not very long ago I was making a journey between Dallas (Texas) and</line>
<line>Houston. In some way it became known in advance that I was on the</line>
<line>train. At nearly every station at which the train stopped, numbers of</line>
<line>white people, including in most cases of the officials of the town,</line>
<line>came aboard and introduced themselves and thanked me heartily for the</line>
<line>work that I was trying to do for the South.</line>

<line>On another occasion, when I was making a trip from Augusta, Georgia, to</line>
<line>Atlanta, being rather tired from much travel, I rode in a Pullman</line>
<line>sleeper. When I went into the car, I found there two ladies from Boston</line>
<line>whom I knew well. These good ladies were perfectly ignorant, it seems,</line>
<line>of the customs of the South, and in the goodness of their hearts</line>
<line>insisted that I take a seat with them in their section. After some</line>
<line>hesitation I consented. I had been there but a few minutes when one of</line>
<line>them, without my knowledge, ordered supper to be served for the three</line>
<line>of us. This embarrassed me still further. The car was full of Southern</line>
<line>white men, most of whom had their eyes on our party. When I found that</line>
<line>supper had been ordered, I tried to contrive some excuse that would</line>
<line>permit me to leave the section, but the ladies insisted that I must eat</line>
<line>with them. I finally settled back in my seat with a sigh, and said to</line>
<line>myself, “I am in for it now, sure.”</line>

<line>To add further to the embarrassment of the situation, soon after the</line>
<line>supper was placed on the table one of the ladies remembered that she</line>
<line>had in her satchel a special kind of tea which she wished served, and</line>
<line>as she said she felt quite sure the porter did not know how to brew it</line>
<line>properly, she insisted upon getting up and preparing and serving it</line>
<line>herself. At last the meal was over; and it seemed the longest one that</line>
<line>I had ever eaten. When we were through, I decided to get myself out of</line>
<line>the embarrassing situation and go to the smoking-room, where most of</line>
<line>the men were by that time, to see how the land lay. In the meantime,</line>
<line>however, it had become known in some way throughout the car who I was.</line>
<line>When I went into the smoking-room I was never more surprised in my life</line>
<line>than when each man, nearly every one of them a citizen of Georgia, came</line>
<line>up and introduced himself to me and thanked me earnestly for the work</line>
<line>that I was trying to do for the whole South. This was not flattery,</line>
<line>because each one of these individuals knew that he had nothing to gain</line>
<line>by trying to flatter me.</line>

<line>From the first I have sought to impress the students with the idea that</line>
<line>Tuskegee is not my institution, or that of the officers, but that it is</line>
<line>their institution, and that they have as much interest in it as any of</line>
<line>the trustees or instructors. I have further sought to have them feel</line>
<line>that I am at the institution as their friend and adviser, and not as</line>
<line>their overseer. It has been my aim to have them speak with directness</line>
<line>and frankness about anything that concerns the life of the school. Two</line>
<line>or three times a year I ask the students to write me a letter</line>
<line>criticising or making complaints or suggestions about anything</line>
<line>connected with the institution. When this is not done, I have them meet</line>
<line>me in the chapel for a heart-to-heart talk about the conduct of the</line>
<line>school. There are no meetings with our students that I enjoy more than</line>
<line>these, and none are more helpful to me in planning for the future.</line>
<line>These meetings, it seems to me, enable me to get at the very heart of</line>
<line>all that concerns the school. Few things help an individual more than</line>
<line>to place responsibility upon him, and to let him know that you trust</line>
<line>him. When I have read of labour troubles between employers and</line>
<line>employees, I have often thought that many strikes and similar</line>
<line>disturbances might be avoided if the employers would cultivate the</line>
<line>habit of getting nearer to their employees, of consulting and advising</line>
<line>with them, and letting them feel that the interests of the two are the</line>
<line>same. Every individual responds to confidence, and this is not more</line>
<line>true of any race than of the Negroes. Let them once understand that you</line>
<line>are unselfishly interested in them, and you can lead them to any</line>
<line>extent.</line>

<line>It was my aim from the first at Tuskegee to not only have the buildings</line>
<line>erected by the students themselves, but to have them make their own</line>
<line>furniture as far as was possible. I now marvel at the patience of the</line>
<line>students while sleeping upon the floor while waiting for some kind of a</line>
<line>bedstead to be constructed, or at their sleeping without any kind of a</line>
<line>mattress while waiting for something that looked like a mattress to be</line>
<line>made.</line>

<line>In the early days we had very few students who had been used to</line>
<line>handling carpenters’ tools, and the bedsteads made by the students then</line>
<line>were very rough and very weak. Not unfrequently when I went into the</line>
<line>students’ rooms in the morning I would find at least two bedsteads</line>
<line>lying about on the floor. The problem of providing mattresses was a</line>
<line>difficult one to solve. We finally mastered this, however, by getting</line>
<line>some cheap cloth and sewing pieces of this together as to make large</line>
<line>bags. These bags we filled with the pine straw—or, as it is sometimes</line>
<line>called, pine needles—which we secured from the forests near by. I am</line>
<line>glad to say that the industry of mattress-making has grown steadily</line>
<line>since then, and has been improved to such an extent that at the present</line>
<line>time it is an important branch of the work which is taught</line>
<line>systematically to a number of our girls, and that the mattresses that</line>
<line>now come out of the mattress-shop at Tuskegee are about as good as</line>
<line>those bought in the average store. For some time after the opening of</line>
<line>the boarding department we had no chairs in the students’ bedrooms or</line>
<line>in the dining rooms. Instead of chairs we used stools which the</line>
<line>students constructed by nailing together three pieces of rough board.</line>
<line>As a rule, the furniture in the students’ rooms during the early days</line>
<line>of the school consisted of a bed, some stools, and sometimes a rough</line>
<line>table made by the students. The plan of having the students make the</line>
<line>furniture is still followed, but the number of pieces in a room has</line>
<line>been increased, and the workmanship has so improved that little fault</line>
<line>can be found with the articles now. One thing that I have always</line>
<line>insisted upon at Tuskegee is that everywhere there should be absolute</line>
<line>cleanliness. Over and over again the students were reminded in those</line>
<line>first years—and are reminded now—that people would excuse us for our</line>
<line>poverty, for our lack of comforts and conveniences, but that they would</line>
<line>not excuse us for dirt.</line>

<line>Another thing that has been insisted upon at the school is the use of</line>
<line>the tooth-brush. “The gospel of the tooth-brush,” as General Armstrong</line>
<line>used to call it, is part of our creed at Tuskegee. No student is</line>
<line>permitted to retain who does not keep and use a tooth-brush. Several</line>
<line>times, in recent years, students have come to us who brought with them</line>
<line>almost no other article except a tooth-brush. They had heard from the</line>
<line>lips of other students about our insisting upon the use of this, and</line>
<line>so, to make a good impression, they brought at least a tooth-brush with</line>
<line>them. I remember that one morning, not long ago, I went with the lady</line>
<line>principal on her usual morning tour of inspection of the girls’ rooms.</line>
<line>We found one room that contained three girls who had recently arrived</line>
<line>at the school. When I asked them if they had tooth-brushes, one of the</line>
<line>girls replied, pointing to a brush: “Yes, sir. That is our brush. We</line>
<line>bought it together, yesterday.” It did not take them long to learn a</line>
<line>different lesson.</line>

<line>It has been interesting to note the effect that the use of the</line>
<line>tooth-brush has had in bringing about a higher degree of civilization</line>
<line>among the students. With few exceptions, I have noticed that, if we can</line>
<line>get a student to the point where, when the first or second tooth-brush</line>
<line>disappears, he of his own motion buys another, I have not been</line>
<line>disappointed in the future of that individual. Absolute cleanliness of</line>
<line>the body has been insisted upon from the first. The students have been</line>
<line>taught to bathe as regularly as to take their meals. This lesson we</line>
<line>began teaching before we had anything in the shape of a bath-house.</line>
<line>Most of the students came from plantation districts, and often we had</line>
<line>to teach them how to sleep at night; that is, whether between the two</line>
<line>sheets—after we got to the point where we could provide them two</line>
<line>sheets—or under both of them. Naturally I found it difficult to teach</line>
<line>them to sleep between two sheets when we were able to supply but one.</line>
<line>The importance of the use of the night-gown received the same</line>
<line>attention.</line>

<line>For a long time one of the most difficult tasks was to teach the</line>
<line>students that all the buttons were to be kept on their clothes, and</line>
<line>that there must be no torn places or grease-spots. This lesson, I am</line>
<line>pleased to be able to say, has been so thoroughly learned and so</line>
<line>faithfully handed down from year to year by one set of students to</line>
<line>another that often at the present time, when the students march out of</line>
<line>the chapel in the evening and their dress is inspected, as it is every</line>
<line>night, not one button is found to be missing.</line>




</chapter>

<chapter>
<title>Chapter XII. Raising Money</title>


<line>When we opened our boarding department, we provided rooms in the attic</line>
<line>of Porter Hall, our first building, for a number of girls. But the</line>
<line>number of students, of both sexes, continued to increase. We could find</line>
<line>rooms outside the school grounds for many of the young men, but the</line>
<line>girls we did not care to expose in this way. Very soon the problem of</line>
<line>providing more rooms for the girls, as well as a larger boarding</line>
<line>department for all the students, grew serious. As a result, we finally</line>
<line>decided to undertake the construction of a still larger building—a</line>
<line>building that would contain rooms for the girls and boarding</line>
<line>accommodations for all.</line>

<line>After having had a preliminary sketch of the needed building made, we</line>
<line>found that it would cost about ten thousand dollars. We had no money</line>
<line>whatever with which to begin; still we decided to give the needed</line>
<line>building a name. We knew we could name it, even though we were in doubt</line>
<line>about our ability to secure the means for its construction. We decided</line>
<line>to call the proposed building Alabama Hall, in honour of the state in</line>
<line>which we were labouring. Again Miss Davidson began making efforts to</line>
<line>enlist the interest and help of the coloured and white people in and</line>
<line>near Tuskegee. They responded willingly, in proportion to their means.</line>
<line>The students, as in the case of our first building, Porter Hall, began</line>
<line>digging out the dirt in order to allow the laying of the foundations.</line>

<line>When we seemed at the end of our resources, so far as securing money</line>
<line>was concerned, something occurred which showed the greatness of General</line>
<line>Armstrong—something which proved how far he was above the ordinary</line>
<line>individual. When we were in the midst of great anxiety as to where and</line>
<line>how we were to get funds for the new building, I received a telegram</line>
<line>from General Armstrong asking me if I could spend a month travelling</line>
<line>with him through the North, and asking me, if I could do so, to come to</line>
<line>Hampton at once. Of course I accepted General Armstrong’s invitation,</line>
<line>and went to Hampton immediately. On arriving there I found that the</line>
<line>General had decided to take a quartette of singers through the North,</line>
<line>and hold meetings for a month in important cities, at which meetings he</line>
<line>and I were to speak. Imagine my surprise when the General told me,</line>
<line>further, that these meetings were to be held, not in the interests of</line>
<line>Hampton, but in the interests of Tuskegee, and that the Hampton</line>
<line>Institute was to be responsible for all the expenses.</line>

<line>Although he never told me so in so many words, I found that General</line>
<line>Armstrong took this method of introducing me to the people of the</line>
<line>North, as well as for the sake of securing some immediate funds to be</line>
<line>used in the erection of Alabama Hall. A weak and narrow man would have</line>
<line>reasoned that all the money which came to Tuskegee in this way would be</line>
<line>just so much taken from the Hampton Institute; but none of these</line>
<line>selfish or short-sighted feelings ever entered the breast of General</line>
<line>Armstrong. He was too big to be little, too good to be mean. He knew</line>
<line>that the people in the North who gave money gave it for the purpose of</line>
<line>helping the whole cause of Negro civilization, and not merely for the</line>
<line>advancement of any one school. The General knew, too, that the way to</line>
<line>strengthen Hampton was to make it a centre of unselfish power in the</line>
<line>working out of the whole Southern problem.</line>

<line>In regard to the addresses which I was to make in the North, I recall</line>
<line>just one piece of advice which the General gave me. He said: “Give them</line>
<line>an idea for every word.” I think it would be hard to improve upon this</line>
<line>advice; and it might be made to apply to all public speaking. From that</line>
<line>time to the present I have always tried to keep his advice in mind.</line>

<line>Meetings were held in New York, Brooklyn, Boston, Philadelphia, and</line>
<line>other large cities, and at all of these meetings General Armstrong</line>
<line>pleaded, together with myself, for help, not for Hampton, but for</line>
<line>Tuskegee. At these meetings an especial effort was made to secure help</line>
<line>for the building of Alabama Hall, as well as to introduce the school to</line>
<line>the attention of the general public. In both these respects the</line>
<line>meetings proved successful.</line>

<line>After that kindly introduction I began going North alone to secure</line>
<line>funds. During the last fifteen years I have been compelled to spend a</line>
<line>large proportion of my time away from the school, in an effort to</line>
<line>secure money to provide for the growing needs of the institution. In my</line>
<line>efforts to get funds I have had some experiences that may be of</line>
<line>interest to my readers. Time and time again I have been asked, by</line>
<line>people who are trying to secure money for philanthropic purposes, what</line>
<line>rule or rules I followed to secure the interest and help of people who</line>
<line>were able to contribute money to worthy objects. As far as the science</line>
<line>of what is called begging can be reduced to rules, I would say that I</line>
<line>have had but two rules. First, always to do my whole duty regarding</line>
<line>making our work known to individuals and organizations; and, second,</line>
<line>not to worry about the results. This second rule has been the hardest</line>
<line>for me to live up to. When bills are on the eve of falling due, with</line>
<line>not a dollar in hand with which to meet them, it is pretty difficult to</line>
<line>learn not to worry, although I think I am learning more and more each</line>
<line>year that all worry simply consumes, and to no purpose, just so much</line>
<line>physical and mental strength that might otherwise be given to effective</line>
<line>work. After considerable experience in coming into contact with wealthy</line>
<line>and noted men, I have observed that those who have accomplished the</line>
<line>greatest results are those who “keep under the body”; are those who</line>
<line>never grow excited or lose self-control, but are always calm,</line>
<line>self-possessed, patient, and polite. I think that President William</line>
<line>McKinley is the best example of a man of this class that I have ever</line>
<line>seen.</line>

<line>In order to be successful in any kind of undertaking, I think the main</line>
<line>thing is for one to grow to the point where he completely forgets</line>
<line>himself; that is, to lose himself in a great cause. In proportion as</line>
<line>one loses himself in the way, in the same degree does he get the</line>
<line>highest happiness out of his work.</line>

<line>My experience in getting money for Tuskegee has taught me to have no</line>
<line>patience with those people who are always condemning the rich because</line>
<line>they are rich, and because they do not give more to objects of charity.</line>
<line>In the first place, those who are guilty of such sweeping criticisms do</line>
<line>not know how many people would be made poor, and how much suffering</line>
<line>would result, if wealthy people were to part all at once with any large</line>
<line>proportion of their wealth in a way to disorganize and cripple great</line>
<line>business enterprises. Then very few persons have any idea of the large</line>
<line>number of applications for help that rich people are constantly being</line>
<line>flooded with. I know wealthy people who receive as much as twenty calls</line>
<line>a day for help. More than once when I have gone into the offices of</line>
<line>rich men, I have found half a dozen persons waiting to see them, and</line>
<line>all come for the same purpose, that of securing money. And all these</line>
<line>calls in person, to say nothing of the applications received through</line>
<line>the mails. Very few people have any idea of the amount of money given</line>
<line>away by persons who never permit their names to be known. I have often</line>
<line>heard persons condemned for not giving away money, who, to my own</line>
<line>knowledge, were giving away thousands of dollars every year so quietly</line>
<line>that the world knew nothing about it.</line>

<line>As an example of this, there are two ladies in New York, whose names</line>
<line>rarely appear in print, but who, in a quiet way, have given us the</line>
<line>means with which to erect three large and important buildings during</line>
<line>the last eight years. Besides the gift of these buildings, they have</line>
<line>made other generous donations to the school. And they not only help</line>
<line>Tuskegee, but they are constantly seeking opportunities to help other</line>
<line>worthy causes.</line>

<line>Although it has been my privilege to be the medium through which a good</line>
<line>many hundred thousand dollars have been received for the work at</line>
<line>Tuskegee, I have always avoided what the world calls “begging.” I often</line>
<line>tell people that I have never “begged” any money, and that I am not a</line>
<line>“beggar.” My experience and observation have convinced me that</line>
<line>persistent asking outright for money from the rich does not, as a rule,</line>
<line>secure help. I have usually proceeded on the principle that persons who</line>
<line>possess sense enough to earn money have sense enough to know how to</line>
<line>give it away, and that the mere making known of the facts regarding</line>
<line>Tuskegee, and especially the facts regarding the work of the graduates,</line>
<line>has been more effective than outright begging. I think that the</line>
<line>presentation of facts, on a high, dignified plane, is all the begging</line>
<line>that most rich people care for.</line>

<line>While the work of going from door to door and from office to office is</line>
<line>hard, disagreeable, and costly in bodily strength, yet it has some</line>
<line>compensations. Such work gives one a rare opportunity to study human</line>
<line>nature. It also has its compensations in giving one an opportunity to</line>
<line>meet some of the best people in the world—to be more correct, I think I</line>
<line>should say the best people in the world. When one takes a broad survey</line>
<line>of the country, he will find that the most useful and influential</line>
<line>people in it are those who take the deepest interest in institutions</line>
<line>that exist for the purpose of making the world better.</line>

<line>At one time, when I was in Boston, I called at the door of a rather</line>
<line>wealthy lady, and was admitted to the vestibule and sent up my card.</line>
<line>While I was waiting for an answer, her husband came in, and asked me in</line>
<line>the most abrupt manner what I wanted. When I tried to explain the</line>
<line>object of my call, he became still more ungentlemanly in his words and</line>
<line>manner, and finally grew so excited that I left the house without</line>
<line>waiting for a reply from the lady. A few blocks from that house I</line>
<line>called to see a gentleman who received me in the most cordial manner.</line>
<line>He wrote me his check for a generous sum, and then, before I had had an</line>
<line>opportunity to thank him, said: “I am so grateful to you, Mr.</line>
<line>Washington, for giving me the opportunity to help a good cause. It is a</line>
<line>privilege to have a share in it. We in Boston are constantly indebted</line>
<line>to you for doing our work.” My experience in securing money convinces</line>
<line>me that the first type of man is growing more rare all the time, and</line>
<line>that the latter type is increasing; that is, that, more and more, rich</line>
<line>people are coming to regard men and women who apply to them for help</line>
<line>for worthy objects, not as beggars, but as agents for doing their work.</line>

<line>In the city of Boston I have rarely called upon an individual for funds</line>
<line>that I have not been thanked for calling, usually before I could get an</line>
<line>opportunity to thank the donor for the money. In that city the donors</line>
<line>seem to feel, in a large degree, that an honour is being conferred upon</line>
<line>them in their being permitted to give. Nowhere else have I met with, in</line>
<line>so large a measure, this fine and Christlike spirit as in the city of</line>
<line>Boston, although there are many notable instances of it outside that</line>
<line>city. I repeat my belief that the world is growing in the direction of</line>
<line>giving. I repeat that the main rule by which I have been guided in</line>
<line>collecting money is to do my full duty in regard to giving people who</line>
<line>have money an opportunity for help.</line>

<line>In the early years of the Tuskegee school I walked the streets or</line>
<line>travelled country roads in the North for days and days without</line>
<line>receiving a dollar. Often as it happened, when during the week I had</line>
<line>been disappointed in not getting a cent from the very individuals from</line>
<line>whom I most expected help, and when I was almost broken down and</line>
<line>discouraged, that generous help has come from some one who I had had</line>
<line>little idea would give at all.</line>

<line>I recall that on one occasion I obtained information that led me to</line>
<line>believe that a gentleman who lived about two miles out in the country</line>
<line>from Stamford, Conn., might become interested in our efforts at</line>
<line>Tuskegee if our conditions and needs were presented to him. On an</line>
<line>unusually cold and stormy day I walked the two miles to see him. After</line>
<line>some difficulty I succeeded in securing an interview with him. He</line>
<line>listened with some degree of interest to what I had to say, but did not</line>
<line>give me anything. I could not help having the feeling that, in a</line>
<line>measure, the three hours that I had spent in seeing him had been thrown</line>
<line>away. Still, I had followed my usual rule of doing my duty. If I had</line>
<line>not seen him, I should have felt unhappy over neglect of duty.</line>

<line>Two years after this visit a letter came to Tuskegee from this man,</line>
<line>which read like this: “Enclosed I send you a New York draft for ten</line>
<line>thousand dollars, to be used in furtherance of your work. I had placed</line>
<line>this sum in my will for your school, but deem it wiser to give it to</line>
<line>you while I live. I recall with pleasure your visit to me two years</line>
<line>ago.”</line>

<line>I can hardly imagine any occurrence which could have given me more</line>
<line>genuine satisfaction than the receipt of this draft. It was by far the</line>
<line>largest single donation which up to that time the school had ever</line>
<line>received. It came at a time when an unusually long period had passed</line>
<line>since we had received any money. We were in great distress because of</line>
<line>lack of funds, and the nervous strain was tremendous. It is difficult</line>
<line>for me to think of any situation that is more trying on the nerves than</line>
<line>that of conducting a large institution, with heavy obligations to meet,</line>
<line>without knowing where the money is to come from to meet these</line>
<line>obligations from month to month.</line>

<line>In our case I felt a double responsibility, and this made the anxiety</line>
<line>all the more intense. If the institution had been officered by white</line>
<line>persons, and had failed, it would have injured the cause of Negro</line>
<line>education; but I knew that the failure of our institution, officered by</line>
<line>Negroes, would not only mean the loss of a school, but would cause</line>
<line>people, in a large degree, to lose faith in the ability of the entire</line>
<line>race. The receipt of this draft for ten thousand dollars, under all</line>
<line>these circumstances, partially lifted a burden that had been pressing</line>
<line>down upon me for days.</line>

<line>From the beginning of our work to the present I have always had the</line>
<line>feeling, and lose no opportunity to impress our teachers with the same</line>
<line>idea, that the school will always be supported in proportion as the</line>
<line>inside of the institution is kept clean and pure and wholesome.</line>

<line>The first time I ever saw the late Collis P. Huntington, the great</line>
<line>railroad man, he gave me two dollars for our school. The last time I</line>
<line>saw him, which was a few months before he died, he gave me fifty</line>
<line>thousand dollars toward our endowment fund. Between these two gifts</line>
<line>there were others of generous proportions which came every year from</line>
<line>both Mr. and Mrs. Huntington.</line>

<line>Some people may say that it was Tuskegee’s good luck that brought to us</line>
<line>this gift of fifty thousand dollars. No, it was not luck. It was hard</line>
<line>work. Nothing ever comes to me, that is worth having, except as the</line>
<line>result of hard work. When Mr. Huntington gave me the first two dollars,</line>
<line>I did not blame him for not giving me more, but made up my mind that I</line>
<line>was going to convince him by tangible results that we were worthy of</line>
<line>larger gifts. For a dozen years I made a strong effort to convince Mr.</line>
<line>Huntington of the value of our work. I noted that just in proportion as</line>
<line>the usefulness of the school grew, his donations increased. Never did I</line>
<line>meet an individual who took a more kindly and sympathetic interest in</line>
<line>our school than did Mr. Huntington. He not only gave money to us, but</line>
<line>took time in which to advise me, as a father would a son, about the</line>
<line>general conduct of the school.</line>

<line>More than once I have found myself in some pretty tight places while</line>
<line>collecting money in the North. The following incident I have never</line>
<line>related but once before, for the reason that I feared that people would</line>
<line>not believe it. One morning I found myself in Providence, Rhode Island,</line>
<line>without a cent of money with which to buy breakfast. In crossing the</line>
<line>street to see a lady from whom I hoped to get some money, I found a</line>
<line>bright new twenty-five-cent piece in the middle of the street track. I</line>
<line>not only had this twenty-five cents for my breakfast, but within a few</line>
<line>minutes I had a donation from the lady on whom I had started to call.</line>

<line>At one of our Commencements I was bold enough to invite the Rev. E.</line>
<line>Winchester Donald, D.D., rector of Trinity Church, Boston, to preach</line>
<line>the Commencement sermon. As we then had no room large enough to</line>
<line>accommodate all who would be present, the place of meeting was under a</line>
<line>large improvised arbour, built partly of brush and partly of rough</line>
<line>boards. Soon after Dr. Donald had begun speaking, the rain came down in</line>
<line>torrents, and he had to stop, while someone held an umbrella over him.</line>

<line>The boldness of what I had done never dawned upon me until I saw the</line>
<line>picture made by the rector of Trinity Church standing before that large</line>
<line>audience under an old umbrella, waiting for the rain to cease so that</line>
<line>he could go on with his address.</line>

<line>It was not very long before the rain ceased and Dr. Donald finished his</line>
<line>sermon; and an excellent sermon it was, too, in spite of the weather.</line>
<line>After he had gone to his room, and had gotten the wet threads of his</line>
<line>clothes dry, Dr. Donald ventured the remark that a large chapel at</line>
<line>Tuskegee would not be out of place. The next day a letter came from two</line>
<line>ladies who were then travelling in Italy, saying that they had decided</line>
<line>to give us the money for such a chapel as we needed.</line>

<line>A short time ago we received twenty thousand dollars from Mr. Andrew</line>
<line>Carnegie, to be used for the purpose of erecting a new library</line>
<line>building. Our first library and reading-room were in a corner of a</line>
<line>shanty, and the whole thing occupied a space about five by twelve feet.</line>
<line>It required ten years of work before I was able to secure Mr.</line>
<line>Carnegie’s interest and help. The first time I saw him, ten years ago,</line>
<line>he seemed to take but little interest in our school, but I was</line>
<line>determined to show him that we were worthy of his help. After ten years</line>
<line>of hard work I wrote him a letter reading as follows:</line>

<line>December 15, 1900.</line>

<line>Mr. Andrew Carnegie, 5 W. Fifty-first St., New York.</line>

<line>Dear Sir: Complying with the request which you made of me when I saw</line>
<line>you at your residence a few days ago, I now submit in writing an appeal</line>
<line>for a library building for our institution.</line>

<line>We have 1100 students, 86 officers and instructors, together with their</line>
<line>families, and about 200 coloured people living near the school, all of</line>
<line>whom would make use of the library building.</line>

<line>We have over 12,000 books, periodicals, etc., gifts from our friends,</line>
<line>but we have no suitable place for them, and we have no suitable</line>
<line>reading-room.</line>

<line>Our graduates go to work in every section of the South, and whatever</line>
<line>knowledge might be obtained in the library would serve to assist in the</line>
<line>elevation of the whole Negro race.</line>

<line>Such a building as we need could be erected for about $20,000. All of</line>
<line>the work for the building, such as brickmaking, brick-masonry,</line>
<line>carpentry, blacksmithing, etc., would be done by the students. The</line>
<line>money which you would give would not only supply the building, but the</line>
<line>erection of the building would give a large number of students an</line>
<line>opportunity to learn the building trades, and the students would use</line>
<line>the money paid to them to keep themselves in school. I do not believe</line>
<line>that a similar amount of money often could be made go so far in</line>
<line>uplifting a whole race.</line>

<line>If you wish further information, I shall be glad to furnish it.</line>

<line>Yours truly,</line>

<line>Booker T. Washington, Principal.</line>

<line>The next mail brought back the following reply: “I will be very glad to</line>
<line>pay the bills for the library building as they are incurred, to the</line>
<line>extent of twenty thousand dollars, and I am glad of this opportunity to</line>
<line>show the interest I have in your noble work.”</line>

<line>I have found that strict business methods go a long way in securing the</line>
<line>interest of rich people. It has been my constant aim at Tuskegee to</line>
<line>carry out, in our financial and other operations, such business methods</line>
<line>as would be approved of by any New York banking house.</line>

<line>I have spoken of several large gifts to the school; but by far the</line>
<line>greater proportion of the money that has built up the institution has</line>
<line>come in the form of small donations from persons of moderate means. It</line>
<line>is upon these small gifts, which carry with them the interest of</line>
<line>hundreds of donors, that any philanthropic work must depend largely for</line>
<line>its support. In my efforts to get money I have often been surprised at</line>
<line>the patience and deep interest of the ministers, who are besieged on</line>
<line>every hand and at all hours of the day for help. If no other</line>
<line>consideration had convinced me of the value of the Christian life, the</line>
<line>Christlike work which the Church of all denominations in America has</line>
<line>done during the last thirty-five years for the elevation of the black</line>
<line>man would have made me a Christian. In a large degree it has been the</line>
<line>pennies, the nickels, and the dimes which have come from the</line>
<line>Sunday-schools, the Christian Endeavour societies, and the missionary</line>
<line>societies, as well as from the church proper, that have helped to</line>
<line>elevate the Negro at so rapid a rate.</line>

<line>This speaking of small gifts reminds me to say that very few Tuskegee</line>
<line>graduates fail to send us an annual contribution. These contributions</line>
<line>range from twenty-five cents up to ten dollars.</line>

<line>Soon after beginning our third year’s work we were surprised to receive</line>
<line>money from three special sources, and up to the present time we have</line>
<line>continued to receive help from them. First, the State Legislature of</line>
<line>Alabama increased its annual appropriation from two thousand dollars to</line>
<line>three thousand dollars; I might add that still later it increased this</line>
<line>sum to four thousand five hundred dollars a year. The effort to secure</line>
<line>this increase was led by the Hon. M.F. Foster, the member of the</line>
<line>Legislature from Tuskegee. Second, we received one thousand dollars</line>
<line>from the John F. Slater Fund. Our work seemed to please the trustees of</line>
<line>this fund, as they soon began increasing their annual grant. This has</line>
<line>been added to from time to time until at present we receive eleven</line>
<line>thousand dollars annually from the Fund. The other help to which I have</line>
<line>referred came in the shape of an allowance from the Peabody Fund. This</line>
<line>was at first five hundred dollars, but it has since been increased to</line>
<line>fifteen hundred dollars.</line>

<line>The effort to secure help from the Slater and Peabody Funds brought me</line>
<line>into contact with two rare men—men who have had much to do in shaping</line>
<line>the policy for the education of the Negro. I refer to the Hon. J.L.M.</line>
<line>Curry, of Washington, who is the general agent for these two funds, and</line>
<line>Mr. Morris K. Jessup, of New York. Dr. Curry is a native of the South,</line>
<line>an ex-Confederate soldier, yet I do not believe there is any man in the</line>
<line>country who is more deeply interested in the highest welfare of the</line>
<line>Negro than Dr. Curry, or one who is more free from race prejudice. He</line>
<line>enjoys the unique distinction of possessing to an equal degree the</line>
<line>confidence of the black man and the Southern white man. I shall never</line>
<line>forget the first time I met him. It was in Richmond, Va., where he was</line>
<line>then living. I had heard much about him. When I first went into his</line>
<line>presence, trembling because of my youth and inexperience, he took me by</line>
<line>the hand so cordially, and spoke such encouraging words, and gave me</line>
<line>such helpful advice regarding the proper course to pursue, that I came</line>
<line>to know him then, as I have known him ever since, as a high example of</line>
<line>one who is constantly and unselfishly at work for the betterment of</line>
<line>humanity.</line>

<line>Mr. Morris K. Jessup, the treasurer of the Slater Fund, I refer to</line>
<line>because I know of no man of wealth and large and complicated business</line>
<line>responsibilities who gives not only money but his time and thought to</line>
<line>the subject of the proper method of elevating the Negro to the extent</line>
<line>that is true of Mr. Jessup. It is very largely through this effort and</line>
<line>influence that during the last few years the subject of industrial</line>
<line>education has assumed the importance that it has, and been placed on</line>
<line>its present footing.</line>




</chapter>

<chapter>
<title>Chapter XIII. Two Thousand Miles For A Five-Minute Speech</title>


<line>Soon after the opening of our boarding department, quite a number of</line>
<line>students who evidently were worthy, but who were so poor that they did</line>
<line>not have any money to pay even the small charges at the school, began</line>
<line>applying for admission. This class was composed of both men and women.</line>
<line>It was a great trial to refuse admission to these applicants, and in</line>
<line>1884 we established a night-school to accommodate a few of them.</line>

<line>The night-school was organized on a plan similar to the one which I had</line>
<line>helped to establish at Hampton. At first it was composed of about a</line>
<line>dozen students. They were admitted to the night-school only when they</line>
<line>had no money with which to pay any part of their board in the regular</line>
<line>day-school. It was further required that they must work for ten hours</line>
<line>during the day at some trade or industry, and study academic branches</line>
<line>for two hours during the evening. This was the requirement for the</line>
<line>first one or two years of their stay. They were to be paid something</line>
<line>above the cost of their board, with the understanding that all of their</line>
<line>earnings, except a very small part, were to be reserved in the school’s</line>
<line>treasury, to be used for paying their board in the regular day-school</line>
<line>after they had entered that department. The night-school, started in</line>
<line>this manner, has grown until there are at present four hundred and</line>
<line>fifty-seven students enrolled in it alone.</line>

<line>There could hardly be a more severe test of a student’s worth than this</line>
<line>branch of the Institute’s work. It is largely because it furnishes such</line>
<line>a good opportunity to test the backbone of a student that I place such</line>
<line>high value upon our night-school. Any one who is willing to work ten</line>
<line>hours a day at the brick-yard, or in the laundry, through one or two</line>
<line>years, in order that he or she may have the privilege of studying</line>
<line>academic branches for two hours in the evening, has enough bottom to</line>
<line>warrant being further educated.</line>

<line>After the student has left the night-school he enters the day-school,</line>
<line>where he takes academic branches four days in a week, and works at his</line>
<line>trade two days. Besides this he usually works at his trade during the</line>
<line>three summer months. As a rule, after a student has succeeded in going</line>
<line>through the night-school test, he finds a way to finish the regular</line>
<line>course in industrial and academic training. No student, no matter how</line>
<line>much money he may be able to command, is permitted to go through school</line>
<line>without doing manual labour. In fact, the industrial work is now as</line>
<line>popular as the academic branches. Some of the most successful men and</line>
<line>women who have graduated from the institution obtained their start in</line>
<line>the night-school.</line>

<line>While a great deal of stress is laid upon the industrial side of the</line>
<line>work at Tuskegee, we do not neglect or overlook in any degree the</line>
<line>religious and spiritual side. The school is strictly undenominational,</line>
<line>but it is thoroughly Christian, and the spiritual training of the</line>
<line>students is not neglected. Our preaching service, prayer-meetings,</line>
<line>Sunday-school, Christian Endeavour Society, Young Men’s Christian</line>
<line>Association, and various missionary organizations, testify to this.</line>

<line>In 1885, Miss Olivia Davidson, to whom I have already referred as being</line>
<line>largely responsible for the success of the school during its early</line>
<line>history, and I were married. During our married life she continued to</line>
<line>divide her time and strength between our home and the work for the</line>
<line>school. She not only continued to work in the school at Tuskegee, but</line>
<line>also kept up her habit of going North to secure funds. In 1889 she</line>
<line>died, after four years of happy married life and eight years of hard</line>
<line>and happy work for the school. She literally wore herself out in her</line>
<line>never ceasing efforts in behalf of the work that she so dearly loved.</line>
<line>During our married life there were born to us two bright, beautiful</line>
<line>boys, Booker Taliaferro and Ernest Davidson. The older of these,</line>
<line>Booker, has already mastered the brick-maker’s trade at Tuskegee.</line>

<line>I have often been asked how I began the practice of public speaking. In</line>
<line>answer I would say that I never planned to give any large part of my</line>
<line>life to speaking in public. I have always had more of an ambition to</line>
<line>_do_ things than merely to talk _about_ doing them. It seems that when</line>
<line>I went North with General Armstrong to speak at the series of public</line>
<line>meetings to which I have referred, the President of the National</line>
<line>Educational Association, the Hon. Thomas W. Bicknell, was present at</line>
<line>one of those meetings and heard me speak. A few days afterward he sent</line>
<line>me an invitation to deliver an address at the next meeting of the</line>
<line>Educational Association. This meeting was to be held in Madison, Wis. I</line>
<line>accepted the invitation. This was, in a sense, the beginning of my</line>
<line>public-speaking career.</line>

<line>On the evening that I spoke before the Association there must have been</line>
<line>not far from four thousand persons present. Without my knowing it,</line>
<line>there were a large number of people present from Alabama, and some from</line>
<line>the town of Tuskegee. These white people afterward frankly told me that</line>
<line>they went to this meeting expecting to hear the South roundly abused,</line>
<line>but were pleasantly surprised to find that there was no word of abuse</line>
<line>in my address. On the contrary, the South was given credit for all the</line>
<line>praiseworthy things that it had done. A white lady who was teacher in a</line>
<line>college in Tuskegee wrote back to the local paper that she was</line>
<line>gratified, as well as surprised, to note the credit which I gave the</line>
<line>white people of Tuskegee for their help in getting the school started.</line>
<line>This address at Madison was the first that I had delivered that in any</line>
<line>large measure dealt with the general problem of the races. Those who</line>
<line>heard it seemed to be pleased with what I said and with the general</line>
<line>position that I took.</line>

<line>When I first came to Tuskegee, I determined that I would make it my</line>
<line>home, that I would take as much pride in the right actions of the</line>
<line>people of the town as any white man could do, and that I would, at the</line>
<line>same time, deplore the wrong-doing of the people as much as any white</line>
<line>man. I determined never to say anything in a public address in the</line>
<line>North that I would not be willing to say in the South. I early learned</line>
<line>that it is a hard matter to convert an individual by abusing him, and</line>
