<line>that this is more often accomplished by giving credit for all the</line>
<line>praiseworthy actions performed than by calling attention alone to all</line>
<line>the evil done.</line>

<line>While pursuing this policy I have not failed, at the proper time and in</line>
<line>the proper manner, to call attention, in no uncertain terms, to the</line>
<line>wrongs which any part of the South has been guilty of. I have found</line>
<line>that there is a large element in the South that is quick to respond to</line>
<line>straightforward, honest criticism of any wrong policy. As a rule, the</line>
<line>place to criticise the South, when criticism is necessary, is in the</line>
<line>South—not in Boston. A Boston man who came to Alabama to criticise</line>
<line>Boston would not effect so much good, I think, as one who had his word</line>
<line>of criticism to say in Boston.</line>

<line>In this address at Madison I took the ground that the policy to be</line>
<line>pursued with references to the races was, by every honourable means, to</line>
<line>bring them together and to encourage the cultivation of friendly</line>
<line>relations, instead of doing that which would embitter. I further</line>
<line>contended that, in relation to his vote, the Negro should more and more</line>
<line>consider the interests of the community in which he lived, rather than</line>
<line>seek alone to please some one who lived a thousand miles away from him</line>
<line>and from his interests.</line>

<line>In this address I said that the whole future of the Negro rested</line>
<line>largely upon the question as to whether or not he should make himself,</line>
<line>through his skill, intelligence, and character, of such undeniable</line>
<line>value to the community in which he lived that the community could not</line>
<line>dispense with his presence. I said that any individual who learned to</line>
<line>do something better than anybody else—learned to do a common thing in</line>
<line>an uncommon manner—had solved his problem, regardless of the colour of</line>
<line>his skin, and that in proportion as the Negro learned to produce what</line>
<line>other people wanted and must have, in the same proportion would he be</line>
<line>respected.</line>

<line>I spoke of an instance where one of our graduates had produced two</line>
<line>hundred and sixty-six bushels of sweet potatoes from an acre of ground,</line>
<line>in a community where the average production had been only forty-nine</line>
<line>bushels to the acre. He had been able to do this by reason of his</line>
<line>knowledge of the chemistry of the soil and by his knowledge of improved</line>
<line>methods of agriculture. The white farmers in the neighbourhood</line>
<line>respected him, and came to him for ideas regarding the raising of sweet</line>
<line>potatoes. These white farmers honoured and respected him because he, by</line>
<line>his skill and knowledge, had added something to the wealth and the</line>
<line>comfort of the community in which he lived. I explained that my theory</line>
<line>of education for the Negro would not, for example, confine him for all</line>
<line>time to farm life—to the production of the best and the most sweet</line>
<line>potatoes—but that, if he succeeded in this line of industry, he could</line>
<line>lay the foundations upon which his children and grand-children could</line>
<line>grow to higher and more important things in life.</line>

<line>Such, in brief, were some of the views I advocated in this first</line>
<line>address dealing with the broad question of the relations of the two</line>
<line>races, and since that time I have not found any reason for changing my</line>
<line>views on any important point.</line>

<line>In my early life I used to cherish a feeling of ill will toward any one</line>
<line>who spoke in bitter terms against the Negro, or who advocated measures</line>
<line>that tended to oppress the black man or take from him opportunities for</line>
<line>growth in the most complete manner. Now, whenever I hear any one</line>
<line>advocating measures that are meant to curtail the development of</line>
<line>another, I pity the individual who would do this. I know that the one</line>
<line>who makes this mistake does so because of his own lack of opportunity</line>
<line>for the highest kind of growth. I pity him because I know that he is</line>
<line>trying to stop the progress of the world, and because I know that in</line>
<line>time the development and the ceaseless advance of humanity will make</line>
<line>him ashamed of his weak and narrow position. One might as well try to</line>
<line>stop the progress of a mighty railroad train by throwing his body</line>
<line>across the track, as to try to stop the growth of the world in the</line>
<line>direction of giving mankind more intelligence, more culture, more</line>
<line>skill, more liberty, and in the direction of extending more sympathy</line>
<line>and more brotherly kindness.</line>

<line>The address which I delivered at Madison, before the National</line>
<line>Educational Association, gave me a rather wide introduction in the</line>
<line>North, and soon after that opportunities began offering themselves for</line>
<line>me to address audiences there.</line>

<line>I was anxious, however, that the way might also be opened for me to</line>
<line>speak directly to a representative Southern white audience. A partial</line>
<line>opportunity of this kind, one that seemed to me might serve as an</line>
<line>entering wedge, presented itself in 1893, when the international</line>
<line>meeting of Christian Workers was held at Atlanta, Ga. When this</line>
<line>invitation came to me, I had engagements in Boston that seemed to make</line>
<line>it impossible for me to speak in Atlanta. Still, after looking over my</line>
<line>list of dates and places carefully, I found that I could take a train</line>
<line>from Boston that would get me into Atlanta about thirty minutes before</line>
<line>my address was to be delivered, and that I could remain in that city</line>
<line>before taking another train for Boston. My invitation to speak in</line>
<line>Atlanta stipulated that I was to confine my address to five minutes.</line>
<line>The question, then, was whether or not I could put enough into a</line>
<line>five-minute address to make it worth while for me to make such a trip.</line>

<line>I knew that the audience would be largely composed of the most</line>
<line>influential class of white men and women, and that it would be a rare</line>
<line>opportunity for me to let them know what we were trying to do at</line>
<line>Tuskegee, as well as to speak to them about the relations of the races.</line>
<line>So I decided to make the trip. I spoke for five minutes to an audience</line>
<line>of two thousand people, composed mostly of Southern and Northern</line>
<line>whites. What I said seemed to be received with favour and enthusiasm.</line>
<line>The Atlanta papers of the next day commented in friendly terms on my</line>
<line>address, and a good deal was said about it in different parts of the</line>
<line>country. I felt that I had in some degree accomplished my object—that</line>
<line>of getting a hearing from the dominant class of the South.</line>

<line>The demands made upon me for public addresses continued to increase,</line>
<line>coming in about equal numbers from my own people and from Northern</line>
<line>whites. I gave as much time to these addresses as I could spare from</line>
<line>the immediate work at Tuskegee. Most of the addresses in the North were</line>
<line>made for the direct purpose of getting funds with which to support the</line>
<line>school. Those delivered before the coloured people had for their main</line>
<line>object the impressing upon them the importance of industrial and</line>
<line>technical education in addition to academic and religious training.</line>

<line>I now come to that one of the incidents in my life which seems to have</line>
<line>excited the greatest amount of interest, and which perhaps went further</line>
<line>than anything else in giving me a reputation that in a sense might be</line>
<line>called National. I refer to the address which I delivered at the</line>
<line>opening of the Atlanta Cotton states and International Exposition, at</line>
<line>Atlanta, Ga., September 18, 1895.</line>

<line>So much has been said and written about this incident, and so many</line>
<line>questions have been asked me concerning the address, that perhaps I may</line>
<line>be excused for taking up the matter with some detail. The five-minute</line>
<line>address in Atlanta, which I came from Boston to deliver, was possibly</line>
<line>the prime cause for an opportunity being given me to make the second</line>
<line>address there. In the spring of 1895 I received a telegram from</line>
<line>prominent citizens in Atlanta asking me to accompany a committee from</line>
<line>that city to Washington for the purpose of appearing before a committee</line>
<line>of Congress in the interest of securing Government help for the</line>
<line>Exposition. The committee was composed of about twenty-five of the most</line>
<line>prominent and most influential white men of Georgia. All the members of</line>
<line>this committee were white men except Bishop Grant, Bishop Gaines, and</line>
<line>myself. The Mayor and several other city and state officials spoke</line>
<line>before the committee. They were followed by the two coloured bishops.</line>
<line>My name was the last on the list of speakers. I had never before</line>
<line>appeared before such a committee, nor had I ever delivered any address</line>
<line>in the capital of the Nation. I had many misgivings as to what I ought</line>
<line>to say, and as to the impression that my address would make. While I</line>
<line>cannot recall in detail what I said, I remember that I tried to impress</line>
<line>upon the committee, with all the earnestness and plainness of any</line>
<line>language that I could command, that if Congress wanted to do something</line>
<line>which would assist in ridding the South of the race question and making</line>
<line>friends between the two races, it should, in every proper way,</line>
<line>encourage the material and intellectual growth of both races. I said</line>
<line>that the Atlanta Exposition would present an opportunity for both races</line>
<line>to show what advance they had made since freedom, and would at the same</line>
<line>time afford encouragement to them to make still greater progress.</line>

<line>I tried to emphasize the fact that while the Negro should not be</line>
<line>deprived by unfair means of the franchise, political agitation alone</line>
<line>would not save him, and that back of the ballot he must have property,</line>
<line>industry, skill, economy, intelligence, and character, and that no race</line>
<line>without these elements could permanently succeed. I said that in</line>
<line>granting the appropriation Congress could do something that would prove</line>
<line>to be of real and lasting value to both races, and that it was the</line>
<line>first great opportunity of the kind that had been presented since the</line>
<line>close of the Civil War.</line>

<line>I spoke for fifteen or twenty minutes, and was surprised at the close</line>
<line>of my address to receive the hearty congratulations of the Georgia</line>
<line>committee and of the members of Congress who were present. The</line>
<line>Committee was unanimous in making a favourable report, and in a few</line>
<line>days the bill passed Congress. With the passing of this bill the</line>
<line>success of the Atlanta Exposition was assured.</line>

<line>Soon after this trip to Washington the directors of the Exposition</line>
<line>decided that it would be a fitting recognition of the coloured race to</line>
<line>erect a large and attractive building which should be devoted wholly to</line>
<line>showing the progress of the Negro since freedom. It was further decided</line>
<line>to have the building designed and erected wholly by Negro mechanics.</line>
<line>This plan was carried out. In design, beauty, and general finish the</line>
<line>Negro Building was equal to the others on the grounds.</line>

<line>After it was decided to have a separate Negro exhibit, the question</line>
<line>arose as to who should take care of it. The officials of the Exposition</line>
<line>were anxious that I should assume this responsibility, but I declined</line>
<line>to do so, on the plea that the work at Tuskegee at that time demanded</line>
<line>my time and strength. Largely at my suggestion, Mr. I. Garland Penn, of</line>
<line>Lynchburg, Va., was selected to be at the head of the Negro department.</line>
<line>I gave him all the aid that I could. The Negro exhibit, as a whole, was</line>
<line>large and creditable. The two exhibits in this department which</line>
<line>attracted the greatest amount of attention were those from the Hampton</line>
<line>Institute and the Tuskegee Institute. The people who seemed to be the</line>
<line>most surprised, as well as pleased, at what they saw in the Negro</line>
<line>Building were the Southern white people.</line>

<line>As the day for the opening of the Exposition drew near, the Board of</line>
<line>Directors began preparing the programme for the opening exercises. In</line>
<line>the discussion from day to day of the various features of this</line>
<line>programme, the question came up as to the advisability of putting a</line>
<line>member of the Negro race on for one of the opening addresses, since the</line>
<line>Negroes had been asked to take such a prominent part in the Exposition.</line>
<line>It was argued, further, that such recognition would mark the good</line>
<line>feeling prevailing between the two races. Of course there were those</line>
<line>who were opposed to any such recognition of the rights of the Negro,</line>
<line>but the Board of Directors, composed of men who represented the best</line>
<line>and most progressive element in the South, had their way, and voted to</line>
<line>invite a black man to speak on the opening day. The next thing was to</line>
<line>decide upon the person who was thus to represent the Negro race. After</line>
<line>the question had been canvassed for several days, the directors voted</line>
<line>unanimously to ask me to deliver one of the opening-day addresses, and</line>
<line>in a few days after that I received the official invitation.</line>

<line>The receiving of this invitation brought to me a sense of</line>
<line>responsibility that it would be hard for any one not placed in my</line>
<line>position to appreciate. What were my feelings when this invitation came</line>
<line>to me? I remembered that I had been a slave; that my early years had</line>
<line>been spent in the lowest depths of poverty and ignorance, and that I</line>
<line>had had little opportunity to prepare me for such a responsibility as</line>
<line>this. It was only a few years before that time that any white man in</line>
<line>the audience might have claimed me as his slave; and it was easily</line>
<line>possible that some of my former owners might be present to hear me</line>
<line>speak.</line>

<line>I knew, too, that this was the first time in the entire history of the</line>
<line>Negro that a member of my race had been asked to speak from the same</line>
<line>platform with white Southern men and women on any important National</line>
<line>occasion. I was asked now to speak to an audience composed of the</line>
<line>wealth and culture of the white South, the representatives of my former</line>
<line>masters. I knew, too, that while the greater part of my audience would</line>
<line>be composed of Southern people, yet there would be present a large</line>
<line>number of Northern whites, as well as a great many men and women of my</line>
<line>own race.</line>

<line>I was determined to say nothing that I did not feel from the bottom of</line>
<line>my heart to be true and right. When the invitation came to me, there</line>
<line>was not one word of intimation as to what I should say or as to what I</line>
<line>should omit. In this I felt that the Board of Directors had paid a</line>
<line>tribute to me. They knew that by one sentence I could have blasted, in</line>
<line>a large degree, the success of the Exposition. I was also painfully</line>
<line>conscious of the fact that, while I must be true to my own race in my</line>
<line>utterances, I had it in my power to make such an ill-timed address as</line>
<line>would result in preventing any similar invitation being extended to a</line>
<line>black man again for years to come. I was equally determined to be true</line>
<line>to the North, as well as to the best element of the white South, in</line>
<line>what I had to say.</line>

<line>The papers, North and South, had taken up the discussion of my coming</line>
<line>speech, and as the time for it drew near this discussion became more</line>
<line>and more widespread. Not a few of the Southern white papers were</line>
<line>unfriendly to the idea of my speaking. From my own race I received many</line>
<line>suggestions as to what I ought to say. I prepared myself as best I</line>
<line>could for the address, but as the eighteenth of September drew nearer,</line>
<line>the heavier my heart became, and the more I feared that my effort would</line>
<line>prove a failure and a disappointment.</line>

<line>The invitation had come at a time when I was very busy with my school</line>
<line>work, as it was the beginning of our school year. After preparing my</line>
<line>address, I went through it, as I usually do with those utterances which</line>
<line>I consider particularly important, with Mrs. Washington, and she</line>
<line>approved of what I intended to say. On the sixteenth of September, the</line>
<line>day before I was to start for Atlanta, so many of the Tuskegee teachers</line>
<line>expressed a desire to hear my address that I consented to read it to</line>
<line>them in a body. When I had done so, and had heard their criticisms and</line>
<line>comments, I felt somewhat relieved, since they seemed to think well of</line>
<line>what I had to say.</line>

<line>On the morning of September 17, together with Mrs. Washington and my</line>
<line>three children, I started for Atlanta. I felt a good deal as I suppose</line>
<line>a man feels when he is on his way to the gallows. In passing through</line>
<line>the town of Tuskegee I met a white farmer who lived some distance out</line>
<line>in the country. In a jesting manner this man said: “Washington, you</line>
<line>have spoken before the Northern white people, the Negroes in the South,</line>
<line>and to us country white people in the South; but Atlanta, to-morrow,</line>
<line>you will have before you the Northern whites, the Southern whites, and</line>
<line>the Negroes all together. I am afraid that you have got yourself in a</line>
<line>tight place.” This farmer diagnosed the situation correctly, but his</line>
<line>frank words did not add anything to my comfort.</line>

<line>In the course of the journey from Tuskegee to Atlanta both coloured and</line>
<line>white people came to the train to point me out, and discussed with</line>
<line>perfect freedom, in my hearings, what was going to take place the next</line>
<line>day. We were met by a committee in Atlanta. Almost the first thing that</line>
<line>I heard when I got off the train in that city was an expression</line>
<line>something like this, from an old coloured man near by: “Dat’s de man of</line>
<line>my race what’s gwine to make a speech at de Exposition to-morrow. I’se</line>
<line>sho’ gwine to hear him.”</line>

<line>Atlanta was literally packed, at the time, with people from all parts</line>
<line>of the country, and with representatives of foreign governments, as</line>
<line>well as with military and civic organizations. The afternoon papers had</line>
<line>forecasts of the next day’s proceedings in flaring headlines. All this</line>
<line>tended to add to my burden. I did not sleep much that night. The next</line>
<line>morning, before day, I went carefully over what I planned to say. I</line>
<line>also kneeled down and asked God’s blessing upon my effort. Right here,</line>
<line>perhaps, I ought to add that I make it a rule never to go before an</line>
<line>audience, on any occasion, without asking the blessing of God upon what</line>
<line>I want to say.</line>

<line>I always make it a rule to make especial preparation for each separate</line>
<line>address. No two audiences are exactly alike. It is my aim to reach and</line>
<line>talk to the heart of each individual audience, taking it into my</line>
<line>confidence very much as I would a person. When I am speaking to an</line>
<line>audience, I care little for how what I am saying is going to sound in</line>
<line>the newspapers, or to another audience, or to an individual. At the</line>
<line>time, the audience before me absorbs all my sympathy, thought, and</line>
<line>energy.</line>

<line>Early in the morning a committee called to escort me to my place in the</line>
<line>procession which was to march to the Exposition grounds. In this</line>
<line>procession were prominent coloured citizens in carriages, as well as</line>
<line>several Negro military organizations. I noted that the Exposition</line>
<line>officials seemed to go out of their way to see that all of the coloured</line>
<line>people in the procession were properly placed and properly treated. The</line>
<line>procession was about three hours in reaching the Exposition grounds,</line>
<line>and during all of this time the sun was shining down upon us</line>
<line>disagreeably hot. When we reached the grounds, the heat, together with</line>
<line>my nervous anxiety, made me feel as if I were about ready to collapse,</line>
<line>and to feel that my address was not going to be a success. When I</line>
<line>entered the audience-room, I found it packed with humanity from bottom</line>
<line>to top, and there were thousands outside who could not get in.</line>

<line>The room was very large, and well suited to public speaking. When I</line>
<line>entered the room, there were vigorous cheers from the coloured portion</line>
<line>of the audience, and faint cheers from some of the white people. I had</line>
<line>been told, while I had been in Atlanta, that while many white people</line>
<line>were going to be present to hear me speak, simply out of curiosity, and</line>
<line>that others who would be present would be in full sympathy with me,</line>
<line>there was a still larger element of the audience which would consist of</line>
<line>those who were going to be present for the purpose of hearing me make a</line>
<line>fool of myself, or, at least, of hearing me say some foolish thing so</line>
<line>that they could say to the officials who had invited me to speak, “I</line>
<line>told you so!”</line>

<line>One of the trustees of the Tuskegee Institute, as well as my personal</line>
<line>friend, Mr. William H. Baldwin, Jr. was at the time General Manager of</line>
<line>the Southern Railroad, and happened to be in Atlanta on that day. He</line>
<line>was so nervous about the kind of reception that I would have, and the</line>
<line>effect that my speech would produce, that he could not persuade himself</line>
<line>to go into the building, but walked back and forth in the grounds</line>
<line>outside until the opening exercises were over.</line>




</chapter>

<chapter>
<title>Chapter XIV. The Atlanta Exposition Address</title>


<line>The Atlanta Exposition, at which I had been asked to make an address as</line>
<line>a representative of the Negro race, as stated in the last chapter, was</line>
<line>opened with a short address from Governor Bullock. After other</line>
<line>interesting exercises, including an invocation from Bishop Nelson, of</line>
<line>Georgia, a dedicatory ode by Albert Howell, Jr., and addresses by the</line>
<line>President of the Exposition and Mrs. Joseph Thompson, the President of</line>
<line>the Woman’s Board, Governor Bullock introduce me with the words, “We</line>
<line>have with us to-day a representative of Negro enterprise and Negro</line>
<line>civilization.”</line>

<line>When I arose to speak, there was considerable cheering, especially from</line>
<line>the coloured people. As I remember it now, the thing that was uppermost</line>
<line>in my mind was the desire to say something that would cement the</line>
<line>friendship of the races and bring about hearty cooperation between</line>
<line>them. So far as my outward surroundings were concerned, the only thing</line>
<line>that I recall distinctly now is that when I got up, I saw thousands of</line>
<line>eyes looking intently into my face. The following is the address which</line>
<line>I delivered:—</line>

<line>Mr. President and Gentlemen of the Board of Directors and Citizens.</line>

<line>One-third of the population of the South is of the Negro race. No</line>
<line>enterprise seeking the material, civil, or moral welfare of this</line>
<line>section can disregard this element of our population and reach the</line>
<line>highest success. I but convey to you, Mr. President and Directors, the</line>
<line>sentiment of the masses of my race when I say that in no way have the</line>
<line>value and manhood of the American Negro been more fittingly and</line>
<line>generously recognized than by the managers of this magnificent</line>
<line>Exposition at every stage of its progress. It is a recognition that</line>
<line>will do more to cement the friendship of the two races than any</line>
<line>occurrence since the dawn of our freedom.</line>

<line>Not only this, but the opportunity here afforded will awaken among us a</line>
<line>new era of industrial progress. Ignorant and inexperienced, it is not</line>
<line>strange that in the first years of our new life we began at the top</line>
<line>instead of at the bottom; that a seat in Congress or the state</line>
<line>legislature was more sought than real estate or industrial skill; that</line>
<line>the political convention or stump speaking had more attractions than</line>
<line>starting a dairy farm or truck garden.</line>

<line>A ship lost at sea for many days suddenly sighted a friendly vessel.</line>
<line>From the mast of the unfortunate vessel was seen a signal, “Water,</line>
<line>water; we die of thirst!” The answer from the friendly vessel at once</line>
<line>came back, “Cast down your bucket where you are.” A second time the</line>
<line>signal, “Water, water; send us water!” ran up from the distressed</line>
<line>vessel, and was answered, “Cast down your bucket where you are.” And a</line>
<line>third and fourth signal for water was answered, “Cast down your bucket</line>
<line>where you are.” The captain of the distressed vessel, at last heeding</line>
<line>the injunction, cast down his bucket, and it came up full of fresh,</line>
<line>sparkling water from the mouth of the Amazon River. To those of my race</line>
<line>who depend on bettering their condition in a foreign land or who</line>
<line>underestimate the importance of cultivating friendly relations with the</line>
<line>Southern white man, who is their next-door neighbour, I would say:</line>
<line>“Cast down your bucket where you are”—cast it down in making friends in</line>
<line>every manly way of the people of all races by whom we are surrounded.</line>

<line>Cast it down in agriculture, mechanics, in commerce, in domestic</line>
<line>service, and in the professions. And in this connection it is well to</line>
<line>bear in mind that whatever other sins the South may be called to bear,</line>
<line>when it comes to business, pure and simple, it is in the South that the</line>
<line>Negro is given a man’s chance in the commercial world, and in nothing</line>
<line>is this Exposition more eloquent than in emphasizing this chance. Our</line>
<line>greatest danger is that in the great leap from slavery to freedom we</line>
<line>may overlook the fact that the masses of us are to live by the</line>
<line>productions of our hands, and fail to keep in mind that we shall</line>
<line>prosper in proportion as we learn to dignify and glorify common labour</line>
<line>and put brains and skill into the common occupations of life; shall</line>
<line>prosper in proportion as we learn to draw the line between the</line>
<line>superficial and the substantial, the ornamental gewgaws of life and the</line>
<line>useful. No race can prosper till it learns that there is as much</line>
<line>dignity in tilling a field as in writing a poem. It is at the bottom of</line>
<line>life we must begin, and not at the top. Nor should we permit our</line>
<line>grievances to overshadow our opportunities.</line>

<line>To those of the white race who look to the incoming of those of foreign</line>
<line>birth and strange tongue and habits of the prosperity of the South,</line>
<line>were I permitted I would repeat what I say to my own race: “Cast down</line>
<line>your bucket where you are.” Cast it down among the eight millions of</line>
<line>Negroes whose habits you know, whose fidelity and love you have tested</line>
<line>in days when to have proved treacherous meant the ruin of your</line>
<line>firesides. Cast down your bucket among these people who have, without</line>
<line>strikes and labour wars, tilled your fields, cleared your forests,</line>
<line>builded your railroads and cities, and brought forth treasures from the</line>
<line>bowels of the earth, and helped make possible this magnificent</line>
<line>representation of the progress of the South. Casting down your bucket</line>
<line>among my people, helping and encouraging them as you are doing on these</line>
<line>grounds, and to education of head, hand, and heart, you will find that</line>
<line>they will buy your surplus land, make blossom the waste places in your</line>
<line>fields, and run your factories. While doing this, you can be sure in</line>
<line>the future, as in the past, that you and your families will be</line>
<line>surrounded by the most patient, faithful, law-abiding, and unresentful</line>
<line>people that the world has seen. As we have proved our loyalty to you in</line>
<line>the past, nursing your children, watching by the sick-bed of your</line>
<line>mothers and fathers, and often following them with tear-dimmed eyes to</line>
<line>their graves, so in the future, in our humble way, we shall stand by</line>
<line>you with a devotion that no foreigner can approach, ready to lay down</line>
<line>our lives, if need be, in defence of yours, interlacing our industrial,</line>
<line>commercial, civil, and religious life with yours in a way that shall</line>
<line>make the interests of both races one. In all things that are purely</line>
<line>social we can be as separate as the fingers, yet one as the hand in all</line>
<line>things essential to mutual progress.</line>

<line>There is no defence or security for any of us except in the highest</line>
<line>intelligence and development of all. If anywhere there are efforts</line>
<line>tending to curtail the fullest growth of the Negro, let these efforts</line>
<line>be turned into stimulating, encouraging, and making him the most useful</line>
<line>and intelligent citizen. Effort or means so invested will pay a</line>
<line>thousand per cent interest. These efforts will be twice</line>
<line>blessed—“blessing him that gives and him that takes.”</line>

<line>There is no escape through law of man or God from the inevitable:—</line>

<line>The laws of changeless justice bind</line>
<line>Oppressor with oppressed;</line>
<line>And close as sin and suffering joined</line>
<line>We march to fate abreast.</line>

<line>Nearly sixteen millions of hands will aid you in pulling the load</line>
<line>upward, or they will pull against you the load downward. We shall</line>
<line>constitute one-third and more of the ignorance and crime of the South,</line>
<line>or one-third its intelligence and progress; we shall contribute</line>
<line>one-third to the business and industrial prosperity of the South, or we</line>
<line>shall prove a veritable body of death, stagnating, depressing,</line>
<line>retarding every effort to advance the body politic.</line>

<line>Gentlemen of the Exposition, as we present to you our humble effort at</line>
<line>an exhibition of our progress, you must not expect overmuch. Starting</line>
<line>thirty years ago with ownership here and there in a few quilts and</line>
<line>pumpkins and chickens (gathered from miscellaneous sources), remember</line>
<line>the path that has led from these to the inventions and production of</line>
<line>agricultural implements, buggies, steam-engines, newspapers, books,</line>
<line>statuary, carving, paintings, the management of drug-stores and banks,</line>
<line>has not been trodden without contact with thorns and thistles. While we</line>
<line>take pride in what we exhibit as a result of our independent efforts,</line>
<line>we do not for a moment forget that our part in this exhibition would</line>
<line>fall far short of your expectations but for the constant help that has</line>
<line>come to our education life, not only from the Southern states, but</line>
<line>especially from Northern philanthropists, who have made their gifts a</line>
<line>constant stream of blessing and encouragement.</line>

<line>The wisest among my race understand that the agitation of questions of</line>
<line>social equality is the extremest folly, and that progress in the</line>
<line>enjoyment of all the privileges that will come to us must be the result</line>
<line>of severe and constant struggle rather than of artificial forcing. No</line>
<line>race that has anything to contribute to the markets of the world is</line>
<line>long in any degree ostracized. It is important and right that all</line>
<line>privileges of the law be ours, but it is vastly more important that we</line>
<line>be prepared for the exercises of these privileges. The opportunity to</line>
<line>earn a dollar in a factory just now is worth infinitely more than the</line>
<line>opportunity to spend a dollar in an opera-house.</line>

<line>In conclusion, may I repeat that nothing in thirty years has given us</line>
<line>more hope and encouragement, and drawn us so near to you of the white</line>
<line>race, as this opportunity offered by the Exposition; and here bending,</line>
<line>as it were, over the altar that represents the results of the struggles</line>
<line>of your race and mine, both starting practically empty-handed three</line>
<line>decades ago, I pledge that in your effort to work out the great and</line>
<line>intricate problem which God has laid at the doors of the South, you</line>
<line>shall have at all times the patient, sympathetic help of my race; only</line>
<line>let this be constantly in mind, that, while from representations in</line>
<line>these buildings of the product of field, of forest, of mine, of</line>
<line>factory, letters, and art, much good will come, yet far above and</line>
<line>beyond material benefits will be that higher good, that, let us pray</line>
<line>God, will come, in a blotting out of sectional differences and racial</line>
<line>animosities and suspicions, in a determination to administer absolute</line>
<line>justice, in a willing obedience among all classes to the mandates of</line>
<line>law. This, this, coupled with our material prosperity, will bring into</line>
<line>our beloved South a new heaven and a new earth.</line>

<line>The first thing that I remember, after I had finished speaking, was</line>
<line>that Governor Bullock rushed across the platform and took me by the</line>
<line>hand, and that others did the same. I received so many and such hearty</line>
<line>congratulations that I found it difficult to get out of the building. I</line>
<line>did not appreciate to any degree, however, the impression which my</line>
<line>address seemed to have made, until the next morning, when I went into</line>
<line>the business part of the city. As soon as I was recognized, I was</line>
<line>surprised to find myself pointed out and surrounded by a crowd of men</line>
<line>who wished to shake hands with me. This was kept up on every street on</line>
<line>to which I went, to an extent which embarrassed me so much that I went</line>
<line>back to my boarding-place. The next morning I returned to Tuskegee. At</line>
<line>the station in Atlanta, and at almost all of the stations at which the</line>
<line>train stopped between that city and Tuskegee, I found a crowd of people</line>
<line>anxious to shake hands with me.</line>

<line>The papers in all parts of the United States published the address in</line>
<line>full, and for months afterward there were complimentary editorial</line>
<line>references to it. Mr. Clark Howell, the editor of the Atlanta</line>
<line>Constitution, telegraphed to a New York paper, among other words, the</line>
<line>following, “I do not exaggerate when I say that Professor Booker T.</line>
<line>Washington’s address yesterday was one of the most notable speeches,</line>
<line>both as to character and as to the warmth of its reception, ever</line>
<line>delivered to a Southern audience. The address was a revelation. The</line>
<line>whole speech is a platform upon which blacks and whites can stand with</line>
<line>full justice to each other.”</line>

<line>The Boston Transcript said editorially: “The speech of Booker T.</line>
<line>Washington at the Atlanta Exposition, this week, seems to have dwarfed</line>
<line>all the other proceedings and the Exposition itself. The sensation that</line>
<line>it has caused in the press has never been equalled.”</line>

<line>I very soon began receiving all kinds of propositions from lecture</line>
<line>bureaus, and editors of magazines and papers, to take the lecture</line>
<line>platform, and to write articles. One lecture bureau offered me fifty</line>
<line>thousand dollars, or two hundred dollars a night and expenses, if I</line>
<line>would place my services at its disposal for a given period. To all</line>
<line>these communications I replied that my life-work was at Tuskegee; and</line>
<line>that whenever I spoke it must be in the interests of Tuskegee school</line>
<line>and my race, and that I would enter into no arrangements that seemed to</line>
<line>place a mere commercial value upon my services.</line>

<line>Some days after its delivery I sent a copy of my address to the</line>
<line>President of the United States, the Hon. Grover Cleveland. I received</line>
<line>from him the following autograph reply:—</line>

<line>Gray Gables, Buzzard’s Bay, Mass.,</line>

<line>October 6, 1895.</line>

<line>Booker T. Washington, Esq.:</line>

<line>My Dear Sir: I thank you for sending me a copy of your address</line>
<line>delivered at the Atlanta Exposition.</line>

<line>I thank you with much enthusiasm for making the address. I have read it</line>
<line>with intense interest, and I think the Exposition would be fully</line>
<line>justified if it did not do more than furnish the opportunity for its</line>
<line>delivery. Your words cannot fail to delight and encourage all who wish</line>
<line>well for your race; and if our coloured fellow-citizens do not from</line>
<line>your utterances gather new hope and form new determinations to gain</line>
<line>every valuable advantage offered them by their citizenship, it will be</line>
<line>strange indeed.</line>

<line>Yours very truly,</line>

<line>Grover Cleveland.</line>

<line>Later I met Mr. Cleveland, for the first time, when, as President, he</line>
<line>visited the Atlanta Exposition. At the request of myself and others he</line>
<line>consented to spend an hour in the Negro Building, for the purpose of</line>
<line>inspecting the Negro exhibit and of giving the coloured people in</line>
<line>attendance an opportunity to shake hands with him. As soon as I met Mr.</line>
<line>Cleveland I became impressed with his simplicity, greatness, and rugged</line>
<line>honesty. I have met him many times since then, both at public functions</line>
<line>and at his private residence in Princeton, and the more I see of him</line>
<line>the more I admire him. When he visited the Negro Building in Atlanta he</line>
<line>seemed to give himself up wholly, for that hour, to the coloured</line>
<line>people. He seemed to be as careful to shake hands with some old</line>
<line>coloured “auntie” clad partially in rags, and to take as much pleasure</line>
<line>in doing so, as if he were greeting some millionaire. Many of the</line>
<line>coloured people took advantage of the occasion to get him to write his</line>
<line>name in a book or on a slip of paper. He was as careful and patient in</line>
<line>doing this as if he were putting his signature to some great state</line>
<line>document.</line>

<line>Mr. Cleveland has not only shown his friendship for me in many personal</line>
<line>ways, but has always consented to do anything I have asked of him for</line>
<line>our school. This he has done, whether it was to make a personal</line>
<line>donation or to use his influence in securing the donations of others.</line>
<line>Judging from my personal acquaintance with Mr. Cleveland, I do not</line>
<line>believe that he is conscious of possessing any colour prejudice. He is</line>
<line>too great for that. In my contact with people I find that, as a rule,</line>
<line>it is only the little, narrow people who live for themselves, who never</line>
<line>read good books, who do not travel, who never open up their souls in a</line>
<line>way to permit them to come into contact with other souls—with the great</line>
<line>outside world. No man whose vision is bounded by colour can come into</line>
<line>contact with what is highest and best in the world. In meeting men, in</line>
<line>many places, I have found that the happiest people are those who do the</line>
<line>most for others; the most miserable are those who do the least. I have</line>
<line>also found that few things, if any, are capable of making one so blind</line>
<line>and narrow as race prejudice. I often say to our students, in the</line>
<line>course of my talks to them on Sunday evenings in the chapel, that the</line>
<line>longer I live and the more experience I have of the world, the more I</line>
<line>am convinced that, after all, the one thing that is most worth living</line>
<line>for—and dying for, if need be—is the opportunity of making some one</line>
<line>else more happy and more useful.</line>

<line>The coloured people and the coloured newspapers at first seemed to be</line>
<line>greatly pleased with the character of my Atlanta address, as well as</line>
<line>with its reception. But after the first burst of enthusiasm began to</line>
<line>die away, and the coloured people began reading the speech in cold</line>
<line>type, some of them seemed to feel that they had been hypnotized. They</line>
<line>seemed to feel that I had been too liberal in my remarks toward the</line>
<line>Southern whites, and that I had not spoken out strongly enough for what</line>
<line>they termed the “rights” of my race. For a while there was a reaction,</line>
<line>so far as a certain element of my own race was concerned, but later</line>
<line>these reactionary ones seemed to have been won over to my way of</line>
<line>believing and acting.</line>

<line>While speaking of changes in public sentiment, I recall that about ten</line>
<line>years after the school at Tuskegee was established, I had an experience</line>
<line>that I shall never forget. Dr. Lyman Abbott, then the pastor of</line>
<line>Plymouth Church, and also editor of the Outlook (then the Christian</line>
<line>Union), asked me to write a letter for his paper giving my opinion of</line>
<line>the exact condition, mental and moral, of the coloured ministers in the</line>
<line>South, as based upon my observations. I wrote the letter, giving the</line>
<line>exact facts as I conceived them to be. The picture painted was a rather</line>
<line>black one—or, since I am black, shall I say “white”? It could not be</line>
<line>otherwise with a race but a few years out of slavery, a race which had</line>
<line>not had time or opportunity to produce a competent ministry.</line>

<line>What I said soon reached every Negro minister in the country, I think,</line>
<line>and the letters of condemnation which I received from them were not</line>
<line>few. I think that for a year after the publication of this article</line>
<line>every association and every conference or religious body of any kind,</line>
<line>of my race, that met, did not fail before adjourning to pass a</line>
<line>resolution condemning me, or calling upon me to retract or modify what</line>
<line>I had said. Many of these organizations went so far in their</line>
<line>resolutions as to advise parents to cease sending their children to</line>
<line>Tuskegee. One association even appointed a “missionary” whose duty it</line>
<line>was to warn the people against sending their children to Tuskegee. This</line>
<line>missionary had a son in the school, and I noticed that, whatever the</line>
<line>“missionary” might have said or done with regard to others, he was</line>
<line>careful not to take his son away from the institution. Many of the</line>
<line>coloured papers, especially those that were the organs of religious</line>
<line>bodies, joined in the general chorus of condemnation or demands for</line>
<line>retraction.</line>

<line>During the whole time of the excitement, and through all the criticism,</line>
<line>I did not utter a word of explanation or retraction. I knew that I was</line>
<line>right, and that time and the sober second thought of the people would</line>
<line>vindicate me. It was not long before the bishops and other church</line>
<line>leaders began to make careful investigation of the conditions of the</line>
<line>ministry, and they found out that I was right. In fact, the oldest and</line>
<line>most influential bishop in one branch of the Methodist Church said that</line>
<line>my words were far too mild. Very soon public sentiment began making</line>
<line>itself felt, in demanding a purifying of the ministry. While this is</line>
<line>not yet complete by any means, I think I may say, without egotism, and</line>
<line>I have been told by many of our most influential ministers, that my</line>
<line>words had much to do with starting a demand for the placing of a higher</line>
<line>type of men in the pulpit. I have had the satisfaction of having many</line>
<line>who once condemned me thank me heartily for my frank words.</line>

<line>The change of the attitude of the Negro ministry, so far as regards</line>
<line>myself, is so complete that at the present time I have no warmer</line>
<line>friends among any class than I have among the clergymen. The</line>
<line>improvement in the character and life of the Negro ministers is one of</line>
<line>the most gratifying evidences of the progress of the race. My</line>
<line>experience with them, as well as other events in my life, convince me</line>
<line>that the thing to do, when one feels sure that he has said or done the</line>
<line>right thing, and is condemned, is to stand still and keep quiet. If he</line>
<line>is right, time will show it.</line>

<line>In the midst of the discussion which was going on concerning my Atlanta</line>
<line>speech, I received the letter which I give below, from Dr. Gilman, the</line>
<line>President of Johns Hopkins University, who had been made chairman of</line>
<line>the judges of award in connection with the Atlanta Exposition:—</line>

<line>Johns Hopkins University, Baltimore,</line>

<line>President’s Office, September 30, 1895.</line>

<line>Dear Mr. Washington: Would it be agreeable to you to be one of the</line>
<line>Judges of Award in the Department of Education at Atlanta? If so, I</line>
<line>shall be glad to place your name upon the list. A line by telegraph</line>
<line>will be welcomed.</line>

<line>Yours very truly,</line>

<line>D.C. Gilman</line>

<line>I think I was even more surprised to receive this invitation than I had</line>
<line>been to receive the invitation to speak at the opening of the</line>
<line>Exposition. It was to be a part of my duty, as one of the jurors, to</line>
<line>pass not only upon the exhibits of the coloured schools, but also upon</line>
<line>those of the white schools. I accepted the position, and spent a month</line>
<line>in Atlanta in performance of the duties which it entailed. The board of</line>
<line>jurors was a large one, containing in all of sixty members. It was</line>
<line>about equally divided between Southern white people and Northern white</line>
<line>people. Among them were college presidents, leading scientists and men</line>
<line>of letters, and specialists in many subjects. When the group of jurors</line>
<line>to which I was assigned met for organization, Mr. Thomas Nelson Page,</line>
<line>who was one of the number, moved that I be made secretary of that</line>
<line>division, and the motion was unanimously adopted. Nearly half of our</line>
<line>division were Southern people. In performing my duties in the</line>
<line>inspection of the exhibits of white schools I was in every case treated</line>
<line>with respect, and at the close of our labours I parted from my</line>
<line>associates with regret.</line>

<line>I am often asked to express myself more freely than I do upon the</line>
<line>political condition and the political future of my race. These</line>
<line>recollections of my experience in Atlanta give me the opportunity to do</line>
<line>so briefly. My own belief is, although I have never before said so in</line>
<line>so many words, that the time will come when the Negro in the South will</line>
<line>be accorded all the political rights which his ability, character, and</line>
<line>material possessions entitle him to. I think, though, that the</line>
<line>opportunity to freely exercise such political rights will not come in</line>
<line>any large degree through outside or artificial forcing, but will be</line>
<line>accorded to the Negro by the Southern white people themselves, and that</line>
<line>they will protect him in the exercise of those rights. Just as soon as</line>
<line>the South gets over the old feeling that it is being forced by</line>
<line>“foreigners,” or “aliens,” to do something which it does not want to</line>
<line>do, I believe that the change in the direction that I have indicated is</line>
<line>going to begin. In fact, there are indications that it is already</line>
<line>beginning in a slight degree.</line>

<line>Let me illustrate my meaning. Suppose that some months before the</line>
<line>opening of the Atlanta Exposition there had been a general demand from</line>
<line>the press and public platform outside the South that a Negro be given a</line>
<line>place on the opening programme, and that a Negro be placed upon the</line>
<line>board of jurors of award. Would any such recognition of the race have</line>
<line>taken place? I do not think so. The Atlanta officials went as far as</line>
<line>they did because they felt it to be a pleasure, as well as a duty, to</line>
<line>reward what they considered merit in the Negro race. Say what we will,</line>
<line>there is something in human nature which we cannot blot out, which</line>
<line>makes one man, in the end, recognize and reward merit in another,</line>
<line>regardless of colour or race.</line>

<line>I believe it is the duty of the Negro—as the greater part of the race</line>
<line>is already doing—to deport himself modestly in regard to political</line>
<line>claims, depending upon the slow but sure influences that proceed from</line>
<line>the possession of property, intelligence, and high character for the</line>
<line>full recognition of his political rights. I think that the according of</line>
<line>the full exercise of political rights is going to be a matter of</line>
<line>natural, slow growth, not an over-night, gourd-vine affair. I do not</line>
<line>believe that the Negro should cease voting, for a man cannot learn the</line>
<line>exercise of self-government by ceasing to vote, any more than a boy can</line>
<line>learn to swim by keeping out of the water, but I do believe that in his</line>
<line>voting he should more and more be influenced by those of intelligence</line>
<line>and character who are his next-door neighbours.</line>

<line>I know coloured men who, through the encouragement, help, and advice of</line>
<line>Southern white people, have accumulated thousands of dollars’ worth of</line>
<line>property, but who, at the same time, would never think of going to</line>
<line>those same persons for advice concerning the casting of their ballots.</line>
<line>This, it seems to me, is unwise and unreasonable, and should cease. In</line>
<line>saying this I do not mean that the Negro should truckle, or not vote</line>
<line>from principle, for the instant he ceases to vote from principle he</line>
<line>loses the confidence and respect of the Southern white man even.</line>

<line>I do not believe that any state should make a law that permits an</line>
<line>ignorant and poverty-stricken white man to vote, and prevents a black</line>
<line>man in the same condition from voting. Such a law is not only unjust,</line>
<line>but it will react, as all unjust laws do, in time; for the effect of</line>
<line>such a law is to encourage the Negro to secure education and property,</line>
<line>and at the same time it encourages the white man to remain in ignorance</line>
<line>and poverty. I believe that in time, through the operation of</line>
<line>intelligence and friendly race relations, all cheating at the</line>
<line>ballot-box in the South will cease. It will become apparent that the</line>
<line>white man who begins by cheating a Negro out of his ballot soon learns</line>
<line>to cheat a white man out of his, and that the man who does this ends</line>
<line>his career of dishonesty by the theft of property or by some equally</line>
<line>serious crime. In my opinion, the time will come when the South will</line>
<line>encourage all of its citizens to vote. It will see that it pays better,</line>
<line>from every standpoint, to have healthy, vigorous life than to have that</line>
<line>political stagnation which always results when one-half of the</line>
<line>population has no share and no interest in the Government.</line>

<line>As a rule, I believe in universal, free suffrage, but I believe that in</line>
<line>the South we are confronted with peculiar conditions that justify the</line>
<line>protection of the ballot in many of the states, for a while at least,</line>
<line>either by an education test, a property test, or by both combined; but</line>
<line>whatever tests are required, they should be made to apply with equal</line>
<line>and exact justice to both races.</line>




</chapter>

<chapter>
<title>Chapter XV. The Secret Of Success In Public Speaking</title>


<line>As to how my address at Atlanta was received by the audience in the</line>
<line>Exposition building, I think I prefer to let Mr. James Creelman, the</line>
<line>noted war correspondent, tell. Mr. Creelman was present, and</line>
<line>telegraphed the following account to the New York World:—</line>

<line>Atlanta, September 18.</line>

<line>While President Cleveland was waiting at Gray Gables to-day, to send</line>
<line>the electric spark that started the machinery of the Atlanta</line>
<line>Exposition, a Negro Moses stood before a great audience of white people</line>
<line>and delivered an oration that marks a new epoch in the history of the</line>
<line>South; and a body of Negro troops marched in a procession with the</line>
<line>citizen soldiery of Georgia and Louisiana. The whole city is thrilling</line>
<line>to-night with a realization of the extraordinary significance of these</line>
<line>two unprecedented events. Nothing has happened since Henry Grady’s</line>
<line>immortal speech before the New England society in New York that</line>
<line>indicates so profoundly the spirit of the New South, except, perhaps,</line>
<line>the opening of the Exposition itself.</line>

<line>When Professor Booker T. Washington, Principal of an industrial school</line>
<line>for coloured people in Tuskegee, Ala. stood on the platform of the</line>
<line>Auditorium, with the sun shining over the heads of his auditors into</line>
<line>his eyes, and with his whole face lit up with the fire of prophecy,</line>
<line>Clark Howell, the successor of Henry Grady, said to me, “That man’s</line>
<line>speech is the beginning of a moral revolution in America.”</line>

<line>It is the first time that a Negro has made a speech in the South on any</line>
<line>important occasion before an audience composed of white men and women.</line>
<line>It electrified the audience, and the response was as if it had come</line>
<line>from the throat of a whirlwind.</line>

<line>Mrs. Thompson had hardly taken her seat when all eyes were turned on a</line>
<line>tall tawny Negro sitting in the front row of the platform. It was</line>
<line>Professor Booker T. Washington, President of the Tuskegee (Alabama)</line>
<line>Normal and Industrial Institute, who must rank from this time forth as</line>
<line>the foremost man of his race in America. Gilmore’s Band played the</line>
<line>“Star-Spangled Banner,” and the audience cheered. The tune changed to</line>
<line>“Dixie” and the audience roared with shrill “hi-yis.” Again the music</line>
<line>changed, this time to “Yankee Doodle,” and the clamour lessened.</line>

<line>All this time the eyes of the thousands present looked straight at the</line>
<line>Negro orator. A strange thing was to happen. A black man was to speak</line>
<line>for his people, with none to interrupt him. As Professor Washington</line>
<line>strode to the edge of the stage, the low, descending sun shot fiery</line>
<line>rays through the windows into his face. A great shout greeted him. He</line>
<line>turned his head to avoid the blinding light, and moved about the</line>
<line>platform for relief. Then he turned his wonderful countenance to the</line>
<line>sun without a blink of the eyelids, and began to talk.</line>

<line>There was a remarkable figure; tall, bony, straight as a Sioux chief,</line>
<line>high forehead, straight nose, heavy jaws, and strong, determined mouth,</line>
<line>with big white teeth, piercing eyes, and a commanding manner. The</line>
<line>sinews stood out on his bronzed neck, and his muscular right arm swung</line>
<line>high in the air, with a lead-pencil grasped in the clinched brown fist.</line>
<line>His big feet were planted squarely, with the heels together and the</line>
<line>toes turned out. His voice range out clear and true, and he paused</line>
<line>impressively as he made each point. Within ten minutes the multitude</line>
<line>was in an uproar of enthusiasm—handkerchiefs were waved, canes were</line>
<line>flourished, hats were tossed in the air. The fairest women of Georgia</line>
<line>stood up and cheered. It was as if the orator had bewitched them.</line>

<line>And when he held his dusky hand high above his head, with the fingers</line>
<line>stretched wide apart, and said to the white people of the South on</line>
<line>behalf of his race, “In all things that are purely social we can be as</line>
<line>separate as the fingers, yet one as the hand in all things essential to</line>
<line>mutual progress,” the great wave of sound dashed itself against the</line>
<line>walls, and the whole audience was on its feet in a delirium of</line>
<line>applause, and I thought at that moment of the night when Henry Grady</line>
<line>stood among the curling wreaths of tobacco-smoke in Delmonico’s</line>
<line>banquet-hall and said, “I am a Cavalier among Roundheads.”</line>

<line>I have heard the great orators of many countries, but not even</line>
<line>Gladstone himself could have pleased a cause with most consummate power</line>
<line>than did this angular Negro, standing in a nimbus of sunshine,</line>
<line>surrounded by the men who once fought to keep his race in bondage. The</line>
<line>roar might swell ever so high, but the expression of his earnest face</line>
<line>never changed.</line>

<line>A ragged, ebony giant, squatted on the floor in one of the aisles,</line>
<line>watched the orator with burning eyes and tremulous face until the</line>
<line>supreme burst of applause came, and then the tears ran down his face.</line>
<line>Most of the Negroes in the audience were crying, perhaps without</line>
<line>knowing just why.</line>

<line>At the close of the speech Governor Bullock rushed across the stage and</line>
<line>seized the orator’s hand. Another shout greeted this demonstration, and</line>
<line>for a few minutes the two men stood facing each other, hand in hand.</line>

<line>So far as I could spare the time from the immediate work at Tuskegee,</line>
<line>after my Atlanta address, I accepted some of the invitations to speak</line>
<line>in public which came to me, especially those that would take me into</line>
<line>territory where I thought it would pay to plead the cause of my race,</line>
<line>but I always did this with the understanding that I was to be free to</line>
<line>talk about my life-work and the needs of my people. I also had it</line>
<line>understood that I was not to speak in the capacity of a professional</line>
<line>lecturer, or for mere commercial gain.</line>

<line>In my efforts on the public platform I never have been able to</line>
<line>understand why people come to hear me speak. This question I never can</line>
<line>rid myself of. Time and time again, as I have stood in the street in</line>
<line>front of a building and have seen men and women passing in large</line>
<line>numbers into the audience room where I was to speak, I have felt</line>
<line>ashamed that I should be the cause of people—as it seemed to me—wasting</line>
<line>a valuable hour of their time. Some years ago I was to deliver an</line>
<line>address before a literary society in Madison, Wis. An hour before the</line>
<line>time set for me to speak, a fierce snow-storm began, and continued for</line>
<line>several hours. I made up my mind that there would be no audience, and</line>
<line>that I should not have to speak, but, as a matter of duty, I went to</line>
<line>the church, and found it packed with people. The surprise gave me a</line>
<line>shock that I did not recover from during the whole evening.</line>

<line>People often ask me if I feel nervous before speaking, or else they</line>
<line>suggest that, since I speak often, they suppose that I get used to it.</line>
<line>In answer to this question I have to say that I always suffer intensely</line>
<line>from nervousness before speaking. More than once, just before I was to</line>
<line>make an important address, this nervous strain has been so great that I</line>
<line>have resolved never again to speak in public. I not only feel nervous</line>
<line>before speaking, but after I have finished I usually feel a sense of</line>
<line>regret, because it seems to me as if I had left out of my address the</line>
<line>main thing and the best thing that I had meant to say.</line>

<line>There is a great compensation, though, for this preliminary nervous</line>
<line>suffering, that comes to me after I have been speaking for about ten</line>
<line>minutes, and have come to feel that I have really mastered my audience,</line>
<line>and that we have gotten into full and complete sympathy with each</line>
<line>other. It seems to me that there is rarely such a combination of mental</line>
<line>and physical delight in any effort as that which comes to a public</line>
<line>speaker when he feels that he has a great audience completely within</line>
<line>his control. There is a thread of sympathy and oneness that connects a</line>
<line>public speaker with his audience, that is just as strong as though it</line>
<line>was something tangible and visible. If in an audience of a thousand</line>
<line>people there is one person who is not in sympathy with my views, or is</line>
<line>inclined to be doubtful, cold, or critical, I can pick him out. When I</line>
<line>have found him I usually go straight at him, and it is a great</line>
<line>satisfaction to watch the process of his thawing out. I find that the</line>
<line>most effective medicine for such individuals is administered at first</line>
<line>in the form of a story, although I never tell an anecdote simply for</line>
<line>the sake of telling one. That kind of thing, I think, is empty and</line>
<line>hollow, and an audience soon finds it out.</line>

<line>I believe that one always does himself and his audience an injustice</line>
<line>when he speaks merely for the sake of speaking. I do not believe that</line>
<line>one should speak unless, deep down in his heart, he feels convinced</line>
<line>that he has a message to deliver. When one feels, from the bottom of</line>
<line>his feet to the top of his head, that he has something to say that is</line>
<line>going to help some individual or some cause, then let him say it; and</line>
<line>in delivering his message I do not believe that many of the artificial</line>
<line>rules of elocution can, under such circumstances, help him very much.</line>
<line>Although there are certain things, such as pauses, breathing, and pitch</line>
<line>of voice, that are very important, none of these can take the place of</line>
<line>soul in an address. When I have an address to deliver, I like to forget</line>
<line>all about the rules for the proper use of the English language, and all</line>
<line>about rhetoric and that sort of thing, and I like to make the audience</line>
<line>forget all about these things, too.</line>

<line>Nothing tends to throw me off my balance so quickly, when I am</line>
<line>speaking, as to have some one leave the room. To prevent this, I make</line>
<line>up my mind, as a rule, that I will try to make my address so</line>
<line>interesting, will try to state so many interesting facts one after</line>
<line>another, that no one can leave. The average audience, I have come to</line>
<line>believe, wants facts rather than generalities or sermonizing. Most</line>
<line>people, I think, are able to draw proper conclusions if they are given</line>
<line>the facts in an interesting form on which to base them.</line>

<line>As to the kind of audience that I like best to talk to, I would put at</line>
<line>the top of the list an organization of strong, wide-awake, business</line>
<line>men, such, for example, as is found in Boston, New York, Chicago, and</line>
<line>Buffalo. I have found no other audience so quick to see a point, and so</line>
<line>responsive. Within the last few years I have had the privilege of</line>
<line>speaking before most of the leading organizations of this kind in the</line>
<line>large cities of the United States. The best time to get hold of an</line>
<line>organization of business men is after a good dinner, although I think</line>
<line>that one of the worst instruments of torture that was ever invented is</line>
<line>the custom which makes it necessary for a speaker to sit through a</line>
<line>fourteen-course dinner, every minute of the time feeling sure that his</line>
<line>speech is going to prove a dismal failure and disappointment.</line>

<line>I rarely take part in one of these long dinners that I do not wish that</line>
<line>I could put myself back in the little cabin where I was a slave boy,</line>
<line>and again go through the experience there—one that I shall never</line>
<line>forget—of getting molasses to eat once a week from the “big house.” Our</line>
<line>usual diet on the plantation was corn bread and pork, but on Sunday</line>
<line>morning my mother was permitted to bring down a little molasses from</line>
<line>the “big house” for her three children, and when it was received how I</line>
<line>did wish that every day was Sunday! I would get my tin plate and hold</line>
<line>it up for the sweet morsel, but I would always shut my eyes while the</line>
<line>molasses was being poured out into the plate, with the hope that when I</line>
<line>opened them I would be surprised to see how much I had got. When I</line>
<line>opened my eyes I would tip the plate in one direction and another, so</line>
<line>as to make the molasses spread all over it, in the full belief that</line>
<line>there would be more of it and that it would last longer if spread out</line>
<line>in this way. So strong are my childish impressions of those Sunday</line>
<line>morning feasts that it would be pretty hard for any one to convince me</line>
<line>that there is not more molasses on a plate when it is spread all over</line>
<line>the plate than when it occupies a little corner—if there is a corner in</line>
<line>a plate. At any rate, I have never believed in “cornering” syrup. My</line>
<line>share of the syrup was usually about two tablespoonfuls, and those two</line>
<line>spoonfuls of molasses were much more enjoyable to me than is a</line>
<line>fourteen-course dinner after which I am to speak.</line>

<line>Next to a company of business men, I prefer to speak to an audience of</line>
<line>Southern people, of either race, together or taken separately. Their</line>
<line>enthusiasm and responsiveness are a constant delight. The “amens” and</line>
<line>“dat’s de truf” that come spontaneously from the coloured individuals</line>
<line>are calculated to spur any speaker on to his best efforts. I think that</line>
<line>next in order of preference I would place a college audience. It has</line>
<line>been my privilege to deliver addresses at many of our leading colleges</line>
<line>including Harvard, Yale, Williams, Amherst, Fisk University, the</line>
<line>University of Pennsylvania, Wellesley, the University of Michigan,</line>
<line>Trinity College in North Carolina, and many others.</line>

<line>It has been a matter of deep interest to me to note the number of</line>
<line>people who have come to shake hands with me after an address, who say</line>
<line>that this is the first time they have ever called a Negro “Mister.”</line>

<line>When speaking directly in the interests of the Tuskegee Institute, I</line>
<line>usually arrange, some time in advance, a series of meetings in</line>
<line>important centres. This takes me before churches, Sunday-schools,</line>
<line>Christian Endeavour Societies, and men’s and women’s clubs. When doing</line>
<line>this I sometimes speak before as many as four organizations in a single</line>
<line>day.</line>

<line>Three years ago, at the suggestion of Mr. Morris K. Jessup, of New</line>
<line>York, and Dr. J.L.M. Curry, the general agent of the fund, the trustees</line>
<line>of the John F. Slater Fund voted a sum of money to be used in paying</line>
<line>the expenses of Mrs. Washington and myself while holding a series of</line>
<line>meetings among the coloured people in the large centres of Negro</line>
<line>population, especially in the large cities of the ex-slaveholding</line>
<line>states. Each year during the last three years we have devoted some</line>
<line>weeks to this work. The plan that we have followed has been for me to</line>
<line>speak in the morning to the ministers, teachers, and professional men.</line>
<line>In the afternoon Mrs. Washington would speak to the women alone, and in</line>
<line>the evening I spoke to a large mass-meeting. In almost every case the</line>
<line>meetings have been attended not only by the coloured people in large</line>
<line>numbers, but by the white people. In Chattanooga, Tenn., for example,</line>
<line>there was present at the mass-meeting an audience of not less than</line>
<line>three thousand persons, and I was informed that eight hundred of these</line>
<line>were white. I have done no work that I really enjoyed more than this,</line>
<line>or that I think has accomplished more good.</line>

<line>These meetings have given Mrs. Washington and myself an opportunity to</line>
<line>get first-hand, accurate information as to the real condition of the</line>
<line>race, by seeing the people in their homes, their churches, their</line>
<line>Sunday-schools, and their places of work, as well as in the prisons and</line>
<line>dens of crime. These meetings also gave us an opportunity to see the</line>
<line>relations that exist between the races. I never feel so hopeful about</line>
<line>the race as I do after being engaged in a series of these meetings. I</line>
<line>know that on such occasions there is much that comes to the surface</line>
<line>that is superficial and deceptive, but I have had experience enough not</line>
<line>to be deceived by mere signs and fleeting enthusiasms. I have taken</line>
<line>pains to go to the bottom of things and get facts, in a cold,</line>
<line>business-like manner.</line>

<line>I have seen the statement made lately, by one who claims to know what</line>
<line>he is talking about, that, taking the whole Negro race into account,</line>
<line>ninety per cent of the Negro women are not virtuous. There never was a</line>
<line>baser falsehood uttered concerning a race, or a statement made that was</line>
<line>less capable of being proved by actual facts.</line>

<line>No one can come into contact with the race for twenty years, as I have</line>
<line>done in the heart of the South, without being convinced that the race</line>
<line>is constantly making slow but sure progress materially, educationally,</line>
<line>and morally. One might take up the life of the worst element in New</line>
<line>York City, for example, and prove almost anything he wanted to prove</line>
<line>concerning the white man, but all will agree that this is not a fair</line>
<line>test.</line>

<line>Early in the year 1897 I received a letter inviting me to deliver an</line>
<line>address at the dedication of the Robert Gould Shaw monument in Boston.</line>
<line>I accepted the invitation. It is not necessary for me, I am sure, to</line>
<line>explain who Robert Gould Shaw was, and what he did. The monument to his</line>
<line>memory stands near the head of the Boston Common, facing the State</line>
<line>House. It is counted to be the most perfect piece of art of the kind to</line>
<line>be found in the country.</line>

<line>The exercises connected with the dedication were held in Music Hall, in</line>
<line>Boston, and the great hall was packed from top to bottom with one of</line>
<line>the most distinguished audiences that ever assembled in the city. Among</line>
<line>those present were more persons representing the famous old</line>
<line>anti-slavery element that it is likely will ever be brought together in</line>
<line>the country again. The late Hon. Roger Wolcott, then Governor of</line>
<line>Massachusetts, was the presiding officer, and on the platform with him</line>
<line>were many other officials and hundreds of distinguished men. A report</line>
<line>of the meeting which appeared in the Boston Transcript will describe it</line>
<line>better than any words of mine could do:—</line>

<line>The core and kernel of yesterday’s great noon meeting, in honour of the</line>
<line>Brotherhood of Man, in Music Hall, was the superb address of the Negro</line>
<line>President of Tuskegee. “Booker T. Washington received his Harvard A.M.</line>
<line>last June, the first of his race,” said Governor Wolcott, “to receive</line>
<line>an honorary degree from the oldest university in the land, and this for</line>
<line>the wise leadership of his people.” When Mr. Washington rose in the</line>
<line>flag-filled, enthusiasm-warmed, patriotic, and glowing atmosphere of</line>
<line>Music Hall, people felt keenly that here was the civic justification of</line>
<line>the old abolition spirit of Massachusetts; in his person the proof of</line>
<line>her ancient and indomitable faith; in his strong thought and rich</line>
<line>oratory, the crown and glory of the old war days of suffering and</line>
<line>strife. The scene was full of historic beauty and deep significance.</line>
<line>“Cold” Boston was alive with the fire that is always hot in her heart</line>
<line>for righteousness and truth. Rows and rows of people who are seldom</line>
<line>seen at any public function, whole families of those who are certain to</line>
<line>be out of town on a holiday, crowded the place to overflowing. The city</line>
<line>was at her birthright _fête_ in the persons of hundreds of her best</line>
<line>citizens, men and women whose names and lives stand for the virtues</line>
<line>that make for honourable civic pride.</line>

<line>Battle-music had filled the air. Ovation after ovation, applause warm</line>
<line>and prolonged, had greeted the officers and friends of Colonel Shaw,</line>
<line>the sculptor, St. Gaudens, the memorial Committee, the Governor and his</line>
<line>staff, and the Negro soldiers of the Fifty-fourth Massachusetts as they</line>
<line>came upon the platform or entered the hall. Colonel Henry Lee, of</line>
<line>Governor Andrew’s old staff, had made a noble, simple presentation</line>
<line>speech for the committee, paying tribute to Mr. John M. Forbes, in</line>
<line>whose stead he served. Governor Wolcott had made his short, memorable</line>
<line>speech, saying, “Fort Wagner marked an epoch in the history of a race,</line>
<line>and called it into manhood.” Mayor Quincy had received the monument for</line>
<line>the city of Boston. The story of Colonel Shaw and his black regiment</line>
<line>had been told in gallant words, and then, after the singing of</line>

<line>Mine eyes have seen the glory</line>
<line>Of the coming of the Lord,</line>

<line>Booker Washington arose. It was, of course, just the moment for him.</line>
<line>The multitude, shaken out of its usual symphony-concert calm, quivered</line>
<line>with an excitement that was not suppressed. A dozen times it had sprung</line>
<line>to its feet to cheer and wave and hurrah, as one person. When this man</line>
<line>of culture and voice and power, as well as a dark skin, began, and</line>
<line>uttered the names of Stearns and of Andrew, feeling began to mount. You</line>
<line>could see tears glisten in the eyes of soldiers and civilians. When the</line>
<line>orator turned to the coloured soldiers on the platform, to the</line>
<line>colour-bearer of Fort Wagner, who smilingly bore still the flag he had</line>
<line>never lowered even when wounded, and said, “To you, to the scarred and</line>
<line>scattered remnants of the Fifty-fourth, who, with empty sleeve and</line>
<line>wanting leg, have honoured this occasion with your presence, to you,</line>
<line>your commander is not dead. Though Boston erected no monument and</line>
<line>history recorded no story, in you and in the loyal race which you</line>
<line>represent, Robert Gould Shaw would have a monument which time could not</line>
