<line>wear away,” then came the climax of the emotion of the day and the</line>
<line>hour. It was Roger Wolcott, as well as the Governor of Massachusetts,</line>
<line>the individual representative of the people’s sympathy as well as the</line>
<line>chief magistrate, who had sprung first to his feet and cried, “Three</line>
<line>cheers to Booker T. Washington!”</line>

<line>Among those on the platform was Sergeant William H. Carney, of New</line>
<line>Bedford, Mass., the brave coloured officer who was the colour-bearer at</line>
<line>Fort Wagner and held the American flag. In spite of the fact that a</line>
<line>large part of his regiment was killed, he escaped, and exclaimed, after</line>
<line>the battle was over, “The old flag never touched the ground.”</line>

<line>This flag Sergeant Carney held in his hands as he sat on the platform,</line>
<line>and when I turned to address the survivors of the coloured regiment who</line>
<line>were present, and referred to Sergeant Carney, he rose, as if by</line>
<line>instinct, and raised the flag. It has been my privilege to witness a</line>
<line>good many satisfactory and rather sensational demonstrations in</line>
<line>connection with some of my public addresses, but in dramatic effect I</line>
<line>have never seen or experienced anything which equalled this. For a</line>
<line>number of minutes the audience seemed to entirely lose control of</line>
<line>itself.</line>

<line>In the general rejoicing throughout the country which followed the</line>
<line>close of the Spanish-American war, peace celebrations were arranged in</line>
<line>several of the large cities. I was asked by President William R.</line>
<line>Harper, of the University of Chicago, who was chairman of the committee</line>
<line>of invitations for the celebration to be held in the city of Chicago,</line>
<line>to deliver one of the addresses at the celebration there. I accepted</line>
<line>the invitation, and delivered two addresses there during the Jubilee</line>
<line>week. The first of these, and the principal one, was given in the</line>
<line>Auditorium, on the evening of Sunday, October 16. This was the largest</line>
<line>audience that I have ever addressed, in any part of the country; and</line>
<line>besides speaking in the main Auditorium, I also addressed, that same</line>
<line>evening, two overflow audiences in other parts of the city.</line>

<line>It was said that there were sixteen thousand persons in the Auditorium,</line>
<line>and it seemed to me as if there were as many more on the outside trying</line>
<line>to get in. It was impossible for any one to get near the entrance</line>
<line>without the aid of a policeman. President William McKinley attended</line>
<line>this meeting, as did also the members of his Cabinet, many foreign</line>
<line>ministers, and a large number of army and navy officers, many of whom</line>
<line>had distinguished themselves in the war which had just closed. The</line>
<line>speakers, besides myself, on Sunday evening, were Rabbi Emil G. Hirsch,</line>
<line>Father Thomas P. Hodnett, and Dr. John H. Barrows.</line>

<line>The Chicago Times-Herald, in describing the meeting, said of my</line>
<line>address:—</line>

<line>He pictured the Negro choosing slavery rather than extinction; recalled</line>
<line>Crispus Attucks shedding his blood at the beginning of the American</line>
<line>Revolution, that white Americans might be free, while black Americans</line>
<line>remained in slavery; rehearsed the conduct of the Negroes with Jackson</line>
<line>at New Orleans; drew a vivid and pathetic picture of the Southern</line>
<line>slaves protecting and supporting the families of their masters while</line>
<line>the latter were fighting to perpetuate black slavery; recounted the</line>
<line>bravery of coloured troops at Port Hudson and Forts Wagner and Pillow,</line>
<line>and praised the heroism of the black regiments that stormed El Caney</line>
<line>and Santiago to give freedom to the enslaved people of Cuba,</line>
<line>forgetting, for the time being, the unjust discrimination that law and</line>
<line>custom make against them in their own country.</line>

<line>In all of these things, the speaker declared, his race had chosen the</line>
<line>better part. And then he made his eloquent appeal to the consciences of</line>
<line>the white Americans: “When you have gotten the full story of the heroic</line>
<line>conduct of the Negro in the Spanish-American war, have heard it from</line>
<line>the lips of Northern soldier and Southern soldier, from ex-abolitionist</line>
<line>and ex-masters, then decide within yourselves whether a race that is</line>
<line>thus willing to die for its country should not be given the highest</line>
<line>opportunity to live for its country.”</line>

<line>The part of the speech which seems to arouse the wildest and most</line>
<line>sensational enthusiasm was that in which I thanked the President for</line>
<line>his recognition of the Negro in his appointments during the</line>
<line>Spanish-American war. The President was sitting in a box at the right</line>
<line>of the stage. When I addressed him I turned toward the box, and as I</line>
<line>finished the sentence thanking him for his generosity, the whole</line>
<line>audience rose and cheered again and again, waving handkerchiefs and</line>
<line>hats and canes, until the President arose in the box and bowed his</line>
<line>acknowledgements. At that the enthusiasm broke out again, and the</line>
<line>demonstration was almost indescribable.</line>

<line>One portion of my address at Chicago seemed to have been misunderstood</line>
<line>by the Southern press, and some of the Southern papers took occasion to</line>
<line>criticise me rather strongly. These criticisms continued for several</line>
<line>weeks, until I finally received a letter from the editor of the</line>
<line>Age-Herald, published in Birmingham, Ala., asking me if I would say</line>
<line>just what I meant by this part of the address. I replied to him in a</line>
<line>letter which seemed to satisfy my critics. In this letter I said that I</line>
<line>had made it a rule never to say before a Northern audience anything</line>
<line>that I would not say before an audience in the South. I said that I did</line>
<line>not think it was necessary for me to go into extended explanations; if</line>
<line>my seventeen years of work in the heart of the South had not been</line>
<line>explanation enough, I did not see how words could explain. I said that</line>
<line>I made the same plea that I had made in my address at Atlanta, for the</line>
<line>blotting out of race prejudice in “commercial and civil relations.” I</line>
<line>said that what is termed social recognition was a question which I</line>
<line>never discussed, and then I quoted from my Atlanta address what I had</line>
<line>said there in regard to that subject.</line>

<line>In meeting crowds of people at public gatherings, there is one type of</line>
<line>individual that I dread. I mean the crank. I have become so accustomed</line>
<line>to these people now that I can pick them out at a distance when I see</line>
<line>them elbowing their way up to me. The average crank has a long beard,</line>
<line>poorly cared for, a lean, narrow face, and wears a black coat. The</line>
<line>front of his vest and coat are slick with grease, and his trousers bag</line>
<line>at the knees.</line>

<line>In Chicago, after I had spoken at a meeting, I met one of these</line>
<line>fellows. They usually have some process for curing all of the ills of</line>
<line>the world at once. This Chicago specimen had a patent process by which</line>
<line>he said Indian corn could be kept through a period of three or four</line>
<line>years, and he felt sure that if the Negro race in the South would, as a</line>
<line>whole, adopt his process, it would settle the whole race question. It</line>
<line>mattered nothing that I tried to convince him that our present problem</line>
<line>was to teach the Negroes how to produce enough corn to last them</line>
<line>through one year. Another Chicago crank had a scheme by which he wanted</line>
<line>me to join him in an effort to close up all the National banks in the</line>
<line>country. If that was done, he felt sure it would put the Negro on his</line>
<line>feet.</line>

<line>The number of people who stand ready to consume one’s time, to no</line>
<line>purpose, is almost countless. At one time I spoke before a large</line>
<line>audience in Boston in the evening. The next morning I was awakened by</line>
<line>having a card brought to my room, and with it a message that some one</line>
<line>was anxious to see me. Thinking that it must be something very</line>
<line>important, I dressed hastily and went down. When I reached the hotel</line>
<line>office I found a blank and innocent-looking individual waiting for me,</line>
<line>who coolly remarked: “I heard you talk at a meeting last night. I</line>
<line>rather liked your talk, and so I came in this morning to hear you talk</line>
<line>some more.”</line>

<line>I am often asked how it is possible for me to superintend the work at</line>
<line>Tuskegee and at the same time be so much away from the school. In</line>
<line>partial answer to this I would say that I think I have learned, in some</line>
<line>degree at least, to disregard the old maxim which says, “Do not get</line>
<line>others to do that which you can do yourself.” My motto, on the other</line>
<line>hand, is, “Do not do that which others can do as well.”</line>

<line>One of the most encouraging signs in connection with the Tuskegee</line>
<line>school is found in the fact that the organization is so thorough that</line>
<line>the daily work of the school is not dependent upon the presence of any</line>
<line>one individual. The whole executive force, including instructors and</line>
<line>clerks, now numbers eighty-six. This force is so organized and</line>
<line>subdivided that the machinery of the school goes on day by day like</line>
<line>clockwork. Most of our teachers have been connected with the</line>
<line>institutions for a number of years, and are as much interested in it as</line>
<line>I am. In my absence, Mr. Warren Logan, the treasurer, who has been at</line>
<line>the school seventeen years, is the executive. He is efficiently</line>
<line>supported by Mrs. Washington, and by my faithful secretary, Mr. Emmett</line>
<line>J. Scott, who handles the bulk of my correspondence and keeps me in</line>
<line>daily touch with the life of the school, and who also keeps me informed</line>
<line>of whatever takes place in the South that concerns the race. I owe more</line>
<line>to his tact, wisdom, and hard work than I can describe.</line>

<line>The main executive work of the school, whether I am at Tuskegee or not,</line>
<line>centres in what we call the executive council. This council meets twice</line>
<line>a week, and is composed of the nine persons who are at the head of the</line>
<line>nine departments of the school. For example: Mrs. B.K. Bruce, the Lady</line>
<line>Principal, the widow of the late ex-senator Bruce, is a member of the</line>
<line>council, and represents in it all that pertains to the life of the</line>
<line>girls at the school. In addition to the executive council there is a</line>
<line>financial committee of six, that meets every week and decides upon the</line>
<line>expenditures for the week. Once a month, and sometimes oftener, there</line>
<line>is a general meeting of all the instructors. Aside from these there are</line>
<line>innumerable smaller meetings, such as that of the instructors in the</line>
<line>Phelps Hall Bible Training School, or of the instructors in the</line>
<line>agricultural department.</line>

<line>In order that I may keep in constant touch with the life of the</line>
<line>institution, I have a system of reports so arranged that a record of</line>
<line>the school’s work reaches me every day of the year, no matter in what</line>
<line>part of the country I am. I know by these reports even what students</line>
<line>are excused from school, and why they are excused—whether for reasons</line>
<line>of ill health or otherwise. Through the medium of these reports I know</line>
<line>each day what the income of the school in money is; I know how many</line>
<line>gallons of milk and how many pounds of butter come from the dairy; what</line>
<line>the bill of fare for the teachers and students is; whether a certain</line>
<line>kind of meat was boiled or baked, and whether certain vegetables served</line>
<line>in the dining room were bought from a store or procured from our own</line>
<line>farm. Human nature I find to be very much the same the world over, and</line>
<line>it is sometimes not hard to yield to the temptation to go to a barrel</line>
<line>of rice that has come from the store—with the grain all prepared to go</line>
<line>in the pot—rather than to take the time and trouble to go to the field</line>
<line>and dig and wash one’s own sweet potatoes, which might be prepared in a</line>
<line>manner to take the place of the rice.</line>

<line>I am often asked how, in the midst of so much work, a large part of</line>
<line>which is for the public, I can find time for any rest or recreation,</line>
<line>and what kind of recreation or sports I am fond of. This is rather a</line>
<line>difficult question to answer. I have a strong feeling that every</line>
<line>individual owes it to himself, and to the cause which he is serving, to</line>
<line>keep a vigorous, healthy body, with the nerves steady and strong,</line>
<line>prepared for great efforts and prepared for disappointments and trying</line>
<line>positions. As far as I can, I make it a rule to plan for each day’s</line>
<line>work—not merely to go through with the same routine of daily duties,</line>
<line>but to get rid of the routine work as early in the day as possible, and</line>
<line>then to enter upon some new or advance work. I make it a rule to clear</line>
<line>my desk every day, before leaving my office, of all correspondence and</line>
<line>memoranda, so that on the morrow I can begin a _new_ day of work. I</line>
<line>make it a rule never to let my work drive me, but to so master it, and</line>
<line>keep it in such complete control, and to keep so far ahead of it, that</line>
<line>I will be the master instead of the servant. There is a physical and</line>
<line>mental and spiritual enjoyment that comes from a consciousness of being</line>
<line>the absolute master of one’s work, in all its details, that is very</line>
<line>satisfactory and inspiring. My experience teaches me that, if one</line>
<line>learns to follow this plan, he gets a freshness of body and vigour of</line>
<line>mind out of work that goes a long way toward keeping him strong and</line>
<line>healthy. I believe that when one can grow to the point where he loves</line>
<line>his work, this gives him a kind of strength that is most valuable.</line>

<line>When I begin my work in the morning, I expect to have a successful and</line>
<line>pleasant day of it, but at the same time I prepare myself for</line>
<line>unpleasant and unexpected hard places. I prepared myself to hear that</line>
<line>one of our school buildings is on fire, or has burned, or that some</line>
<line>disagreeable accident has occurred, or that some one has abused me in a</line>
<line>public address or printed article, for something that I have done or</line>
<line>omitted to do, or for something that he had heard that I had</line>
<line>said—probably something that I had never thought of saying.</line>

<line>In nineteen years of continuous work I have taken but one vacation.</line>
<line>That was two years ago, when some of my friends put the money into my</line>
<line>hands and forced Mrs. Washington and myself to spend three months in</line>
<line>Europe. I have said that I believe it is the duty of every one to keep</line>
<line>his body in good condition. I try to look after the little ills, with</line>
<line>the idea that if I take care of the little ills the big ones will not</line>
<line>come. When I find myself unable to sleep well, I know that something is</line>
<line>wrong. If I find any part of my system the least weak, and not</line>
<line>performing its duty, I consult a good physician. The ability to sleep</line>
<line>well, at any time and in any place, I find of great advantage. I have</line>
<line>so trained myself that I can lie down for a nap of fifteen or twenty</line>
<line>minutes, and get up refreshed in body and mind.</line>

<line>I have said that I make it a rule to finish up each day’s work before</line>
<line>leaving it. There is, perhaps, one exception to this. When I have an</line>
<line>unusually difficult question to decide—one that appeals strongly to the</line>
<line>emotions—I find it a safe rule to sleep over it for a night, or to wait</line>
<line>until I have had an opportunity to talk it over with my wife and</line>
<line>friends.</line>

<line>As to my reading; the most time I get for solid reading is when I am on</line>
<line>the cars. Newspapers are to me a constant source of delight and</line>
<line>recreation. The only trouble is that I read too many of them. Fiction I</line>
<line>care little for. Frequently I have to almost force myself to read a</line>
<line>novel that is on every one’s lips. The kind of reading that I have the</line>
<line>greatest fondness for is biography. I like to be sure that I am reading</line>
<line>about a real man or a real thing. I think I do not go too far when I</line>
<line>say that I have read nearly every book and magazine article that has</line>
<line>been written about Abraham Lincoln. In literature he is my patron</line>
<line>saint.</line>

<line>Out of the twelve months in a year I suppose that, on an average, I</line>
<line>spend six months away from Tuskegee. While my being absent from the</line>
<line>school so much unquestionably has its disadvantages, yet there are at</line>
<line>the same time some compensations. The change of work brings a certain</line>
<line>kind of rest. I enjoy a ride of a long distance on the cars, when I am</line>
<line>permitted to ride where I can be comfortable. I get rest on the cars,</line>
<line>except when the inevitable individual who seems to be on every train</line>
<line>approaches me with the now familiar phrase: “Isn’t this Booker</line>
<line>Washington? I want to introduce myself to you.” Absence from the school</line>
<line>enables me to lose sight of the unimportant details of the work, and</line>
<line>study it in a broader and more comprehensive manner than I could do on</line>
<line>the grounds. This absence also brings me into contact with the best</line>
<line>work being done in educational lines, and into contact with the best</line>
<line>educators in the land.</line>

<line>But, after all this is said, the time when I get the most solid rest</line>
<line>and recreation is when I can be at Tuskegee, and, after our evening</line>
<line>meal is over, can sit down, as is our custom, with my wife and Portia</line>
<line>and Baker and Davidson, my three children, and read a story, or each</line>
<line>take turns in telling a story. To me there is nothing on earth equal to</line>
<line>that, although what is nearly equal to it is to go with them for an</line>
<line>hour or more, as we like to do on Sunday afternoons, into the woods,</line>
<line>where we can live for a while near the heart of nature, where no one</line>
<line>can disturb or vex us, surrounded by pure air, the trees, the</line>
<line>shrubbery, the flowers, and the sweet fragrance that springs from a</line>
<line>hundred plants, enjoying the chirp of the crickets and the songs of the</line>
<line>birds. This is solid rest.</line>

<line>My garden, also, what little time I can be at Tuskegee, is another</line>
<line>source of rest and enjoyment. Somehow I like, as often as possible, to</line>
<line>touch nature, not something that is artificial or an imitation, but the</line>
<line>real thing. When I can leave my office in time so that I can spend</line>
<line>thirty or forty minutes in spading the ground, in planting seeds, in</line>
<line>digging about the plants, I feel that I am coming into contact with</line>
<line>something that is giving me strength for the many duties and hard</line>
<line>places that await me out in the big world. I pity the man or woman who</line>
<line>has never learned to enjoy nature and to get strength and inspiration</line>
<line>out of it.</line>

<line>Aside from the large number of fowls and animals kept by the school, I</line>
<line>keep individually a number of pigs and fowls of the best grades, and in</line>
<line>raising these I take a great deal of pleasure. I think the pig is my</line>
<line>favourite animal. Few things are more satisfactory to me than a</line>
<line>high-grade Berkshire or Poland China pig.</line>

<line>Games I care little for. I have never seen a game of football. In cards</line>
<line>I do not know one card from another. A game of old-fashioned marbles</line>
<line>with my two boys, once in a while, is all I care for in this direction.</line>
<line>I suppose I would care for games now if I had had any time in my youth</line>
<line>to give to them, but that was not possible.</line>




</chapter>

<chapter>
<title>Chapter XVI. Europe</title>


<line>In 1893 I was married to Miss Margaret James Murray, a native of</line>
<line>Mississippi, and a graduate of Fisk University, in Nashville, Tenn.,</line>
<line>who had come to Tuskegee as a teacher several years before, and at the</line>
<line>time we were married was filling the position of Lady Principal. Not</line>
<line>only is Mrs. Washington completely one with me in the work directly</line>
<line>connected with the school, relieving me of many burdens and</line>
<line>perplexities, but aside from her work on the school grounds, she</line>
<line>carries on a mothers’ meeting in the town of Tuskegee, and a plantation</line>
<line>work among the women, children, and men who live in a settlement</line>
<line>connected with a large plantation about eight miles from Tuskegee. Both</line>
<line>the mothers’ meeting and the plantation work are carried on, not only</line>
<line>with a view to helping those who are directly reached, but also for the</line>
<line>purpose of furnishing object-lessons in these two kinds of work that</line>
<line>may be followed by our students when they go out into the world for</line>
<line>their own life-work.</line>

<line>Aside from these two enterprises, Mrs. Washington is also largely</line>
<line>responsible for a woman’s club at the school which brings together,</line>
<line>twice a month, the women who live on the school grounds and those who</line>
<line>live near, for the discussion of some important topic. She is also the</line>
<line>President of what is known as the Federation of Southern Coloured</line>
<line>Women’s Clubs, and is Chairman of the Executive Committee of the</line>
<line>National Federation of Coloured Women’s Clubs.</line>

<line>Portia, the oldest of my three children, has learned dressmaking. She</line>
<line>has unusual ability in instrumental music. Aside from her studies at</line>
<line>Tuskegee, she has already begun to teach there.</line>

<line>Booker Taliaferro is my next oldest child. Young as he is, he has</line>
<line>already nearly mastered the brickmason’s trade. He began working at</line>
<line>this trade when he was quite small, dividing his time between this and</line>
<line>class work; and he has developed great skill in the trade and a</line>
<line>fondness for it. He says that he is going to be an architect and</line>
<line>brickmason. One of the most satisfactory letters that I have ever</line>
<line>received from any one came to me from Booker last summer. When I left</line>
<line>home for the summer, I told him that he must work at his trade half of</line>
<line>each day, and that the other half of the day he could spend as he</line>
<line>pleased. When I had been away from home two weeks, I received the</line>
<line>following letter from him:</line>

<line>Tuskegee, Alabama.</line>

<line>My dear Papa: Before you left home you told me to work at my trade half</line>
<line>of each day. I like my work so much that I want to work at my trade all</line>
<line>day. Besides, I want to earn all the money I can, so that when I go to</line>
<line>another school I shall have money to pay my expenses.</line>

<line>Your son,</line>

<line>Booker.</line>

<line>My youngest child, Ernest Davidson Washington, says that he is going to</line>
<line>be a physician. In addition to going to school, where he studies books</line>
<line>and has manual training, he regularly spends a portion of his time in</line>
<line>the office of our resident physician, and has already learned to do</line>
<line>many of the duties which pertain to a doctor’s office.</line>

<line>The thing in my life which brings me the keenest regret is that my work</line>
<line>in connection with public affairs keeps me for so much of the time away</line>
<line>from my family, where, of all places in the world, I delight to be. I</line>
<line>always envy the individual whose life-work is so laid that he can spend</line>
<line>his evenings at home. I have sometimes thought that people who have</line>
<line>this rare privilege do not appreciate it as they should. It is such a</line>
<line>rest and relief to get away from crowds of people, and handshaking, and</line>
<line>travelling, to get home, even if it be for but a very brief while.</line>

<line>Another thing at Tuskegee out of which I get a great deal of pleasure</line>
<line>and satisfaction is in the meeting with our students, and teachers, and</line>
<line>their families, in the chapel for devotional exercises every evening at</line>
<line>half-past eight, the last thing before retiring for the night. It is an</line>
<line>inspiring sight when one stands on the platform there and sees before</line>
<line>him eleven or twelve hundred earnest young men and women; and one</line>
<line>cannot but feel that it is a privilege to help to guide them to a</line>
<line>higher and more useful life.</line>

<line>In the spring of 1899 there came to me what I might describe as almost</line>
<line>the greatest surprise of my life. Some good ladies in Boston arranged a</line>
<line>public meeting in the interests of Tuskegee, to be held in the Hollis</line>
<line>Street Theatre. This meeting was attended by large numbers of the best</line>
<line>people of Boston, of both races. Bishop Lawrence presided. In addition</line>
<line>to an address made by myself, Mr. Paul Lawrence Dunbar read from his</line>
<line>poems, and Dr. W.E.B. Du Bois read an original sketch.</line>

<line>Some of those who attended this meeting noticed that I seemed unusually</line>
<line>tired, and some little time after the close of the meeting, one of the</line>
<line>ladies who had been interested in it asked me in a casual way if I had</line>
<line>ever been to Europe. I replied that I never had. She asked me if I had</line>
<line>ever thought of going, and I told her no; that it was something</line>
<line>entirely beyond me. This conversation soon passed out of my mind, but a</line>
<line>few days afterward I was informed that some friends in Boston,</line>
<line>including Mr. Francis J. Garrison, had raised a sum of money sufficient</line>
<line>to pay all the expenses of Mrs. Washington and myself during a three or</line>
<line>four months’ trip to Europe. It was added with emphasis that we _must_</line>
<line>go. A year previous to this Mr. Garrison had attempted to get me to</line>
<line>promise to go to Europe for a summer’s rest, with the understanding</line>
<line>that he would be responsible for raising the money among his friends</line>
<line>for the expenses of the trip. At that time such a journey seemed so</line>
<line>entirely foreign to anything that I should ever be able to undertake</line>
<line>that I did confess I did not give the matter very serious attention;</line>
<line>but later Mr. Garrison joined his efforts to those of the ladies whom I</line>
<line>have mentioned, and when their plans were made known to me Mr. Garrison</line>
<line>not only had the route mapped out, but had, I believe, selected the</line>
<line>steamer upon which we were to sail.</line>

<line>The whole thing was so sudden and so unexpected that I was completely</line>
<line>taken off my feet. I had been at work steadily for eighteen years in</line>
<line>connection with Tuskegee, and I had never thought of anything else but</line>
<line>ending my life in that way. Each day the school seemed to depend upon</line>
<line>me more largely for its daily expenses, and I told these Boston friends</line>
<line>that, while I thanked them sincerely for their thoughtfulness and</line>
<line>generosity, I could not go to Europe, for the reason that the school</line>
<line>could not live financially while I was absent. They then informed me</line>
<line>that Mr. Henry L. Higginson, and some other good friends who I know do</line>
<line>not want their names made public, were then raising a sum of money</line>
<line>which would be sufficient to keep the school in operation while I was</line>
<line>away. At this point I was compelled to surrender. Every avenue of</line>
<line>escape had been closed.</line>

<line>Deep down in my heart the whole thing seemed more like a dream than</line>
<line>like reality, and for a long time it was difficult for me to make</line>
<line>myself believe that I was actually going to Europe. I had been born and</line>
<line>largely reared in the lowest depths of slavery, ignorance, and poverty.</line>
<line>In my childhood I had suffered for want of a place to sleep, for lack</line>
<line>of food, clothing, and shelter. I had not had the privilege of sitting</line>
<line>down to a dining-table until I was quite well grown. Luxuries had</line>
<line>always seemed to me to be something meant for white people, not for my</line>
<line>race. I had always regarded Europe, and London, and Paris, much as I</line>
<line>regarded heaven. And now could it be that I was actually going to</line>
<line>Europe? Such thoughts as these were constantly with me.</line>

<line>Two other thoughts troubled me a good deal. I feared that people who</line>
<line>heard that Mrs. Washington and I were going to Europe might not know</line>
<line>all the circumstances, and might get the idea that we had become, as</line>
<line>some might say, “stuck up,” and were trying to “show off.” I recalled</line>
<line>that from my youth I had heard it said that too often, when people of</line>
<line>my race reached any degree of success, they were inclined to unduly</line>
<line>exalt themselves; to try and ape the wealthy, and in so doing to lose</line>
<line>their heads. The fear that people might think this of us haunted me a</line>
<line>good deal. Then, too, I could not see how my conscience would permit me</line>
<line>to spare the time from my work and be happy. It seemed mean and selfish</line>
<line>in me to be taking a vacation while others were at work, and while</line>
<line>there was so much that needed to be done. From the time I could</line>
<line>remember, I had always been at work, and I did not see how I could</line>
<line>spend three or four months in doing nothing. The fact was that I did</line>
<line>not know how to take a vacation.</line>

<line>Mrs. Washington had much the same difficulty in getting away, but she</line>
<line>was anxious to go because she thought that I needed the rest. There</line>
<line>were many important National questions bearing upon the life of the</line>
<line>race which were being agitated at that time, and this made it all the</line>
<line>harder for us to decide to go. We finally gave our Boston friends our</line>
<line>promise that we would go, and then they insisted that the date of our</line>
<line>departure be set as soon as possible. So we decided upon May 10. My</line>
<line>good friend Mr. Garrison kindly took charge of all the details</line>
<line>necessary for the success of the trip, and he, as well as other</line>
<line>friends, gave us a great number of letters of introduction to people in</line>
<line>France and England, and made other arrangements for our comfort and</line>
<line>convenience abroad. Good-bys were said at Tuskegee, and we were in New</line>
<line>York May 9, ready to sail the next day. Our daughter Portia, who was</line>
<line>then studying in South Framingham, Mass., came to New York to see us</line>
<line>off. Mr. Scott, my secretary, came with me to New York, in order that I</line>
<line>might clear up the last bit of business before I left. Other friends</line>
<line>also came to New York to see us off. Just before we went on board the</line>
<line>steamer another pleasant surprise came to us in the form of a letter</line>
<line>from two generous ladies, stating that they had decided to give us the</line>
<line>money with which to erect a new building to be used in properly housing</line>
<line>all our industries for girls at Tuskegee.</line>

<line>We were to sail on the Friesland, of the Red Star Line, and a beautiful</line>
<line>vessel she was. We went on board just before noon, the hour of sailing.</line>
<line>I had never before been on board a large ocean steamer, and the feeling</line>
<line>which took possession of me when I found myself there is rather hard to</line>
<line>describe. It was a feeling, I think, of awe mingled with delight. We</line>
<line>were agreeably surprised to find that the captain, as well as several</line>
<line>of the other officers, not only knew who we were, but was expecting us</line>
<line>and gave us a pleasant greeting. There were several passengers whom we</line>
<line>knew, including Senator Sewell, of New Jersey, and Edward Marshall, the</line>
<line>newspaper correspondent. I had just a little fear that we would not be</line>
<line>treated civilly by some of the passengers. This fear was based upon</line>
<line>what I had heard other people of my race, who had crossed the ocean,</line>
<line>say about unpleasant experiences in crossing the ocean in American</line>
<line>vessels. But in our case, from the captain down to the most humble</line>
<line>servant, we were treated with the greatest kindness. Nor was this</line>
<line>kindness confined to those who were connected with the steamer; it was</line>
<line>shown by all the passengers also. There were not a few Southern men and</line>
<line>women on board, and they were as cordial as those from other parts of</line>
<line>the country.</line>

<line>As soon as the last good-bys were said, and the steamer had cut loose</line>
<line>from the wharf, the load of care, anxiety, and responsibility which I</line>
<line>had carried for eighteen years began to lift itself from my shoulders</line>
<line>at the rate, it seemed to me, of a pound a minute. It was the first</line>
<line>time in all those years that I had felt, even in a measure, free from</line>
<line>care; and my feeling of relief it is hard to describe on paper. Added</line>
<line>to this was the delightful anticipation of being in Europe soon. It all</line>
<line>seemed more like a dream than like a reality.</line>

<line>Mr. Garrison had thoughtfully arranged to have us have one of the most</line>
<line>comfortable rooms on the ship. The second or third day out I began to</line>
<line>sleep, and I think that I slept at the rate of fifteen hours a day</line>
<line>during the remainder of the ten days’ passage. Then it was that I began</line>
<line>to understand how tired I really was. These long sleeps I kept up for a</line>
<line>month after we landed on the other side. It was such an unusual feeling</line>
<line>to wake up in the morning and realize that I had no engagements; did</line>
<line>not have to take a train at a certain hour; did not have an appointment</line>
<line>to meet some one, or to make an address, at a certain hour. How</line>
<line>different all this was from the experiences that I have been through</line>
<line>when travelling, when I have sometimes slept in three different beds in</line>
<line>a single night!</line>

<line>When Sunday came, the captain invited me to conduct the religious</line>
<line>services, but, not being a minister, I declined. The passengers,</line>
<line>however, began making requests that I deliver an address to them in the</line>
<line>dining-saloon some time during the voyage, and this I consented to do.</line>
<line>Senator Sewell presided at this meeting. After ten days of delightful</line>
<line>weather, during which I was not seasick for a day, we landed at the</line>
<line>interesting old city of Antwerp, in Belgium.</line>

<line>The next day after we landed happened to be one of those numberless</line>
<line>holidays which the people of those countries are in the habit of</line>
<line>observing. It was a bright, beautiful day. Our room in the hotel faced</line>
<line>the main public square, and the sights there—the people coming in from</line>
<line>the country with all kinds of beautiful flowers to sell, the women</line>
<line>coming in with their dogs drawing large, brightly polished cans filled</line>
<line>with milk, the people streaming into the cathedral—filled me with a</line>
<line>sense of newness that I had never before experienced.</line>

<line>After spending some time in Antwerp, we were invited to go with a part</line>
<line>of a half-dozen persons on a trip through Holland. This party included</line>
<line>Edward Marshall and some American artists who had come over on the same</line>
<line>steamer with us. We accepted the invitation, and enjoyed the trip</line>
<line>greatly. I think it was all the more interesting and instructive</line>
<line>because we went for most of the way on one of the slow, old-fashioned</line>
<line>canal-boats. This gave us an opportunity of seeing and studying the</line>
<line>real life of the people in the country districts. We went in this way</line>
<line>as far as Rotterdam, and later went to The Hague, where the Peace</line>
<line>Conference was then in session, and where we were kindly received by</line>
<line>the American representatives.</line>

<line>The thing that impressed itself most on me in Holland was the</line>
<line>thoroughness of the agriculture and the excellence of the Holstein</line>
<line>cattle. I never knew, before visiting Holland, how much it was possible</line>
<line>for people to get out of a small plot of ground. It seemed to me that</line>
<line>absolutely no land was wasted. It was worth a trip to Holland, too,</line>
<line>just to get a sight of three or four hundred fine Holstein cows grazing</line>
<line>in one of those intensely green fields.</line>

<line>From Holland we went to Belgium, and made a hasty trip through that</line>
<line>country, stopping at Brussels, where we visited the battlefield of</line>
<line>Waterloo. From Belgium we went direct to Paris, where we found that Mr.</line>
<line>Theodore Stanton, the son of Mrs. Elizabeth Cady Stanton, had kindly</line>
<line>provided accommodations for us. We had barely got settled in Paris</line>
<line>before an invitation came to me from the University Club of Paris to be</line>
<line>its guest at a banquet which was soon to be given. The other guests</line>
<line>were ex-President Benjamin Harrison and Archbishop Ireland, who were in</line>
<line>Paris at the time. The American Ambassador, General Horace Porter,</line>
<line>presided at the banquet. My address on this occasion seemed to give</line>
<line>satisfaction to those who heard it. General Harrison kindly devoted a</line>
<line>large portion of his remarks at dinner to myself and to the influence</line>
<line>of the work at Tuskegee on the American race question. After my address</line>
<line>at this banquet other invitations came to me, but I declined the most</line>
<line>of them, knowing that if I accepted them all, the object of my visit</line>
<line>would be defeated. I did, however, consent to deliver an address in the</line>
<line>American chapel the following Sunday morning, and at this meeting</line>
<line>General Harrison, General Porter, and other distinguished Americans</line>
<line>were present.</line>

<line>Later we received a formal call from the American Ambassador, and were</line>
<line>invited to attend a reception at his residence. At this reception we</line>
<line>met many Americans, among them Justices Fuller and Harlan, of the</line>
<line>United States Supreme Court. During our entire stay of a month in</line>
<line>Paris, both the American Ambassador and his wife, as well as several</line>
<line>other Americans, were very kind to us.</line>

<line>While in Paris we saw a good deal of the now famous American Negro</line>
<line>painter, Mr. Henry O. Tanner, whom we had formerly known in America. It</line>
<line>was very satisfactory to find how well known Mr. Tanner was in the</line>
<line>field of art, and to note the high standing which all classes accorded</line>
<line>to him. When we told some Americans that we were going to the</line>
<line>Luxembourg Palace to see a painting by an American Negro, it was hard</line>
<line>to convince them that a Negro had been thus honoured. I do not believe</line>
<line>that they were really convinced of the fact until they saw the picture</line>
<line>for themselves. My acquaintance with Mr. Tanner reenforced in my mind</line>
<line>the truth which I am constantly trying to impress upon our students at</line>
<line>Tuskegee—and on our people throughout the country, as far as I can</line>
<line>reach them with my voice—that any man, regardless of colour, will be</line>
<line>recognized and rewarded just in proportion as he learns to do something</line>
<line>well—learns to do it better than some one else—however humble the thing</line>
<line>may be. As I have said, I believe that my race will succeed in</line>
<line>proportion as it learns to do a common thing in an uncommon manner;</line>
<line>learns to do a thing so thoroughly that no one can improve upon what it</line>
<line>has done; learns to make its services of indispensable value. This was</line>
<line>the spirit that inspired me in my first effort at Hampton, when I was</line>
<line>given the opportunity to sweep and dust that schoolroom. In a degree I</line>
<line>felt that my whole future life depended upon the thoroughness with</line>
<line>which I cleaned that room, and I was determined to do it so well that</line>
<line>no one could find any fault with the job. Few people ever stopped, I</line>
<line>found, when looking at his pictures, to inquire whether Mr. Tanner was</line>
<line>a Negro painter, a French painter, or a German painter. They simply</line>
<line>knew that he was able to produce something which the world wanted—a</line>
<line>great painting—and the matter of his colour did not enter into their</line>
<line>minds. When a Negro girl learns to cook, to wash dishes, to sew, or</line>
<line>write a book, or a Negro boy learns to groom horses, or to grow sweet</line>
<line>potatoes, or to produce butter, or to build a house, or to be able to</line>
<line>practise medicine, as well or better than some one else, they will be</line>
<line>rewarded regardless of race or colour. In the long run, the world is</line>
<line>going to have the best, and any difference in race, religion, or</line>
<line>previous history will not long keep the world from what it wants.</line>

<line>I think that the whole future of my race hinges on the question as to</line>
<line>whether or not it can make itself of such indispensable value that the</line>
<line>people in the town and the state where we reside will feel that our</line>
<line>presence is necessary to the happiness and well-being of the community.</line>
<line>No man who continues to add something to the material, intellectual,</line>
<line>and moral well-being of the place in which he lives is long left</line>
<line>without proper reward. This is a great human law which cannot be</line>
<line>permanently nullified.</line>

<line>The love of pleasure and excitement which seems in a large measure to</line>
<line>possess the French people impressed itself upon me. I think they are</line>
<line>more noted in this respect than is true of the people of my own race.</line>
<line>In point of morality and moral earnestness I do not believe that the</line>
<line>French are ahead of my own race in America. Severe competition and the</line>
<line>great stress of life have led them to learn to do things more</line>
<line>thoroughly and to exercise greater economy; but time, I think, will</line>
<line>bring my race to the same point. In the matter of truth and high honour</line>
<line>I do not believe that the average Frenchman is ahead of the American</line>
<line>Negro; while so far as mercy and kindness to dumb animals go, I believe</line>
<line>that my race is far ahead. In fact, when I left France, I had more</line>
<line>faith in the future of the black man in America than I had ever</line>
<line>possessed.</line>

<line>From Paris we went to London, and reached there early in July, just</line>
<line>about the height of the London social season. Parliament was in</line>
<line>session, and there was a great deal of gaiety. Mr. Garrison and other</line>
<line>friends had provided us with a large number of letters of introduction,</line>
<line>and they had also sent letters to other persons in different parts of</line>
<line>the United Kingdom, apprising these people of our coming. Very soon</line>
<line>after reaching London we were flooded with invitations to attend all</line>
<line>manner of social functions, and a great many invitations came to me</line>
<line>asking that I deliver public addresses. The most of these invitations I</line>
<line>declined, for the reason that I wanted to rest. Neither were we able to</line>
<line>accept more than a small proportion of the other invitations. The Rev.</line>
<line>Dr. Brooke Herford and Mrs. Herford, whom I had known in Boston,</line>
<line>consulted with the American Ambassador, the Hon. Joseph Choate, and</line>
<line>arranged for me to speak at a public meeting to be held in Essex Hall.</line>
<line>Mr. Choate kindly consented to preside. The meeting was largely</line>
<line>attended. There were many distinguished persons present, among them</line>
<line>several members of Parliament, including Mr. James Bryce, who spoke at</line>
<line>the meeting. What the American Ambassador said in introducing me, as</line>
<line>well as a synopsis of what I said, was widely published in England and</line>
<line>in the American papers at the time. Dr. and Mrs. Herford gave Mrs.</line>
<line>Washington and myself a reception, at which we had the privilege of</line>
<line>meeting some of the best people in England. Throughout our stay in</line>
<line>London Ambassador Choate was most kind and attentive to us. At the</line>
<line>Ambassador’s reception I met, for the first time, Mark Twain.</line>

<line>We were the guests several times of Mrs. T. Fisher Unwin, the daughter</line>
<line>of the English statesman, Richard Cobden. It seemed as if both Mr. and</line>
<line>Mrs. Unwin could not do enough for our comfort and happiness. Later,</line>
<line>for nearly a week, we were the guests of the daughter of John Bright,</line>
<line>now Mrs. Clark, of Street, England. Both Mr. and Mrs. Clark, with their</line>
<line>daughter, visited us at Tuskegee the next year. In Birmingham, England,</line>
<line>we were the guests for several days of Mr. Joseph Sturge, whose father</line>
<line>was a great abolitionist and friend of Whittier and Garrison. It was a</line>
<line>great privilege to meet throughout England those who had known and</line>
<line>honoured the late William Lloyd Garrison, the Hon. Frederick Douglass,</line>
<line>and other abolitionists. The English abolitionists with whom we came in</line>
<line>contact never seemed to tire of talking about these two Americans.</line>
<line>Before going to England I had had no proper conception of the deep</line>
<line>interest displayed by the abolitionists of England in the cause of</line>
<line>freedom, nor did I realize the amount of substantial help given by</line>
<line>them.</line>

<line>In Bristol, England, both Mrs. Washington and I spoke at the Women’s</line>
<line>Liberal Club. I was also the principal speaker at the Commencement</line>
<line>exercises of the Royal College for the Blind. These exercises were held</line>
<line>in the Crystal Palace, and the presiding officer was the late Duke of</line>
<line>Westminster, who was said to be, I believe, the richest man in England,</line>
<line>if not in the world. The Duke, as well as his wife and their daughter,</line>
<line>seemed to be pleased with what I said, and thanked me heartily. Through</line>
<line>the kindness of Lady Aberdeen, my wife and I were enabled to go with a</line>
<line>party of those who were attending the International Congress of Women,</line>
<line>then in session in London, to see Queen Victoria, at Windsor Castle,</line>
<line>where, afterward, we were all the guests of her Majesty at tea. In our</line>
<line>party was Miss Susan B. Anthony, and I was deeply impressed with the</line>
<line>fact that one did not often get an opportunity to see, during the same</line>
<line>hour, two women so remarkable in different ways as Susan B. Anthony and</line>
<line>Queen Victoria.</line>

<line>In the House of Commons, which we visited several times, we met Sir</line>
<line>Henry M. Stanley. I talked with him about Africa and its relation to</line>
<line>the American Negro, and after my interview with him I became more</line>
<line>convinced than ever that there was no hope of the American Negro’s</line>
<line>improving his condition by emigrating to Africa.</line>

<line>On various occasions Mrs. Washington and I were the guests of</line>
<line>Englishmen in their country homes, where, I think, one sees the</line>
<line>Englishman at his best. In one thing, at least, I feel sure that the</line>
<line>English are ahead of Americans, and that is, that they have learned how</line>
<line>to get more out of life. The home life of the English seems to me to be</line>
<line>about as perfect as anything can be. Everything moves like clockwork. I</line>
<line>was impressed, too, with the deference that the servants show to their</line>
<line>“masters” and “mistresses,”—terms which I suppose would not be</line>
<line>tolerated in America. The English servant expects, as a rule, to be</line>
<line>nothing but a servant, and so he perfects himself in the art to a</line>
<line>degree that no class of servants in America has yet reached. In our</line>
<line>country the servant expects to become, in a few years, a “master”</line>
<line>himself. Which system is preferable? I will not venture an answer.</line>

<line>Another thing that impressed itself upon me throughout England was the</line>
<line>high regard that all classes have for law and order, and the ease and</line>
<line>thoroughness with which everything is done. The Englishmen, I found,</line>
<line>took plenty of time for eating, as for everything else. I am not sure</line>
<line>if, in the long run, they do not accomplish as much or more than</line>
<line>rushing, nervous Americans do.</line>

<line>My visit to England gave me a higher regard for the nobility than I had</line>
<line>had. I had no idea that they were so generally loved and respected by</line>
<line>the classes, nor had I any correct conception of how much time and</line>
<line>money they spent in works of philanthropy, and how much real heart they</line>
<line>put into this work. My impression had been that they merely spent money</line>
<line>freely and had a “good time.”</line>

<line>It was hard for me to get accustomed to speaking to English audiences.</line>
<line>The average Englishman is so serious, and is so tremendously in earnest</line>
<line>about everything, that when I told a story that would have made an</line>
<line>American audience roar with laughter, the Englishmen simply looked me</line>
<line>straight in the face without even cracking a smile.</line>

<line>When the Englishman takes you into his heart and friendship, he binds</line>
<line>you there as with cords of steel, and I do not believe that there are</line>
<line>many other friendships that are so lasting or so satisfactory. Perhaps</line>
<line>I can illustrate this point in no better way than by relating the</line>
<line>following incident. Mrs. Washington and I were invited to attend a</line>
<line>reception given by the Duke and Duchess of Sutherland, at Stafford</line>
<line>House—said to be the finest house in London; I may add that I believe</line>
<line>the Duchess of Sutherland is said to be the most beautiful woman in</line>
<line>England. There must have been at least three hundred persons at this</line>
<line>reception. Twice during the evening the Duchess sought us out for a</line>
<line>conversation, and she asked me to write her when we got home, and tell</line>
<line>her more about the work at Tuskegee. This I did. When Christmas came we</line>
<line>were surprised and delighted to receive her photograph with her</line>
<line>autograph on it. The correspondence has continued, and we now feel that</line>
<line>in the Duchess of Sutherland we have one of our warmest friends.</line>

<line>After three months in Europe we sailed from Southampton in the</line>
<line>steamship St. Louis. On this steamer there was a fine library that had</line>
<line>been presented to the ship by the citizens of St. Louis, Mo. In this</line>
<line>library I found a life of Frederick Douglass, which I began reading. I</line>
<line>became especially interested in Mr. Douglass’s description of the way</line>
<line>he was treated on shipboard during his first or second visit to</line>
<line>England. In this description he told how he was not permitted to enter</line>
<line>the cabin, but had to confine himself to the deck of the ship. A few</line>
<line>minutes after I had finished reading this description I was waited on</line>
<line>by a committee of ladies and gentlemen with the request that I deliver</line>
<line>an address at a concert which was to begin the following evening. And</line>
<line>yet there are people who are bold enough to say that race feeling in</line>
<line>America is not growing less intense! At this concert the Hon. Benjamin</line>
<line>B. Odell, Jr., the present governor of New York, presided. I was never</line>
<line>given a more cordial hearing anywhere. A large proportion of the</line>
<line>passengers were Southern people. After the concert some of the</line>
<line>passengers proposed that a subscription be raised to help the work at</line>
<line>Tuskegee, and the money to support several scholarships was the result.</line>

<line>While we were in Paris I was very pleasantly surprised to receive the</line>
<line>following invitation from the citizens of West Virginia and of the city</line>
<line>near which I had spent my boyhood days:—</line>

<line>Charleston, W. Va., May 16, 1899.</line>

<line>Professor Booker T. Washington, Paris, France:</line>

<line>Dear Sir: Many of the best citizens of West Virginia have united in</line>
<line>liberal expressions of admiration and praise of your worth and work,</line>
<line>and desire that on your return from Europe you should favour them with</line>
<line>your presence and with the inspiration of your words. We must sincerely</line>
<line>indorse this move, and on behalf of the citizens of Charleston extend</line>
<line>to your our most cordial invitation to have you come to us, that we may</line>
<line>honour you who have done so much by your life and work to honour us.</line>

<line>We are,</line>

<line>Very truly yours,</line>

<line>The Common Council of the City of Charleston,</line>

<line>By W. Herman Smith, Mayor.</line>

<line>This invitation from the City Council of Charleston was accompanied by</line>
<line>the following:—</line>

<line>Professor Booker T. Washington, Paris, France:</line>

<line>Dear Sir: We, the citizens of Charleston and West Virginia, desire to</line>
<line>express our pride in you and the splendid career that you have thus far</line>
<line>accomplished, and ask that we be permitted to show our pride and</line>
<line>interest in a substantial way.</line>

<line>Your recent visit to your old home in our midst awoke within us the</line>
<line>keenest regret that we were not permitted to hear you and render some</line>
<line>substantial aid to your work, before you left for Europe.</line>

<line>In view of the foregoing, we earnestly invite you to share the</line>
<line>hospitality of our city upon your return from Europe, and give us the</line>
<line>opportunity to hear you and put ourselves in touch with your work in a</line>
<line>way that will be most gratifying to yourself, and that we may receive</line>
<line>the inspiration of your words and presence.</line>

<line>An early reply to this invitation, with an indication of the time you</line>
<line>may reach our city, will greatly oblige,</line>

<line>Yours very respectfully,</line>

<line>The Charleston Daily Gazette, The Daily Mail-Tribune; G.W. Atkinson,</line>
<line>Governor; E.L. Boggs, Secretary to Governor; Wm. M.O. Dawson, Secretary</line>
<line>of State; L.M. La Follette, Auditor; J.R. Trotter, Superintendent of</line>
<line>Schools; E.W. Wilson, ex-Governor; W.A. MacCorkle, ex-Governor; John Q.</line>
<line>Dickinson, President Kanawha Valley Bank; L. Prichard, President</line>
<line>Charleston National Bank; Geo. S. Couch, President Kanawha National</line>
<line>Bank; Ed. Reid, Cashier Kanawha National Bank; Geo. S. Laidley,</line>
<line>Superintended City Schools; L.E. McWhorter, President Board of</line>
<line>Education; Chas. K. Payne, wholesale merchant; and many others.</line>

<line>This invitation, coming as it did from the City Council, the state</line>
<line>officers, and all the substantial citizens of both races of the</line>
<line>community where I had spent my boyhood, and from which I had gone a few</line>
<line>years before, unknown, in poverty and ignorance, in quest of an</line>
<line>education, not only surprised me, but almost unmanned me. I could not</line>
<line>understand what I had done to deserve it all.</line>

<line>I accepted the invitation, and at the appointed day was met at the</line>
<line>railway station at Charleston by a committee headed by ex-Governor W.A.</line>
<line>MacCorkle, and composed of men of both races. The public reception was</line>
<line>held in the Opera-House at Charleston. The Governor of the state, the</line>
<line>Hon. George W. Atkinson, presided, and an address of welcome was made</line>
<line>by ex-Governor MacCorkle. A prominent part in the reception was taken</line>
<line>by the coloured citizens. The Opera-House was filled with citizens of</line>
<line>both races, and among the white people were many for whom I had worked</line>
<line>when I was a boy. The next day Governor and Mrs. Atkinson gave me a</line>
<line>public reception at the State House, which was attended by all classes.</line>

<line>Not long after this the coloured people in Atlanta, Georgia, gave me a</line>
<line>reception at which the Governor of the state presided, and a similar</line>
<line>reception was given me in New Orleans, which was presided over by the</line>
<line>Mayor of the city. Invitations came from many other places which I was</line>
<line>not able to accept.</line>




</chapter>

<chapter>
<title>Chapter XVII. Last Words</title>


<line>Before going to Europe some events came into my life which were great</line>
<line>surprises to me. In fact, my whole life has largely been one of</line>
<line>surprises. I believe that any man’s life will be filled with constant,</line>
<line>unexpected encouragements of this kind if he makes up his mind to do</line>
<line>his level best each day of his life—that is, tries to make each day</line>
<line>reach as nearly as possible the high-water mark of pure, unselfish,</line>
<line>useful living. I pity the man, black or white, who has never</line>
<line>experienced the joy and satisfaction that come to one by reason of an</line>
<line>effort to assist in making some one else more useful and more happy.</line>

<line>Six months before he died, and nearly a year after he had been stricken</line>
<line>with paralysis, General Armstrong expressed a wish to visit Tuskegee</line>
<line>again before he passed away. Notwithstanding the fact that he had lost</line>
<line>the use of his limbs to such an extent that he was practically</line>
<line>helpless, his wish was gratified, and he was brought to Tuskegee. The</line>
<line>owners of the Tuskegee Railroad, white men living in the town, offered</line>
<line>to run a special train, without cost, out of the main station—Chehaw,</line>
<line>five miles away—to meet him. He arrived on the school grounds about</line>
<line>nine o’clock in the evening. Some one had suggested that we give the</line>
<line>General a “pine-knot torchlight reception.” This plan was carried out,</line>
<line>and the moment that his carriage entered the school grounds he began</line>
<line>passing between two lines of lighted and waving “fat pine” wood knots</line>
<line>held by over a thousand students and teachers. The whole thing was so</line>
<line>novel and surprising that the General was completely overcome with</line>
<line>happiness. He remained a guest in my home for nearly two months, and,</line>
<line>although almost wholly without the use of voice or limb, he spent</line>
<line>nearly every hour in devising ways and means to help the South. Time</line>
<line>and time again he said to me, during this visit, that it was not only</line>
<line>the duty of the country to assist in elevating the Negro of the South,</line>
<line>but the poor white man as well. At the end of his visit I resolved anew</line>
<line>to devote myself more earnestly than ever to the cause which was so</line>
<line>near his heart. I said that if a man in his condition was willing to</line>
<line>think, work, and act, I should not be wanting in furthering in every</line>
<line>possible way the wish of his heart.</line>

<line>The death of General Armstrong, a few weeks later, gave me the</line>
<line>privilege of getting acquainted with one of the finest, most unselfish,</line>
<line>and most attractive men that I have ever come in contact with. I refer</line>
<line>to the Rev. Dr. Hollis B. Frissell, now the Principal of the Hampton</line>
<line>Institute, and General Armstrong’s successor. Under the clear, strong,</line>
<line>and almost perfect leadership of Dr. Frissell, Hampton has had a career</line>
<line>of prosperity and usefulness that is all that the General could have</line>
<line>wished for. It seems to be the constant effort of Dr. Frissell to hide</line>
<line>his own great personality behind that of General Armstrong—to make</line>
<line>himself of “no reputation” for the sake of the cause.</line>

<line>More than once I have been asked what was the greatest surprise that</line>
<line>ever came to me. I have little hesitation in answering that question.</line>
<line>It was the following letter, which came to me one Sunday morning when I</line>
<line>was sitting on the veranda of my home at Tuskegee, surrounded by my</line>
<line>wife and three children:—</line>

<line>Harvard University, Cambridge, May 28, 1896.</line>

<line>President Booker T. Washington,</line>

<line>My Dear Sir: Harvard University desired to confer on you at the</line>
<line>approaching Commencement an honorary degree; but it is our custom to</line>
<line>confer degrees only on gentlemen who are present. Our Commencement</line>
<line>occurs this year on June 24, and your presence would be desirable from</line>
<line>about noon till about five o’clock in the afternoon. Would it be</line>
<line>possible for you to be in Cambridge on that day?</line>

<line>Believe me, with great regard,</line>

<line>Very truly yours,</line>

<line>Charles W. Eliot.</line>

<line>This was a recognition that had never in the slightest manner entered</line>
<line>into my mind, and it was hard for me to realize that I was to be</line>
<line>honoured by a degree from the oldest and most renowned university in</line>
<line>America. As I sat upon my veranda, with this letter in my hand, tears</line>
<line>came into my eyes. My whole former life—my life as a slave on the</line>
<line>plantation, my work in the coal-mine, the times when I was without food</line>
<line>and clothing, when I made my bed under a sidewalk, my struggles for an</line>
<line>education, the trying days I had had at Tuskegee, days when I did not</line>
<line>know where to turn for a dollar to continue the work there, the</line>
<line>ostracism and sometimes oppression of my race,—all this passed before</line>
<line>me and nearly overcame me.</line>

<line>I had never sought or cared for what the world calls fame. I have</line>
<line>always looked upon fame as something to be used in accomplishing good.</line>
<line>I have often said to my friends that if I can use whatever prominence</line>
<line>may have come to me as an instrument with which to do good, I am</line>
<line>content to have it. I care for it only as a means to be used for doing</line>
<line>good, just as wealth may be used. The more I come into contact with</line>
<line>wealthy people, the more I believe that they are growing in the</line>
<line>direction of looking upon their money simply as an instrument which God</line>
<line>has placed in their hand for doing good with. I never go to the office</line>
<line>of Mr. John D. Rockefeller, who more than once has been generous to</line>
<line>Tuskegee, without being reminded of this. The close, careful, and</line>
<line>minute investigation that he always makes in order to be sure that</line>
<line>every dollar that he gives will do the most good—an investigation that</line>
<line>is just as searching as if he were investing money in a business</line>
<line>enterprise—convinces me that the growth in this direction is most</line>
<line>encouraging.</line>

<line>At nine o’clock, on the morning of June 24, I met President Eliot, the</line>
<line>Board of Overseers of Harvard University, and the other guests, at the</line>
<line>designated place on the university grounds, for the purpose of being</line>
<line>escorted to Sanders Theatre, where the Commencement exercises were to</line>
<line>be held and degrees conferred. Among others invited to be present for</line>
<line>the purpose of receiving a degree at this time were General Nelson A.</line>
<line>Miles, Dr. Bell, the inventor of the Bell telephone, Bishop Vincent,</line>
<line>and the Rev. Minot J. Savage. We were placed in line immediately behind</line>
<line>the President and the Board of Overseers, and directly afterward the</line>
<line>Governor of Massachusetts, escorted by the Lancers, arrived and took</line>
<line>his place in the line of march by the side of President Eliot. In the</line>
<line>line there were also various other officers and professors, clad in cap</line>
<line>and gown. In this order we marched to Sanders Theatre, where, after the</line>
<line>usual Commencement exercises, came the conferring of the honorary</line>
<line>degrees. This, it seems, is always considered the most interesting</line>
<line>feature at Harvard. It is not known, until the individuals appear, upon</line>
<line>whom the honorary degrees are to be conferred, and those receiving</line>
<line>these honours are cheered by the students and others in proportion to</line>
<line>their popularity. During the conferring of the degrees excitement and</line>
<line>enthusiasm are at the highest pitch.</line>

<line>When my name was called, I rose, and President Eliot, in beautiful and</line>
<line>strong English, conferred upon me the degree of Master of Arts. After</line>
<line>these exercises were over, those who had received honorary degrees were</line>
<line>invited to lunch with the President. After the lunch we were formed in</line>
<line>line again, and were escorted by the Marshal of the day, who that year</line>
<line>happened to be Bishop William Lawrence, through the grounds, where, at</line>
<line>different points, those who had been honoured were called by name and</line>
<line>received the Harvard yell. This march ended at Memorial Hall, where the</line>
<line>alumni dinner was served. To see over a thousand strong men,</line>
<line>representing all that is best in State, Church, business, and</line>
<line>education, with the glow and enthusiasm of college loyalty and college</line>
<line>pride,—which has, I think, a peculiar Harvard flavour,—is a sight that</line>
<line>does not easily fade from memory.</line>

<line>Among the speakers after dinner were President Eliot, Governor Roger</line>
<line>Wolcott, General Miles, Dr. Minot J. Savage, the Hon. Henry Cabot</line>
<line>Lodge, and myself. When I was called upon, I said, among other things:—</line>

<line>It would in some measure relieve my embarrassment if I could, even in a</line>
<line>slight degree, feel myself worthy of the great honour which you do me</line>
<line>to-day. Why you have called me from the Black Belt of the South, from</line>
<line>among my humble people, to share in the honours of this occasion, is</line>
<line>not for me to explain; and yet it may not be inappropriate for me to</line>
<line>suggest that it seems to me that one of the most vital questions that</line>
<line>touch our American life is how to bring the strong, wealthy, and</line>
<line>learned into helpful touch with the poorest, most ignorant, and</line>
<line>humblest, and at the same time make one appreciate the vitalizing,</line>
<line>strengthening influence of the other. How shall we make the mansion on</line>
<line>yon Beacon Street feel and see the need of the spirits in the lowliest</line>
<line>cabin in Alabama cotton-fields or Louisiana sugar-bottoms? This problem</line>
<line>Harvard University is solving, not by bringing itself down, but by</line>
<line>bringing the masses up.</line>


<line>If my life in the past has meant anything in the lifting up of my</line>
<line>people and the bringing about of better relations between your race and</line>
<line>mine, I assure you from this day it will mean doubly more. In the</line>
<line>economy of God there is but one standard by which an individual can</line>
<line>succeed—there is but one for a race. This country demands that every</line>
<line>race shall measure itself by the American standard. By it a race must</line>
<line>rise or fall, succeed or fail, and in the last analysis mere sentiment</line>
<line>counts for little. During the next half-century and more, my race must</line>
<line>continue passing through the severe American crucible. We are to be</line>
<line>tested in our patience, our forbearance, our perseverance, our power to</line>
<line>endure wrong, to withstand temptations, to economize, to acquire and</line>
<line>use skill; in our ability to compete, to succeed in commerce, to</line>
<line>disregard the superficial for the real, the appearance for the</line>
<line>substance, to be great and yet small, learned and yet simple, high and</line>
<line>yet the servant of all.</line>

<line>As this was the first time that a New England university had conferred</line>
<line>an honorary degree upon a Negro, it was the occasion of much newspaper</line>
<line>comment throughout the country. A correspondent of a New York paper</line>
<line>said:—</line>

<line>When the name of Booker T. Washington was called, and he arose to</line>
<line>acknowledge and accept, there was such an outburst of applause as</line>
<line>greeted no other name except that of the popular soldier patriot,</line>
<line>General Miles. The applause was not studied and stiff, sympathetic and</line>
<line>condoling; it was enthusiasm and admiration. Every part of the audience</line>
<line>from pit to gallery joined in, and a glow covered the cheeks of those</line>
<line>around me, proving sincere appreciation of the rising struggle of an</line>
<line>ex-slave and the work he has accomplished for his race.</line>

<line>A Boston paper said, editorially:—</line>

<line>In conferring the honorary degree of Master of Arts upon the Principal</line>
<line>of Tuskegee Institute, Harvard University has honoured itself as well</line>
<line>as the object of this distinction. The work which Professor Booker T.</line>
<line>Washington has accomplished for the education, good citizenship, and</line>
<line>popular enlightenment in his chosen field of labour in the South</line>
<line>entitles him to rank with our national benefactors. The university</line>
<line>which can claim him on its list of sons, whether in regular course or</line>
<line>honoris causa, may be proud.</line>

<line>It has been mentioned that Mr. Washington is the first of his race to</line>
<line>receive an honorary degree from a New England university. This, in</line>
<line>itself, is a distinction. But the degree was not conferred because Mr.</line>
<line>Washington is a coloured man, or because he was born in slavery, but</line>
<line>because he has shown, by his work for the elevation of the people of</line>
<line>the Black Belt of the South, a genius and a broad humanity which count</line>
<line>for greatness in any man, whether his skin be white or black.</line>

<line>Another Boston paper said:—</line>

<line>It is Harvard which, first among New England colleges, confers an</line>
<line>honorary degree upon a black man. No one who has followed the history</line>
<line>of Tuskegee and its work can fail to admire the courage, persistence,</line>
<line>and splendid common sense of Booker T. Washington. Well may Harvard</line>
<line>honour the ex-slave, the value of whose services, alike to his race and</line>
<line>country, only the future can estimate.</line>

<line>The correspondent of the New York Times wrote:—</line>

<line>All the speeches were enthusiastically received, but the coloured man</line>
<line>carried off the oratorical honours, and the applause which broke out</line>
<line>when he had finished was vociferous and long-continued.</line>

<line>Soon after I began work at Tuskegee I formed a resolution, in the</line>
<line>secret of my heart, that I would try to build up a school that would be</line>
<line>of so much service to the country that the President of the United</line>
<line>States would one day come to see it. This was, I confess, rather a bold</line>
<line>resolution, and for a number of years I kept it hidden in my own</line>
<line>thoughts, not daring to share it with any one.</line>

<line>In November, 1897, I made the first move in this direction, and that</line>
<line>was in securing a visit from a member of President McKinley’s Cabinet,</line>
<line>the Hon. James Wilson, Secretary of Agriculture. He came to deliver an</line>
<line>address at the formal opening of the Slater-Armstrong Agricultural</line>
<line>Building, our first large building to be used for the purpose of giving</line>
<line>training to our students in agriculture and kindred branches.</line>

<line>In the fall of 1898 I heard that President McKinley was likely to visit</line>
<line>Atlanta, Georgia, for the purpose of taking part in the Peace Jubilee</line>
<line>exercises to be held there to commemorate the successful close of the</line>
<line>Spanish-American war. At this time I had been hard at work, together</line>
<line>with our teachers, for eighteen years, trying to build up a school that</line>
<line>we thought would be of service to the Nation, and I determined to make</line>
<line>a direct effort to secure a visit from the President and his Cabinet. I</line>
<line>went to Washington, and I was not long in the city before I found my</line>
<line>way to the White House. When I got there I found the waiting rooms full</line>
<line>of people, and my heart began to sink, for I feared there would not be</line>
<line>much chance of my seeing the President that day, if at all. But, at any</line>
<line>rate, I got an opportunity to see Mr. J. Addison Porter, the secretary</line>
<line>to the President, and explained to him my mission. Mr. Porter kindly</line>
<line>sent my card directly to the President, and in a few minutes word came</line>
<line>from Mr. McKinley that he would see me.</line>

<line>How any man can see so many people of all kinds, with all kinds of</line>
<line>errands, and do so much hard work, and still keep himself calm,</line>
<line>patient, and fresh for each visitor in the way that President McKinley</line>
<line>does, I cannot understand. When I saw the President he kindly thanked</line>
<line>me for the work which we were doing at Tuskegee for the interests of</line>
<line>the country. I then told him, briefly, the object of my visit. I</line>
<line>impressed upon him the fact that a visit from the Chief Executive of</line>
<line>the Nation would not only encourage our students and teachers, but</line>
<line>would help the entire race. He seemed interested, but did not make a</line>
<line>promise to go to Tuskegee, for the reason that his plans about going to</line>
<line>Atlanta were not then fully made; but he asked me to call the matter to</line>
<line>his attention a few weeks later.</line>

<line>By the middle of the following month the President had definitely</line>
<line>decided to attend the Peace Jubilee at Atlanta. I went to Washington</line>
<line>again and saw him, with a view of getting him to extend his trip to</line>
<line>Tuskegee. On this second visit Mr. Charles W. Hare, a prominent white</line>
<line>citizen of Tuskegee, kindly volunteered to accompany me, to reenforce</line>
<line>my invitation with one from the white people of Tuskegee and the</line>
<line>vicinity.</line>

<line>Just previous to my going to Washington the second time, the country</line>
<line>had been excited, and the coloured people greatly depressed, because of</line>
<line>several severe race riots which had occurred at different points in the</line>
<line>South. As soon as I saw the President, I perceived that his heart was</line>
<line>greatly burdened by reason of these race disturbances. Although there</line>
<line>were many people waiting to see him, he detained me for some time,</line>
<line>discussing the condition and prospects of the race. He remarked several</line>
<line>times that he was determined to show his interest and faith in the</line>
<line>race, not merely in words, but by acts. When I told him that I thought</line>
<line>that at that time scarcely anything would go farther in giving hope and</line>
<line>encouragement to the race than the fact that the President of the</line>
<line>Nation would be willing to travel one hundred and forty miles out of</line>
<line>his way to spend a day at a Negro institution, he seemed deeply</line>
<line>impressed.</line>

<line>While I was with the President, a white citizen of Atlanta, a Democrat</line>
<line>and an ex-slaveholder, came into the room, and the President asked his</line>
<line>opinion as to the wisdom of his going to Tuskegee. Without hesitation</line>
