<line>the Atlanta man replied that it was the proper thing for him to do.</line>
<line>This opinion was reenforced by that friend of the race, Dr. J.L.M.</line>
<line>Curry. The President promised that he would visit our school on the</line>
<line>16th of December.</line>

<line>When it became known that the President was going to visit our school,</line>
<line>the white citizens of the town of Tuskegee—a mile distant from the</line>
<line>school—were as much pleased as were our students and teachers. The</line>
<line>white people of this town, including both men and women, began</line>
<line>arranging to decorate the town, and to form themselves into committees</line>
<line>for the purpose of cooperating with the officers of our school in order</line>
<line>that the distinguished visitor might have a fitting reception. I think</line>
<line>I never realized before this how much the white people of Tuskegee and</line>
<line>vicinity thought of our institution. During the days when we were</line>
<line>preparing for the President’s reception, dozens of these people came to</line>
<line>me and said that, while they did not want to push themselves into</line>
<line>prominence, if there was anything they could do to help, or to relieve</line>
<line>me personally, I had but to intimate it and they would be only too glad</line>
<line>to assist. In fact, the thing that touched me almost as deeply as the</line>
<line>visit of the President itself was the deep pride which all classes of</line>
<line>citizens in Alabama seemed to take in our work.</line>

<line>The morning of December 16th brought to the little city of Tuskegee</line>
<line>such a crowd as it had never seen before. With the President came Mrs.</line>
<line>McKinley and all of the Cabinet officers but one; and most of them</line>
<line>brought their wives or some members of their families. Several</line>
<line>prominent generals came, including General Shafter and General Joseph</line>
<line>Wheeler, who were recently returned from the Spanish-American war.</line>
<line>There was also a host of newspaper correspondents. The Alabama</line>
<line>Legislature was in session in Montgomery at this time. This body passed</line>
<line>a resolution to adjourn for the purpose of visiting Tuskegee. Just</line>
<line>before the arrival of the President’s party the Legislature arrived,</line>
<line>headed by the governor and other state officials.</line>

<line>The citizens of Tuskegee had decorated the town from the station to the</line>
<line>school in a generous manner. In order to economize in the matter of</line>
<line>time, we arranged to have the whole school pass in review before the</line>
<line>President. Each student carried a stalk of sugar-cane with some open</line>
<line>bolls of cotton fastened to the end of it. Following the students the</line>
<line>work of all departments of the school passed in review, displayed on</line>
<line>“floats” drawn by horses, mules, and oxen. On these floats we tried to</line>
<line>exhibit not only the present work of the school, but to show the</line>
<line>contrasts between the old methods of doing things and the new. As an</line>
<line>example, we showed the old method of dairying in contrast with the</line>
<line>improved methods, the old methods of tilling the soil in contrast with</line>
<line>the new, the old methods of cooking and housekeeping in contrast with</line>
<line>the new. These floats consumed an hour and a half of time in passing.</line>

<line>In his address in our large, new chapel, which the students had</line>
<line>recently completed, the President said, among other things:—</line>

<line>To meet you under such pleasant auspices and to have the opportunity of</line>
<line>a personal observation of your work is indeed most gratifying. The</line>
<line>Tuskegee Normal and Industrial Institute is ideal in its conception,</line>
<line>and has already a large and growing reputation in the country, and is</line>
<line>not unknown abroad. I congratulate all who are associated in this</line>
<line>undertaking for the good work which it is doing in the education of its</line>
<line>students to lead lives of honour and usefulness, thus exalting the race</line>
<line>for which it was established.</line>

<line>Nowhere, I think, could a more delightful location have been chosen for</line>
<line>this unique educational experiment, which has attracted the attention</line>
<line>and won the support even of conservative philanthropists in all</line>
<line>sections of the country.</line>

<line>To speak of Tuskegee without paying special tribute to Booker T.</line>
<line>Washington’s genius and perseverance would be impossible. The inception</line>
<line>of this noble enterprise was his, and he deserves high credit for it.</line>
<line>His was the enthusiasm and enterprise which made its steady progress</line>
<line>possible and established in the institution its present high standard</line>
<line>of accomplishment. He has won a worthy reputation as one of the great</line>
<line>leaders of his race, widely known and much respected at home and abroad</line>
<line>as an accomplished educator, a great orator, and a true philanthropist.</line>

<line>The Hon. John D. Long, the Secretary of the Navy, said in part:—</line>

<line>I cannot make a speech to-day. My heart is too full—full of hope,</line>
<line>admiration, and pride for my countrymen of both sections and both</line>
<line>colours. I am filled with gratitude and admiration for your work, and</line>
<line>from this time forward I shall have absolute confidence in your</line>
<line>progress and in the solution of the problem in which you are engaged.</line>

<line>The problem, I say, has been solved. A picture has been presented</line>
<line>to-day which should be put upon canvas with the pictures of Washington</line>
<line>and Lincoln, and transmitted to future time and generations—a picture</line>
<line>which the press of the country should spread broadcast over the land, a</line>
<line>most dramatic picture, and that picture is this: The President of the</line>
<line>United States standing on this platform; on one side the Governor of</line>
<line>Alabama, on the other, completing the trinity, a representative of a</line>
<line>race only a few years ago in bondage, the coloured President of the</line>
<line>Tuskegee Normal and Industrial Institute.</line>

<line>God bless the President under whose majesty such a scene as that is</line>
<line>presented to the American people. God bless the state of Alabama, which</line>
<line>is showing that it can deal with this problem for itself. God bless the</line>
<line>orator, philanthropist, and disciple of the Great Master—who, if he</line>
<line>were on earth, would be doing the same work—Booker T. Washington.</line>

<line>Postmaster General Smith closed the address which he made with these</line>
<line>words:—</line>

<line>We have witnessed many spectacles within the last few days. We have</line>
<line>seen the magnificent grandeur and the magnificent achievements of one</line>
<line>of the great metropolitan cities of the South. We have seen heroes of</line>
<line>the war pass by in procession. We have seen floral parades. But I am</line>
<line>sure my colleagues will agree with me in saying that we have witnessed</line>
<line>no spectacle more impressive and more encouraging, more inspiring for</line>
<line>our future, than that which we have witnessed here this morning.</line>

<line>Some days after the President returned to Washington I received the</line>
<line>letter which follows:—</line>

<line>Executive Mansion, Washington, Dec. 23, 1899.</line>

<line>Dear Sir: By this mail I take pleasure in sending you engrossed copies</line>
<line>of the souvenir of the visit of the President to your institution.</line>
<line>These sheets bear the autographs of the President and the members of</line>
<line>the Cabinet who accompanied him on the trip. Let me take this</line>
<line>opportunity of congratulating you most heartily and sincerely upon the</line>
<line>great success of the exercises provided for and entertainment furnished</line>
<line>us under your auspices during our visit to Tuskegee. Every feature of</line>
<line>the programme was perfectly executed and was viewed or participated in</line>
<line>with the heartiest satisfaction by every visitor present. The unique</line>
<line>exhibition which you gave of your pupils engaged in their industrial</line>
<line>vocations was not only artistic but thoroughly impressive. The tribute</line>
<line>paid by the President and his Cabinet to your work was none too high,</line>
<line>and forms a most encouraging augury, I think, for the future prosperity</line>
<line>of your institution. I cannot close without assuring you that the</line>
<line>modesty shown by yourself in the exercises was most favourably</line>
<line>commented upon by all the members of our party.</line>

<line>With best wishes for the continued advance of your most useful and</line>
<line>patriotic undertaking, kind personal regards, and the compliments of</line>
<line>the season, believe me, always,</line>

<line>Very sincerely yours,</line>

<line>John Addison Porter,</line>

<line>Secretary to the President.</line>

<line>To President Booker T. Washington, Tuskegee Normal and Industrial</line>
<line>Institute, Tuskegee, Ala.</line>

<line>Twenty years have now passed since I made the first humble effort at</line>
<line>Tuskegee, in a broken-down shanty and an old hen-house, without owning</line>
<line>a dollar’s worth of property, and with but one teacher and thirty</line>
<line>students. At the present time the institution owns twenty-three hundred</line>
<line>acres of land, one thousand of which are under cultivation each year,</line>
<line>entirely by student labour. There are now upon the grounds, counting</line>
<line>large and small, sixty-six buildings; and all except four of these have</line>
<line>been almost wholly erected by the labour of our students. While the</line>
<line>students are at work upon the land and in erecting buildings, they are</line>
<line>taught, by competent instructors, the latest methods of agriculture and</line>
<line>the trades connected with building.</line>

<line>There are in constant operation at the school, in connection with</line>
<line>thorough academic and religious training, thirty industrial</line>
<line>departments. All of these teach industries at which our men and women</line>
<line>can find immediate employment as soon as they leave the institution.</line>
<line>The only difficulty now is that the demand for our graduates from both</line>
<line>white and black people in the South is so great that we cannot supply</line>
<line>more than one-half the persons for whom applications come to us.</line>
<line>Neither have we the buildings nor the money for current expenses to</line>
<line>enable us to admit to the school more than one-half the young men and</line>
<line>women who apply to us for admission.</line>

<line>In our industrial teaching we keep three things in mind: first, that</line>
<line>the student shall be so educated that he shall be enabled to meet</line>
<line>conditions as they exist now, in the part of the South where he</line>
<line>lives—in a word, to be able to do the thing which the world wants done;</line>
<line>second, that every student who graduates from the school shall have</line>
<line>enough skill, coupled with intelligence and moral character, to enable</line>
<line>him to make a living for himself and others; third, to send every</line>
<line>graduate out feeling and knowing that labour is dignified and</line>
<line>beautiful—to make each one love labour instead of trying to escape it.</line>
<line>In addition to the agricultural training which we give to young men,</line>
<line>and the training given to our girls in all the usual domestic</line>
<line>employments, we now train a number of girls in agriculture each year.</line>
<line>These girls are taught gardening, fruit-growing, dairying, bee-culture,</line>
<line>and poultry-raising.</line>

<line>While the institution is in no sense denominational, we have a</line>
<line>department known as the Phelps Hall Bible Training School, in which a</line>
<line>number of students are prepared for the ministry and other forms of</line>
<line>Christian work, especially work in the country districts. What is</line>
<line>equally important, each one of the students works half of each day at</line>
<line>some industry, in order to get skill and the love of work, so that when</line>
<line>he goes out from the institution he is prepared to set the people with</line>
<line>whom he goes to labour a proper example in the matter of industry.</line>

<line>The value of our property is now over $700,000. If we add to this our</line>
<line>endowment fund, which at present is $1,000,000, the value of the total</line>
<line>property is now $1,700,000. Aside from the need for more buildings and</line>
<line>for money for current expenses, the endowment fund should be increased</line>
<line>to at least $3,000,000. The annual current expenses are now about</line>
<line>$150,000. The greater part of this I collect each year by going from</line>
<line>door to door and from house to house. All of our property is free from</line>
<line>mortgage, and is deeded to an undenominational board of trustees who</line>
<line>have the control of the institution.</line>

<line>From thirty students the number has grown to fourteen hundred, coming</line>
<line>from twenty-seven states and territories, from Africa, Cuba, Porto</line>
<line>Rico, Jamaica, and other foreign countries. In our departments there</line>
<line>are one hundred and ten officers and instructors; and if we add the</line>
<line>families of our instructors, we have a constant population upon our</line>
<line>grounds of not far from seventeen hundred people.</line>

<line>I have often been asked how we keep so large a body of people together,</line>
<line>and at the same time keep them out of mischief. There are two answers:</line>
<line>that the men and women who come to us for an education are in earnest;</line>
<line>and that everybody is kept busy. The following outline of our daily</line>
<line>work will testify to this:—</line>

<line>5 a.m., rising bell; 5.50 a.m., warning breakfast bell; 6 a.m.,</line>
<line>breakfast bell; 6.20 a.m., breakfast over; 6.20 to 6.50 a.m., rooms are</line>
<line>cleaned; 6.50, work bell; 7.30, morning study hours; 8.20, morning</line>
<line>school bell; 8.25, inspection of young men’s toilet in ranks; 8.40,</line>
<line>devotional exercises in chapel; 8.55, “five minutes with the daily</line>
<line>news;” 9 a.m., class work begins; 12, class work closes; 12.15 p.m.,</line>
<line>dinner; 1 p.m., work bell; 1.30 p.m., class work begins; 3.30 p.m.,</line>
<line>class work ends; 5.30 p.m., bell to “knock off” work; 6 p.m., supper;</line>
<line>7.10 p.m., evening prayers; 7.30 p.m., evening study hours; 8.45 p.m.,</line>
<line>evening study hour closes; 9.20 p.m., warning retiring bell; 9.30 p.m.,</line>
<line>retiring bell.</line>

<line>We try to keep constantly in mind the fact that the worth of the school</line>
<line>is to be judged by its graduates. Counting those who have finished the</line>
<line>full course, together with those who have taken enough training to</line>
<line>enable them to do reasonably good work, we can safely say that at least</line>
<line>six thousand men and women from Tuskegee are now at work in different</line>
<line>parts of the South; men and women who, by their own example or by</line>
<line>direct efforts, are showing the masses of our race now to improve their</line>
<line>material, educational, and moral and religious life. What is equally</line>
<line>important, they are exhibiting a degree of common sense and</line>
<line>self-control which is causing better relations to exist between the</line>
<line>races, and is causing the Southern white man to learn to believe in the</line>
<line>value of educating the men and women of my race. Aside from this, there</line>
<line>is the influence that is constantly being exerted through the mothers’</line>
<line>meeting and the plantation work conducted by Mrs. Washington.</line>

<line>Wherever our graduates go, the changes which soon begin to appear in</line>
<line>the buying of land, improving homes, saving money, in education, and in</line>
<line>high moral characters are remarkable. Whole communities are fast being</line>
<line>revolutionized through the instrumentality of these men and women.</line>

<line>Ten years ago I organized at Tuskegee the first Negro Conference. This</line>
<line>is an annual gathering which now brings to the school eight or nine</line>
<line>hundred representative men and women of the race, who come to spend a</line>
<line>day in finding out what the actual industrial, mental, and moral</line>
<line>conditions of the people are, and in forming plans for improvement. Out</line>
<line>from this central Negro Conference at Tuskegee have grown numerous</line>
<line>state and local conferences which are doing the same kind of work. As a</line>
<line>result of the influence of these gatherings, one delegate reported at</line>
<line>the last annual meeting that ten families in his community had bought</line>
<line>and paid for homes. On the day following the annual Negro Conference,</line>
<line>there is the “Workers’ Conference.” This is composed of officers and</line>
<line>teachers who are engaged in educational work in the larger institutions</line>
<line>in the South. The Negro Conference furnishes a rare opportunity for</line>
<line>these workers to study the real condition of the rank and file of the</line>
<line>people.</line>

<line>In the summer of 1900, with the assistance of such prominent coloured</line>
<line>men as Mr. T. Thomas Fortune, who has always upheld my hands in every</line>
<line>effort, I organized the National Negro Business League, which held its</line>
<line>first meeting in Boston, and brought together for the first time a</line>
<line>large number of the coloured men who are engaged in various lines of</line>
<line>trade or business in different parts of the United States. Thirty</line>
<line>states were represented at our first meeting. Out of this national</line>
<line>meeting grew state and local business leagues.</line>

<line>In addition to looking after the executive side of the work at</line>
<line>Tuskegee, and raising the greater part of the money for the support of</line>
<line>the school, I cannot seem to escape the duty of answering at least a</line>
<line>part of the calls which come to me unsought to address Southern white</line>
<line>audiences and audiences of my own race, as well as frequent gatherings</line>
<line>in the North. As to how much of my time is spent in this way, the</line>
<line>following clipping from a Buffalo (N.Y.) paper will tell. This has</line>
<line>reference to an occasion when I spoke before the National Educational</line>
<line>Association in that city.</line>

<line>Booker T. Washington, the foremost educator among the coloured people</line>
<line>of the world, was a very busy man from the time he arrived in the city</line>
<line>the other night from the West and registered at the Iroquois. He had</line>
<line>hardly removed the stains of travel when it was time to partake of</line>
<line>supper. Then he held a public levee in the parlours of the Iroquois</line>
<line>until eight o’clock. During that time he was greeted by over two</line>
<line>hundred eminent teachers and educators from all parts of the United</line>
<line>States. Shortly after eight o’clock he was driven in a carriage to</line>
<line>Music Hall, and in one hour and a half he made two ringing addresses,</line>
<line>to as many as five thousand people, on Negro education. Then Mr.</line>
<line>Washington was taken in charge by a delegation of coloured citizens,</line>
<line>headed by the Rev. Mr. Watkins, and hustled off to a small informal</line>
<line>reception, arranged in honour of the visitor by the people of his race.</line>

<line>Nor can I, in addition to making these addresses, escape the duty of</line>
<line>calling the attention of the South and of the country in general,</line>
<line>through the medium of the press, to matters that pertain to the</line>
<line>interests of both races. This, for example, I have done in regard to</line>
<line>the evil habit of lynching. When the Louisiana State Constitutional</line>
<line>Convention was in session, I wrote an open letter to that body pleading</line>
<line>for justice for the race. In all such efforts I have received warm and</line>
<line>hearty support from the Southern newspapers, as well as from those in</line>
<line>all other parts of the country.</line>

<line>Despite superficial and temporary signs which might lead one to</line>
<line>entertain a contrary opinion, there was never a time when I felt more</line>
<line>hopeful for the race than I do at the present. The great human law that</line>
<line>in the end recognizes and rewards merit is everlasting and universal.</line>
<line>The outside world does not know, neither can it appreciate, the</line>
<line>struggle that is constantly going on in the hearts of both the Southern</line>
<line>white people and their former slaves to free themselves from racial</line>
<line>prejudice; and while both races are thus struggling they should have</line>
<line>the sympathy, the support, and the forbearance of the rest of the</line>
<line>world.</line>

<line>As I write the closing words of this autobiography I find myself—not by</line>
<line>design—in the city of Richmond, Virginia: the city which only a few</line>
<line>decades ago was the capital of the Southern Confederacy, and where,</line>
<line>about twenty-five years ago, because of my poverty I slept night after</line>
<line>night under a sidewalk.</line>

<line>This time I am in Richmond as the guest of the coloured people of the</line>
<line>city; and came at their request to deliver an address last night to</line>
<line>both races in the Academy of Music, the largest and finest audience</line>
<line>room in the city. This was the first time that the coloured people had</line>
<line>ever been permitted to use this hall. The day before I came, the City</line>
<line>Council passed a vote to attend the meeting in a body to hear me speak.</line>
<line>The state Legislature, including the House of Delegates and the Senate,</line>
<line>also passed a unanimous vote to attend in a body. In the presence of</line>
<line>hundreds of coloured people, many distinguished white citizens, the</line>
<line>City Council, the state Legislature, and state officials, I delivered</line>
<line>my message, which was one of hope and cheer; and from the bottom of my</line>
<line>heart I thanked both races for this welcome back to the state that gave</line>
<line>me birth.</line>
